8 Fleet Orchestration

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Fleet stack focus for this chapter.

Why can jobs wait while expensive accelerators sit idle?

A thousand idle accelerators still incur ownership or reservation costs while queued experiments make no progress. More hardware does not resolve that waste when available resources cannot form the groups that pending jobs need. Free devices may be scattered across machines with unsuitable connections, or several jobs may each hold resources while waiting for the rest. On one machine, improving execution usually begins with the work already running. Across a shared fleet, useful progress also depends on which work can start, how long it waits, and what other work its allocation displaces. A short experiment needs a timely result, a long training run needs continuity, and a serving workload needs capacity before requests miss their deadlines. Favoring any one claim without considering the others can leave the fleet busy while delaying the results it exists to produce. Interrupting a job can free capacity, but preserving its state and restoring progress consume time and bandwidth. Waiting for a better placement can accelerate execution while extending completion time. Fair access therefore cannot be judged from device counts alone, and high utilization cannot establish that capacity is being used well. The engineering challenge is to honor these competing claims under changing demand and uncertain resource availability, while keeping the allocation process itself reliable. In C³ terms, orchestration pays coordination overhead to turn compute and communication capacity into useful progress while preserving fairness and latency headroom.

Learning Objectives
  • Explain how batch and declarative control planes admit, place, and reconcile allocations under stale state and partial failure
  • Apply gang, multi-dimensional fit, and topology constraints before dominant-resource fairness to find allocations a job can run
  • Analyze conflicts among throughput, latency, fairness, and cost using slowdown, queueing delay, and ML-specific scheduling signals
  • Design topology-aware placements that map tensor-, pipeline-, and data-parallel groups onto NVLink domains and switch tiers
  • Design elastic, preemption, and spot policies whose throughput or price gains repay their transition and interruption costs
  • Diagnose utilization losses by separating allocated, active, and productive capacity and tracing each gap to its cause
  • Evaluate serving-capacity and quota policies that balance serving headroom, team guarantees, and reclaimable borrowed capacity
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

8.1 The Scheduling Problem

Consider two research teams sharing a 1,000-GPU cluster: Team A submits a 512-GPU job that will run for a month, while Team B submits hundreds of 8-GPU experiments that each run for an hour. If the scheduler continually admits whichever jobs fit, Team B’s small experiments can indefinitely block Team A’s large training run. The scheduling problem is to resolve this conflict among throughput, fairness, and cluster utilization.

Production traces show that this mix is the normal case rather than a contrived one. In the Kalos cluster that Shanghai AI Laboratory used for language-model development in 2023, 86 percent of 19,907 GPU jobs requested a single GPU, yet together they consumed 0.1 percent of the cluster’s GPU time. Jobs of 256 GPUs or more made up 4 percent of submissions and consumed 96 percent of GPU time (Hu et al. 2024), two distributions that figure 1 plots side by side. Counted by jobs, the cluster is a stream of small experiments; counted by GPU-hours, it is a few percent of jobs that hold nearly all the accelerators. A scheduler must serve both views at once, which is why fairness, packing, and preemption policies cannot be judged by either count alone.

Mechanisms for synchronizing workers efficiently (Collective Communication), partitioning computation across them (Distributed Training), and recovering from hardware failures (Fault Tolerance) solve the single-job scaling problem. Each of them assumed that the job already held its accelerators. Fault tolerance assumed more than that. Its recovery paths drew on a spare pool deep enough to replace a drained node and on spot capacity whose loss the job could absorb. Neither exists unless something outside the job grants it, holds it in reserve, or takes it back. That something is orchestration, the policy that decides which jobs run, where they run, when one is preempted for another, and how competing demands share the fleet. An allocation decision must hold across unreliable nodes, balance competing fleet objectives, and satisfy each job’s feasibility constraints before platform control planes, topology-aware placement, elastic and cost-aware policies, workload-aware ML schedulers, and serving, multi-tenant, and diagnostic practices can turn raw capacity into useful work. Training and serving draw on the same fleet, so the capacity envelope this chapter allocates is the one both depend on; how a serving system spends its share, request by request, is the subject of Serving at Scale.

In the fleet stack (shown in the margin), orchestration occupies the Reliability & Control row of the distribution band, but the infrastructure band beneath it constrains its decisions. The scheduler must account for GPU heterogeneity, NVLink topology, rack power limits, and network bisection bandwidth. When scheduling goes wrong, debugging can require examining all four bands to identify whether the root cause lies in infrastructure (hardware constraints), distribution (algorithm limitations), serving (workload mismatch), or governance (policy misconfiguration). Orchestration connects physical infrastructure to distributed algorithms, translating resource requests into placements that respect both hardware topology and organizational policy.

Translating those requests into placements grows harder as the fleet and its flagship workloads scale. In a 10,000-GPU cluster, a single pretraining run of a GPT-3-class 175B-parameter model (Brown et al. 2020), the running instance of Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) throughout this book, can hold a 1,024-GPU allocation for weeks. Because the scaling laws of AI Scaling Laws reward larger models trained on more tokens, each new generation of such runs asks for a larger gang held for longer, so the scheduler’s hardest requests keep growing. When enough jobs of that class are queued that together they ask for more GPUs than the cluster holds, alongside thousands of smaller experiments and inference workloads competing for the same pool, naive policies break down. A first-come-first-served policy without backfilling or preemption can let large jobs occupy the cluster for weeks, delaying smaller experiments that researchers need to iterate quickly. A fair-share policy that divides capacity without respecting each job’s minimum resource bundle can leave many partial allocations and no runnable large job.

Brown, Tom B., Benjamin Mann, Nick Ryder, Melanie Subbiah, Jared Kaplan, Prafulla Dhariwal, Arvind Neelakantan, et al. 2020. “Language Models Are Few-Shot Learners.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 33: 1877–901. https://doi.org/10.48550/arxiv.2005.14165.

↩︎ Archetype A and its 175B-parameter running instance are established in Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3).

The economic stakes make these decisions consequential at every scale. A continuously provisioned 10,000-GPU cluster, valued at the chapter’s cloud-equivalent rate of $2/GPU-hour, represents $480,000 per day in capacity, whether the GPUs are computing useful work or sitting idle while jobs wait in a queue. If scheduling inefficiencies leave 30 percent of GPUs idle, that translates to $144,000 per day in unused capacity value, or about $52.6M annually. Holding useful work per active GPU-hour constant, improving utilization from 50 percent to 80 percent adds 3,000 fully active GPU-equivalents of productive capacity without purchasing additional hardware. That count measures the gain in useful work. Procurement planning asks a different question, namely how much hardware the gain replaces. For that comparison the answer is 6,000 raw GPUs, because new GPUs run under the same scheduling policy would also reach only 50 percent utilization. Recovered capacity becomes cash savings only when it avoids purchases or billed resource time. Scheduling can therefore increase the useful work delivered by an existing investment, even when the fleet’s immediate expenditure remains unchanged.

Workload semantics, rather than an ML-versus-high-performance computing (HPC) boundary, determine scheduling flexibility. Rigid synchronous jobs in both domains require a fixed set of co-scheduled ranks, whereas malleable jobs can make progress across a declared range of resource counts. Gang scheduling1 is the central allocation constraint for a rigid training group: a job configured to require 1,024 GPUs cannot complete its distributed iterations with only half of them. Malleable data-parallel training can instead vary its replica count at explicit reconfiguration boundaries, but tensor- and pipeline-parallel groups usually retain a rigid base allocation. The scheduler must therefore model each job’s minimum viable gang and permitted resize points rather than infer rigidity from the workload’s field.

1 Gang scheduling: Formalized by Ousterhout (1982) for parallel systems, where the “Ousterhout matrix” co-schedules related threads across processors in synchronized time slices. In a rigid ML training group, incomplete allocation prevents collective completion, so held resources can remain idle until the missing ranks launch.

Ousterhout, John K. 1982. “Scheduling Techniques for Concurrent Systems.” Proceedings of the 3rd International Conference on Distributed Computing Systems (ICDCS), 22–30.

2 Placement group: A cloud-native abstraction that requests instances be placed within a high-bandwidth, low-latency network domain. For the ML Fleet, placement groups reduce placement scatter and may expose higher network limits than unconstrained placement. They do not guarantee workload-level bisection bandwidth: topology oversubscription, concurrent traffic, routing, and the collective pattern still determine delivered AllReduce bandwidth.

If gang scheduling determines which resources must be available together, topology awareness via placement groups2 determines how efficiently they can communicate. Tensor-parallel groups benefit from placement within a high-bandwidth NVLink domain; a placement across slower links must still meet the job’s communication budget. Two allocations of the same size can therefore deliver very different useful throughput.

GPU heterogeneity is the third dimension. Many clusters contain mixtures of A100 and H100 accelerators with different compute throughput and interconnect bandwidth. Their training-throughput ratio depends on the workload, numerical precision, and communication path, so the scheduler needs measured performance and the applicable allocation cost. Memory capacity adds a hard admission test once pools differ in it. The common A100 and H100 variants both carry 80 GB of high-bandwidth memory (HBM), but smaller-memory variants of the same accelerator and older generations often share the fleet. A job whose working set fits only the larger devices cannot run on the rest at any priority. The debugging example in section 8.12 returns to exactly such a mixed fleet. The scheduler must match workloads to appropriate hardware based on actual requirements, not user preferences, while maintaining high utilization across heterogeneous pools. When users request specific GPU types out of habit rather than necessity, the mismatch between requested and required resources can leave entire hardware pools idle while queues overflow on preferred hardware.

Job durations are also uncertain: a training run may converge early and complete in days rather than weeks, hardware failures may abort jobs and return resources at unpredictable times, and hyperparameter searches may terminate unpromising configurations early. In both ML and scientific computing, fixed-step jobs with stable iteration times permit better release-time estimates than jobs whose stopping conditions or execution rates vary. The scheduler must therefore distinguish an estimated completion time from an enforced runtime limit when deciding whether another job can safely use a temporary gap in capacity.

The interaction between these challenges creates combinatorial complexity. A scheduler must simultaneously handle gang scheduling constraints (atomic allocation), heterogeneous resources (capability matching), topology requirements (NVLink locality for tensor parallelism), priority and fairness policies (organizational equity), preemption decisions (which running jobs to interrupt), and cost optimization (spot vs. on-demand placement). Each dimension constrains the others. A topology-optimal placement may violate fairness policies, a cost-optimal spot placement may violate reliability requirements, and a fairness-driven allocation may fragment resources in ways that prevent gang scheduling. The scheduler therefore does more than pack jobs onto GPUs. It must maintain a consistent, economically useful view of a failing, heterogeneous fleet. At that scale, placement stops being only an optimization problem and becomes a distributed systems problem of keeping state coherent across thousands of machines.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In the Kalos language-model cluster trace, most jobs requested a single GPU yet consumed a tiny share of GPU time, while jobs of 256 or more GPUs were a few percent of submissions and consumed nearly all GPU time. What does this distribution imply for judging a scheduling policy?

    1. Judge the policy by job count alone, because most users submit small jobs.
    2. Judge the policy by GPU-hours alone, because large jobs hold nearly all of the capacity.
    3. Judge the policy under both views, because small jobs set most users’ waits while a few large gangs hold most of the capacity.
    4. Treat the distribution as an artifact of one cluster, because production fleets have uniform job sizes.

  2. A 10,000-GPU cluster raises utilization from 50 percent to 80 percent with no new hardware, holding useful work per active GPU-hour constant. Why does the gain count as 3,000 productive GPU-equivalents but replace 6,000 raw GPUs, and when does it become a cash saving?

  3. True or False: Whether a job must be scheduled as a rigid gang follows from whether it is labeled an ML training job or an HPC job.

See Answers →

8.2 Distributed Scheduling Complexity

A single-machine scheduler works inside one operating-system boundary, where three properties hold closely enough to design around: it observes resource state directly and consistently, its allocation decisions take effect atomically, and failures are nearly binary (the machine is up or it is down). Device faults and delayed cleanup still occur, but they do not hide behind a network. A cluster scheduler surrenders all three properties. It coordinates independent nodes through delayed messages and uncertain observations, so an authoritative allocation decision does not establish that every node has received or completed it. Several distributed systems problems emerge from that gap.

Partial failures pose the first challenge. A node can fail between allocation and job start, creating a gap between the scheduler’s decision and its effect. The scheduler may successfully allocate 32 GPUs across 4 nodes, only to have one node fail before the job launches. The remaining 24 GPUs sit idle while the scheduler detects the failure, updates its state, and re-plans the allocation. Meanwhile, other jobs that could have used those 24 GPUs have already been placed elsewhere, potentially on suboptimal hardware. Before the first training step, recovery must repair allocation and launch state even though no new model progress exists to restore. Once execution begins, resource recovery must also coordinate with the framework’s checkpoint and membership mechanisms.

Network partitions create a second problem. The scheduler may lose connectivity to a subset of nodes while those nodes continue operating normally. From the scheduler’s perspective, their status is uncertain; from the nodes’ perspective, jobs may still be running and producing useful work. Reallocating their GPUs risks double allocation if the partition heals while the old work still runs; waiting for reconnection leaves possibly healthy capacity idle. The partition’s duration cannot be known in advance, so any fixed timeout is a guess about network behavior that may prove wrong. A timeout can trigger recovery, but it does not prove that the old execution has stopped. Reusing a GPU therefore requires confirmed cleanup or an enforced revocation of the old allocation. Restarting a job on reachable replacement nodes also requires fencing stale attempts from shared outputs. The recovery policy can choose how long to wait for reconnection, but each choice must preserve exclusive ownership where the workload requires it.

State inconsistency emerges as a third challenge that compounds the first two. Resource state may differ between the scheduler’s view and reality on individual nodes. A GPU the scheduler believes is free may still be cleaning up from a previous job: flushing caches, deallocating device memory, running a zombie process from a failed container, or completing a CUDA driver reset after an error. Conversely, a GPU marked as “in use” may have been freed by a job that terminated between heartbeat intervals. The scheduler therefore operates on a model of cluster state that is always slightly stale. The degree of staleness varies across nodes with heartbeat frequency, network latency, and the complexity of cleanup operations.

Ordering without global time presents the fourth issue. Allocation messages issued before and after a partition can arrive out of order. Logical timestamps can describe an order, but do not by themselves prevent conflicting ownership. The control plane must serialize conflicting allocation decisions, and the resource manager must reject stale allocation generations before acting on them. Otherwise, an old launch request can revive work after a newer allocation has taken ownership.

These four challenges echo the CAP theorem of The CAP theorem reality, under which a partitioned system cannot guarantee both consistency and availability. CAP is a theorem about distributed data systems, not a literal proof about every scheduler, but the analogy draws attention to which allocation decisions remain safe when nodes cannot communicate. Slurm, an HPC workload manager, and Kubernetes, a container orchestration platform, both need authoritative allocation records and mechanisms that reconcile those records with execution. Optimistic concurrency permits schedulers to plan against potentially stale snapshots, then validate proposed changes before committing them. Rejecting a conflicting proposal costs scheduling work; accepting conflicting ownership would violate a different, essential invariant.

The control-plane tension becomes concrete when the scheduler interacts with the ML framework’s own distributed coordination plane during a partition. A scheduler may decide that a subset of nodes is unavailable while workers on those nodes are still blocked inside a collective operation. That creates a brief window of conflicting intent: the scheduler wants to recover the allocation, but the training job has not yet converged on a new membership view. Elastic launch and rendezvous mechanisms such as TorchElastic reduce this ambiguity. They use heartbeats and re-rendezvous so the framework can either reform a viable worker group or terminate cleanly, which gives the scheduler a clearer recovery signal than an opaque collective timeout (PyTorch Contributors 2026b, 2026a). This layered coordination, with scheduler-level policy for coarse allocation and framework-level membership for workers, is the characteristic architecture of production distributed training clusters.

PyTorch Contributors. 2026b. Torchrun (Elastic Launch). PyTorch documentation.
PyTorch Contributors. 2026a. Torch Distributed Elastic Rendezvous. PyTorch documentation.

8.2.1 Admission, placement, and reconciliation

When a single undifferentiated queue mixes quota checks, hardware binding, and crash recovery, operators cannot tell whether a pending job is blocked by team quota, fragmented topology, or a dead node whose lease has not yet expired. A stale retry can also assign the same accelerators to two jobs at once. A scheduler control plane avoids this ambiguity by separating three decisions. Admission control decides whether a job may enter the runnable set under quota, priority, gang, and safety constraints. Placement binds an admitted job to a feasible set of nodes while accounting for accelerator capability, topology, power, storage, and network capacity. Reconciliation compares desired allocations with observed node and job state, then starts, retries, evicts, or fences work until the two agree. Keeping these stages explicit makes it possible to distinguish admission denial from placement failure, while versioned decisions and resource-level enforcement prevent stale observations from authorizing competing owners.

Control-plane architectures distribute these decisions differently (Hindman et al. 2011; Burns et al. 2016). A centralized scheduler serializes admission and placement against one authoritative state, which simplifies conflict handling but concentrates scheduling latency and failure impact. A two-level design offers resources to framework schedulers, gaining policy specialization at the cost of delayed or rejected offers. A shared-state design lets several schedulers plan concurrently against a common state and resolves conflicts through version checks or optimistic concurrency. In every design, reconciliation closes the loop. Node agents must enforce allocation generations and lease validity so that an old decision cannot revive after a newer placement has taken ownership.

Hindman, Benjamin, Andy Konwinski, Matei Zaharia, Ali Ghodsi, Anthony D. Joseph, Randy Katz, Scott Shenker, and Ion Stoica. 2011. “Mesos: A Platform for Fine-Grained Resource Sharing in the Data Center.” 8th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 11), 295–308.

Feasibility also extends beyond free accelerator count. Power-aware admission rejects or delays a placement whose worst-case draw would exceed a rack or facility envelope; relying on average draw risks correlated throttling when colocated jobs enter compute-heavy phases together. I/O-aware placement treats dataset-read and checkpoint-write bandwidth as reservable capacity, so a gang that fits in GPU memory does not start where concurrent input pipelines or checkpoint bursts will saturate the same storage path. These constraints can deliberately leave accelerators idle. The idle capacity is the cost of preserving the power and data-movement budgets required for the admitted jobs to make progress.

8.2.2 Failure as a scheduling input

A scheduler that allocates 10,000 GPUs as if every granted node will stay healthy until the job finishes will see a multi-thousand-GPU training gang stall every few hours waiting for replacement capacity that was already handed out to other workloads. As The Mathematics of Inevitable Failure established, the fleet failure rate is the per-device rate multiplied by the population (equation 7.4), so a cluster of ten thousand GPUs loses a device every few hours (Cluster MTBF Scaling). Where fault tolerance turns that failure rate into checkpoints, detection, and elastic recovery for a running job, the scheduler must plan for failure in every allocation decision. The scheduler uses two failure rates. It sizes spare capacity and replacement paths from the GPU-only count of devices it must replace. Job interruptions, by contrast, follow the shorter full-system interval of Cluster MTBF calculation, which adds PCIe and network failure domains.

The scheduler cannot treat the cluster as a static resource pool where resources, once allocated, remain available until voluntarily released. It must anticipate loss of allocated resources during execution and provide replacement capacity or rescheduling paths. The response depends on the fault and the validity of the surviving state. A transient disruption may permit recovery in place if the execution state remains usable; a persistent hardware failure requires replacement resources. Even a short disruption can force checkpoint recovery if the framework cannot safely continue (Tiwari et al. 2015).

Tiwari, Devesh, Saurabh Gupta, James Rogers, Don Maxwell, Paolo Rech, Sudharshan Vazhkudai, Daniel Oliveira, et al. 2015. “Understanding GPU Errors on Large-Scale HPC Systems and the Implications for System Design and Operation.” 2015 IEEE 21st International Symposium on High Performance Computer Architecture (HPCA), 331–42. https://doi.org/10.1109/hpca.2015.7056044.

When a node heartbeat is missed, the scheduler faces three recovery policies with different delay and capacity costs. It can wait to see whether the heartbeat resumes, though this delays recovery if the machine has permanently crashed. It can instead begin recovery promptly on replacement resources, provided stale execution is fenced before conflicting work resumes. An intermediate approach marks the node as suspect and prepositions replacement capacity while checking its status; this reduces launch delay if replacement becomes necessary, but holds capacity that could otherwise serve pending work. Whichever policy it picks, reuse of the node’s GPUs still waits on the fencing that network partitions require. Historical failure and recovery measurements set how long to wait and how much replacement capacity to hold. The recovery-time decomposition of fault tolerance (equation 7.10) shows where scheduler policy intervenes most effectively. Of its detection, restart, load, and warmup terms, the scheduler owns most of \(\JobRestartTime\), the time to allocate replacement resources and launch processes, and that is the term its spare-capacity policy shortens. Waiting longer on a missed heartbeat lengthens \(\FailureDetectionTime\) in exchange for avoiding a needless restart, so the wait is worth extending only while a transient fault remains more likely than a permanent one.

↪ The same decomposition in scheduler-facing phases (detect, reschedule, reload, and replay of work since the last checkpoint) is developed in The anatomy of recovery time.

These requirements connect the scheduler directly to checkpoint and recovery infrastructure. Scheduling decisions about spare capacity, preemption policies, and elastic training support determine how quickly the system recovers from statistically expected failures. A scheduler that reserves spare capacity or supports elastic recovery can restore throughput much faster than one that requires every failed job to wait for a full fresh allocation and reload. The reserve fraction follows from the measured failure rate and recovery time of the fleet’s own workloads. Once the control plane can maintain coherent ownership across admission, placement, reconciliation, and node failures, it still needs an explicit policy objective to decide which competing jobs receive capacity when demand exceeds what the fleet can run at once.

↩︎ Failure detection, recovery procedures, and spare pools are established in Failure Detection and Recovery.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A scheduler control plane separates admission control, placement, and reconciliation. State what each decides, and explain why keeping them separate helps an operator diagnose a job that has not started.

  2. A network partition cuts the scheduler off from four nodes running a training job, and their heartbeats time out. What must hold before the scheduler reassigns those nodes’ GPUs to another job?

    1. Nothing beyond the timeout, because a missed heartbeat proves the old processes have stopped.
    2. The old allocation must be revoked with confirmed cleanup or fenced, for example by rejecting stale allocation generations, so the old work cannot keep using or writing through those GPUs.
    3. The partition must heal first, because no recovery may begin while nodes are unreachable.
    4. The new job must run at higher priority, because priority resolves conflicting ownership.

  3. True or False: A hypothetical per-device annual hardware-fault probability of 0.001 is sufficient to conclude that all-cause interruptions will remain rare during multi-week jobs on a 10,000-GPU fleet.

See Answers →

8.3 Scheduling Objectives and Their Conflicts

Giving a large training gang an unbroken allocation for weeks amortizes launch and data-movement overhead, but leaves short interactive debugging jobs waiting hours in the queue. Preempting the large gang so short jobs start immediately burns cluster capacity on checkpoint saves and restarts. Every scheduling policy trades four competing objectives against one another: throughput, fairness, latency, and cost efficiency. Throughput benefits from allocations that sustain useful work and amortize launch, interruption, and data-movement overhead; a large allocation helps only while the job scales effectively. Maintaining fairness demands an equitable distribution of resources across competing tenants to prevent a single dominant project from monopolizing the fleet. Dominant resource fairness (Ghodsi et al. 2011) formalizes this balance by equalizing each tenant’s largest normalized share across GPUs, CPU, and memory. Reducing queue latency helps short, interactive jobs complete promptly, but aggressive preemption can repeatedly interrupt long-running training. Pursuing cost efficiency introduces different costs for different policies: spot capacity risks interruption, off-peak scheduling delays admission, and dense packing can increase interference. Each policy must be evaluated against useful work completed and the delays it imposes.

Fairness requires an outcome metric rather than an input quota rule alone. For job \(\JobIndex\), slowdown \(\JobSlowdown_j=(\JobQueueWaitTime_j+\IsolatedJobRunTime_j)/\IsolatedJobRunTime_j\) compares its queueing-plus-running time with its isolated runtime \(\IsolatedJobRunTime_j\), assuming the running time is unchanged by sharing. A one-hour job that waits nine hours has \(\JobSlowdown_j=10\), even if its team eventually receives its nominal GPU share. When contention or resizing changes execution time, the numerator must instead use the observed queueing-plus-running duration while the denominator retains the isolated baseline. A scheduler should report the distribution of slowdown by user and job class, together with the age of the oldest pending feasible job. Dominant-share parity detects unequal resource allocation, slowdown exposes unequal delay, and the maximum feasible wait exposes starvation risk. These metrics separate a policy that is fair in allocated GPU-hours from one that is fair in the time users wait to obtain useful results.

Each of the four objectives, pursued alone, schedules the same pool differently and gives up something the others protect (figure 2). Favoring a large unbroken allocation can delay short experiments, while repeatedly admitting short jobs can prevent a large gang from assembling. These conflicts depend on the workload and available capacity; improving one objective does not necessarily worsen every other objective. Resource-share fairness and completion-delay fairness therefore need separate measurements.

Consider a 64-GPU fine-tuning run of the same 175B-parameter model, a far smaller allocation than its 1,024-GPU pretraining run. If it sustains high utilization for days, an uninterrupted allocation amortizes launch overhead across a long execution. Admitting it may nevertheless require draining 64 suitably placed GPUs, delaying smaller experiments. Conversely, repeatedly filling available gaps with short jobs can prevent the large job from assembling its required group. Preemption or resizing can recover flexibility only if the job supports the transition and its cost is acceptable. The policy must therefore compare useful throughput with completion delay for both job classes. In scheduler terminology, those choices often appear as quality-of-service classes: job-priority tiers that decide which objective wins when throughput, latency, fairness, and cost collide.

Queueing adds a further conflict between utilization and latency, because a fleet run close to full load leaves arriving jobs little spare capacity. In a single-server queue with random arrivals, mean waiting time, expressed as a multiple of the mean job duration, is the product of a load factor \(\ServingUtilization/(1-\ServingUtilization)\) and a variability factor \((1+\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient^2)/2\). Here \(\ServingUtilization\) is offered load relative to service capacity, and \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient\) is the coefficient of variation of job duration. The load factor stays small through moderate load and grows without bound as load approaches capacity, so waiting time bends sharply upward near saturation instead of rising in proportion to utilization. The variability factor scales the whole curve. A queue that mixes minute-long debugging jobs with week-long training runs waits several times longer at the same load than a queue of similar-length jobs. Each added point of utilization therefore costs more waiting time in an ML fleet than in a uniform one. Notebook 8.1 derives the relation and prices both factors.

Napkin Math 8.1: The queuing theory of GPU clusters
To isolate the effect of runtime variability, consider an M/G/1 queue with first-come-first-served service. Kendall notation names the queue by its arrival process, service-time distribution, and server count: M for memoryless (Poisson) arrivals, G for a general service-time distribution, and 1 for a single server. Treating a fleet as one server means modeling its aggregate capacity as a single resource that works through one job at a time, with each job’s service time equal to its GPU-hours divided by the fleet’s GPU count. That mapping ignores gang packing and parallel admission, so it isolates the variability effect rather than predicting a real fleet’s wait. Jobs arrive according to a Poisson process \((\ArrivalRate)\), and independent service times follow a general distribution \((\Glyph{G})\) with mean \(1/\ServiceRate\) and finite standard deviation \(\ServiceTimeStandardDeviation\). When offered load is below service capacity, the Pollaczek-Khinchine formula gives the steady-state expected waiting time in the queue \((\QueueWaitTime)\):

\[ \frac{\QueueWaitTime}{1/\ServiceRate} = \frac{\ServingUtilization}{1-\ServingUtilization} \cdot \frac{1+\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient^2}{2} \]

Here, \(\ServingUtilization = \ArrivalRate/\ServiceRate\) represents offered load relative to the model’s service capacity, \(1/\ServiceRate\) is the average job duration, and \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient= \ServiceTimeStandardDeviation\ServiceRate\) is the coefficient of variation of job duration. The right-hand side is therefore the waiting time expressed as a multiple of the average job duration; a fleet’s measured GPU-busy percentage is not automatically this model’s load.

An exponential service-time distribution provides a reference case, yielding \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient\approx 1\). A mixed ML queue can have much greater variability when short debugging jobs measured in minutes share capacity with training runs measured in weeks. The representative \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient=3\) calculation in this callout captures that variability without claiming a universal fleet constant. The impact on wait times is multiplicative. For the scheduling argument here, the single-server multiplier is sufficient; Queuing theory for batched inference develops an explicit single-server surrogate for batched serving and explains why multi-server, finite-buffer M/G/c/K systems require additional modeling.

At 80 percent utilization \((\ServingUtilization = 0.8)\): with an exponential workload \((\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient= 1)\), \(\QueueWaitTime=\) 4 \(\times\) the average job duration. With an illustrative high-variability workload \((\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient= 3)\), \(\QueueWaitTime=\) 20 \(\times\) the average job duration. In this M/G/1 model, service-time variability multiplies waiting time at the same offered load. The example does not prescribe a universal GPU-utilization threshold or model gang placement directly. It explains why capacity planning needs the service-time distribution and queue-delay objective as well as average load.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A one-hour job waits nine hours in the queue and then runs for its isolated runtime of one hour. Its ____, the ratio of its queueing-plus-running time to its isolated runtime, is 10, even if its team eventually receives its nominal GPU share.

  2. Two teams receive equal dominant shares of the fleet over a month, yet one team’s jobs routinely wait days. Which measurements expose this unfairness, and why does share parity miss it?

  3. Based on the Pollaczek-Khinchine queue model \(\frac{W_q}{1/\mu} = \frac{\rho}{1-\rho} \cdot \frac{1+C_s^2}{2}\), under this illustrative single-server approximation, how does a queue with heavy-tailed job durations (\(C_s = 3\)) operating at \(\rho = 0.8\) utilization compare in expected waiting time (\(W_q\)) to a queue with the same mean service time and exponential durations (\(C_s = 1\)) at the same utilization?

    1. The ML cluster experiences a \(2\times\) increase in expected wait time (\(8\times\) vs. \(4\times\) the average job duration).
    2. The ML cluster experiences a \(5\times\) increase in expected wait time (\(20\times\) vs. \(4\times\) the average job duration).
    3. The ML cluster experiences a \(9\times\) increase in expected wait time (\(36\times\) vs. \(4\times\) the average job duration).
    4. The ML cluster experiences no change in expected wait time because utilization \(\rho\) dominates the Pollaczek-Khinchine denominator.

See Answers →

8.4 Core Allocation Constraints

Awarding the 64-GPU fine-tuning run its fair share of the cluster delivers zero progress if those GPUs sit on nodes with exhausted host memory, lack the interconnect its parallel layout requires, or arrive only 32 at a time. A scheduling objective can choose only among allocations that a job can actually run. Fairness must be computed across every resource a tenant consumes, a request must fit every dimension of the node that receives it, a rigid group must arrive whole, and the resources each job holds must not block one another. Each of these constraints narrows the feasible set before any objective selects within it.

8.4.1 Dominant-resource fairness allocation

Equalizing GPU counts alone fails when one tenant’s jobs saturate host CPU and memory while another’s saturate the network fabric, leaving free GPUs unusable behind exhausted auxiliary resources. Dominant-resource fairness (DRF) makes a multi-resource entitlement comparable by normalizing each tenant’s allocation against fleet capacity and taking its largest share (Ghodsi et al. 2011). Consider the deliberately small cluster in table 1: 8 GPUs, 32 CPU cores, 256 GB of memory, and 80 Gb/s of network capacity. Tenant A’s replica requires 1 GPU, 4 cores, 32 GB, and 10 Gb/s; Tenant B’s distributed worker requires 2 GPUs, 2 cores, 16 GB, and 20 Gb/s.

Ghodsi, Ali, Matei Zaharia, Benjamin Hindman, Andy Konwinski, Scott Shenker, and Ion Stoica. 2011. “Dominant Resource Fairness: Fair Allocation of Multiple Resource Types.” 8th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 11), 323–36.

For tenant \(\TenantIndex\), define its dominant share as \(\DominantShare_i=\max_\ResourceTypeIndex(\TenantResourceAllocation/\FleetResourceCapacity)\), where \(\TenantResourceAllocation\) is its allocation of resource \(\ResourceTypeIndex\) and \(\FleetResourceCapacity\) is fleet capacity for that resource. The calculation equalizes \(\DominantShare_i\), not every resource share. One A replica raises A’s dominant share by \(1/8\); one B worker raises B’s by \(2/8\). Progressive filling therefore grants roughly two A replicas for each B worker until both dominant shares reach \(0.50\). This is a fair policy target, but it is not yet a feasible placement. If B’s two workers form one four-GPU gang, the four GPUs must become available atomically. If tensor parallelism requires one NVLink domain, four free GPUs scattered across nodes do not satisfy the allocation. If the job requires 80 GB HBM accelerators, four incompatible GPUs do not count at all. A production scheduler therefore applies capability, gang, and topology constraints to the feasible set, then uses DRF to choose fairly among allocations that can actually run. The order is not a convenience. Feasibility comes before fairness (principle 15) because a fair share of accelerators on which the job cannot run delivers no progress to the tenant who holds it. When no equal-share placement exists, the scheduler should report the constraint causing the deviation rather than silently presenting an infeasible entitlement as service delivered.

Lighthouse 8.1: Lighthouse archetypes: Placement changes fairness
The same resource share has different meaning across the three lighthouse workloads introduced in Three systems archetypes:

  • Archetype A (GPT-4/Llama-3) needs a rigid, topology-compatible gang. Fractional allocations below its minimum parallel configuration deliver no progress, so fairness must include maximum feasible wait for a contiguous placement rather than GPU-hours alone.
  • Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) couples accelerator memory to sparse-routing bandwidth. A nominal GPU allocation is infeasible when embedding shards or network capacity cannot follow it, so network and memory must enter the dominant share and placement must protect the serving-latency envelope.
  • Archetype C (Federated MobileNet) schedules participation rather than data-center GPUs. Eligibility filters for power, connectivity, and device capability can systematically exclude parts of the population, so fairness must be audited through participation and outcome rates across device and user groups, not equal device counts in a round.

The common rule is to define the feasible bundle first and assess fairness over useful progress second. Treating every accelerator or client as interchangeable would conceal the constraint that makes each archetype difficult.

8.4.2 Bin packing

Even when a cluster has dozens of free GPUs in total, a pending 8-GPU job will sit blocked if previous placements scattered those free GPUs in 1- and 2-GPU remnants across partially occupied nodes. Fitting jobs of varying sizes into fixed-capacity nodes without stranding capacity is an instance of bin packing.3 Because general bin packing is NP-hard, no known algorithm finds optimal solutions in polynomial time. Practical heuristics such as first-fit decreasing and best-fit decreasing offer tractable choices, but their performance depends on job sizes and placement constraints. How much usable capacity a heuristic recovers in a heterogeneous ML fleet has to be measured against that fleet’s job mix, because the classical guarantees cover only one-dimensional packing.

3 Bin packing: First-fit decreasing has a classical asymptotic \(11/9\) approximation ratio for one-dimensional bin packing, with an additive constant in the finite-instance bound. That result does not guarantee the same ratio for ML placement with several resource dimensions, topology constraints, and indivisible gangs. A scheduler must evaluate its heuristic against feasible placements and the stranded capacity it leaves.

Consider a 64-node cluster with 8 GPUs per node, totaling 512 GPUs. If every job requires 6 GPUs within one node, only one such job fits per node, leaving 2 GPUs unused and reducing effective capacity for this workload to 75 percent. The remaining 2 GPUs per node cannot be combined across nodes into another feasible six-GPU allocation, although smaller compatible jobs could use them. With a mix of 1-GPU, 3-GPU, and 7-GPU jobs, fragmentation depends on the placement and the pending requests; total free capacity can exceed a waiting job’s demand while no individual node has enough space. Figure 3 maps this stranded capacity across an \(8{\times}8\) allocation grid. Although 19 slots (30 percent of cluster capacity, dashed white cells) sit idle across nodes 0 through 7, active allocations (J1 through J15) leave every node partially occupied, which blocks a pending 8-GPU job that requires an entire unfragmented node.

ML placement is a multi-dimensional packing problem. Jobs require GPUs together with CPU cores for data preprocessing, host memory for data loading and augmentation pipelines, local SSD storage for dataset caching, and network bandwidth for gradient synchronization. A job requesting 4 GPUs, 32 CPU cores, and 256 GB of RAM may not fit on a node that has 4 free GPUs but only 16 free CPU cores because other jobs have consumed the host CPU for preprocessing. The scheduler must simultaneously satisfy all resource dimensions, and the job fits only if every dimension has sufficient capacity on the selected node. A bottleneck in any one dimension can therefore strand capacity in the others.

Locality constraints further restrict placement beyond simple resource availability. A training job may require tensor-parallel groups to remain within NVLink-connected nodes when its communication budget cannot tolerate the lower per-endpoint bandwidth of the external fabric (450 GB/s per direction intra-node vs. 50 GB/s per port inter-node). On this topology, where NVLink does not connect the nodes, a job requesting “4 GPUs with NVLink connectivity” cannot use 2 GPUs from node A and 2 from node B, even if all 4 GPUs are individually available and the total capacity is sufficient. These topology constraints turn resource counts into a placement problem where which specific resources are available matters as much as how many. A capacity-feasible packing must also pass the job’s locality constraints.

Schedulers address fragmentation through several complementary strategies, each targeting a different aspect of the problem. The first, backfill, lets smaller jobs run in the gaps while a large job waits for contiguous resources; section 8.4.3 develops it together with the reservation it must protect and the runtime estimates it depends on. A second strategy, periodic defragmentation, migrates or preempts low-priority jobs to consolidate free resources into contiguous blocks, analogous to memory compaction in operating systems. The scheduler identifies nodes where partial allocations leave stranded resources, preempts the jobs occupying those resources, and re-schedules them on nodes where they can be packed more efficiently. The cost includes state transfer and restart, plus replay of work not captured by a valid checkpoint. A graceful checkpoint can preserve recent progress, but still takes time and storage bandwidth. The scheduler must weigh these costs against the benefit of starting large jobs sooner, and low-demand periods reduce the number of competing jobs affected by the move.

A third strategy, over-subscription, admits more demand than a capacity dimension can sustain simultaneously, but only where the resource is divisible and the scheduler can bound interference. Average activity of a GPU’s streaming multiprocessors (SMs), its compute units, does not create fractional whole GPUs. A job that reserves device memory, requires an exclusive CUDA context, or belongs to a rigid gang still occupies its assigned device while kernels are idle. Statistical multiplexing can be safe for explicitly shareable CPU, network, storage, or partitioned GPU capacity when tenants have measured, weakly correlated peaks and enforceable limits. It is unsafe to convert 70 percent average GPU utilization into a 1.4\(\times\) device overcommit ratio. Whole-device capacity should instead be recovered through preemption, elastic resizing, or an isolation mechanism such as Multi-Instance GPU (MIG) hardware partitioning or NVIDIA’s Multi-Process Service (MPS), which lets several processes’ kernels share one device. Either mechanism helps only if the scheduler models its memory, compute, and fault-sharing contract explicitly. Both reappear with the serving workloads they suit best in section 8.10.1.

8.4.3 Gang scheduling

Partial allocation of rigid multi-GPU jobs creates a distributed scheduling failure mode in which two jobs each hold half the resources they need and block each other indefinitely while every allocated GPU sits idle. In the left panel of figure 4, jobs J1 and J2 each hold 4 of the 8 GPUs they require, and the whole pool stalls. Atomic gang scheduling (right panel) instead grants J1 all 8 GPUs as one allocation while J2 waits without holding any, which prevents this allocation deadlock.

Definition 8.1: Gang scheduling

Gang scheduling is the ML cluster scheduling policy that atomically allocates the resources required by a job’s minimum viable execution group, so a rigid group either receives its complete allocation or waits without retaining a partial one.

  1. Significance: A rigid synchronous job configured for 1,024 GPUs but holding only 512 GPUs completes zero training steps, not half as many: each collective stalls until every rank in its communication group arrives, so the held GPUs consume power and capital at zero useful output. Without atomic allocation, two such jobs can each acquire half the cluster and deadlock, both at zero progress while consuming the full cluster’s operating cost.
  2. Distinction: Unlike a malleable service or parallel job that can make progress across a resource range, rigid synchronous training has step-function utility: the minimum viable gang or nothing. The distinction is a property of the execution graph, not of whether the workload is labeled ML, cloud, or HPC.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that priorities or quotas alone solve this. Atomicity must extend to every co-scheduled resource the job needs to make progress (accelerators, high-bandwidth interconnect domains, network capacity for collectives); enforcing it at GPU granularity alone recreates the deadlock one resource layer down.

↩︎ Ring and tree AllReduce schedules, each of which completes only after every rank’s contribution has been reduced, are established in AllReduce Algorithms and Topologies.

4 Hold-and-wait: One of the four Coffman conditions (mutual exclusion, hold-and-wait, no preemption, and circular wait), all of which must hold for deadlock to occur (Coffman et al. 1971). Gang scheduling removes hold-and-wait through atomic allocation, which is why it prevents this deadlock by construction; the price is reduced packing flexibility.

Coffman, Edward G., Michael Elphick, and Arie Shoshani. 1971. “System Deadlocks.” ACM Computing Surveys 3 (2): 67–78. https://doi.org/10.1145/356586.356588.

The partial-allocation pattern is a hold-and-wait deadlock4 in which two jobs each hold half the cluster and neither can proceed. Its price is the whole allocation for as long as the cycle persists. At the scale of the chapter’s reference workload, two 1,024-GPU gangs that have each acquired 900 GPUs from a pool too small to complete either one burn about $1,800 per hour apiece. The limiting case is two jobs that each request the entire cluster, the example OpenAI used to motivate group admission (war story 8.1). On the 10,000-GPU cluster, a deadlock that strands every device wastes $20,000 per hour at the chapter’s rate while producing no progress for either job. Measured against the stake computed at the start of the chapter, raising utilization from 50 percent to 80 percent recovers about $144,000 per day in equivalent capacity value. About 7 hours of such a deadlock therefore erase a full day of that gain. Gang scheduling does not create the gain, but it protects it. For a rigid job \(\RigidGangJob\) requiring \(\DeviceCount\) GPUs, the scheduler must grant all \(\DeviceCount\) GPUs atomically or leave the job queued without a partial allocation. This removes hold-and-wait for the resources covered by that allocation; it does not prevent later deadlocks in application code or auxiliary resources.

Naive gang scheduling is wasteful in predictable ways. Atomic allocation means the waiting job holds nothing, yet a large gang will never assemble if every freed GPU goes to a smaller job. The scheduler therefore places a reservation for the job at the head of the queue, draining capacity toward it by declining to start new work on GPUs that the gang will need. If the cluster has 900 free GPUs and that job requests 1,024, the reservation keeps the 900 GPUs idle until 124 more become available, potentially wasting thousands of GPU-hours. Backfill scheduling addresses this by identifying jobs in the queue that can fit within the 900 available GPUs without delaying the large job’s expected start time (figure 5), an idea popularized in the ANL/IBM SP scheduling system (Lifka 1995). A 128-GPU job with an estimated runtime of 2 hours is a backfill candidate if the 124 additional GPUs needed by the large job will remain unavailable for at least 2 hours. Protecting the reservation requires an enforced runtime bound or a preemption contract that releases the backfill allocation in time, including any checkpoint and cleanup delay. The estimate identifies an opportunity; the release contract makes the reservation enforceable.

Lifka, David A. 1995. “The ANL/IBM SP Scheduling System.” In Job Scheduling Strategies for Parallel Processing, edited by Dror G. Feitelson and Larry Rudolph, vol. 949. Lecture Notes in Computer Science. Springer. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-60153-8_35.

The accuracy of runtime estimates determines backfill effectiveness, and this creates a game-theoretic challenge. If users overestimate runtimes, fewer jobs appear to fit a backfill window. If they underestimate, the scheduler may need to preempt a backfilled job to honor the reservation, paying recovery costs and losing unsaved progress. A policy that kills jobs at their declared limit encourages conservative estimates. Tiresias (Gu et al. 2019), discussed in section 8.9.1, avoids duration estimates when setting priority by using observed resource consumption. That choice addresses ordering under uncertainty, while reservation protection still needs an enforced runtime bound or release contract that says when a backfilled job will give its capacity back.

8.4.4 Deadlock prevention and detection

Gang scheduling eliminates the hold-and-wait condition for the atomically allocated bundle, but it does not immunize the cluster against all forms of resource contention. Later requests for auxiliary resources can still create a cycle of dependencies. Priority rules can also prevent a resource holder from making progress without creating a cycle. Distinguishing these cases matters because breaking a deadlock and providing a starved job with enough resources to exit require different interventions.

Priority inversion occurs when a high-priority job waits on a low-priority resource holder whose progress other work delays. Consider the 64-GPU fine-tuning run of the 175B-parameter model, submitted at high priority. The scheduler has reserved 60 available GPUs, but the remaining 4 are held by a low-priority data processing job. Normally, the scheduler would preempt the low-priority job to satisfy the high-priority request. However, if a stream of medium-priority development jobs saturates the cluster’s CPU or network bandwidth, starving the low-priority job of the resources it needs to checkpoint and exit, the low-priority job stalls. Its graceful release path cannot complete. The high-priority job waits for the low-priority job, which is effectively blocked by the medium-priority jobs. The 60-GPU idle block persists until the exit path receives resources or an enforced termination policy reclaims the remaining GPUs, potentially losing uncheckpointed work.

Priority inversion: the high-priority job waits on a low-priority holder that medium-priority work starves.

Definition 8.2: Priority inversion

Priority inversion is an ML cluster scheduling pathology in which a high-priority task is forced to wait for a lower-priority task to release a shared resource.

  1. Significance: It ties the progress of a high-priority job, and the accelerators reserved for it, to the progress of the lowest-priority job in the wait-for chain. In ML clusters, this typically occurs when a low-priority job holding GPUs is starved of auxiliary resources (for example, bandwidth for checkpointing), preventing it from finishing and releasing the accelerators needed by high-priority workloads.
  2. Distinction: Unlike standard queuing (where tasks wait their turn), priority inversion involves an active blockage: the high-priority task is ready to run but is transitively dependent on a task that the scheduler does not prioritize.
  3. Common pitfall: A frequent misconception is that strict priority levels solve this. Raising the waiting job’s priority does not supply the CPU or bandwidth that its lower-priority resource holder needs to exit; the preemption policy must protect that release path.

Prevention and detection address different parts of this problem. Atomic allocation prevents hold-and-wait for the resources included in the gang, while reserving checkpoint bandwidth allows a preempted job to complete its exit path. Timeouts instead bound how long the system waits before investigating or reclaiming an allocation; they do not prevent deadlock states from arising. Monitoring can trigger recovery when progress stops, but safe reclamation still requires enforcing the new ownership decision.

Detection, unlike exact placement search, is computationally cheap. Before feasibility filtering, a cluster with 10,000 GPUs and 500 pending jobs can have more than \(10^{15}\) candidate allocations, which puts exhaustive allocation search out of reach. Detection instead works on a wait-for graph, a directed graph with one vertex per job and an edge from job A to job B whenever A waits for a resource that B holds. A cycle identifies a potential deadlock. It establishes deadlock when every edge is an unavoidable blocking dependency and no alternative resource allocation can let a member proceed. Finding a cycle in an already constructed graph takes time linear in its vertices and edges. The difficulty lies in construction. Resource state changes continuously across a distributed control plane, so the scheduler rarely holds a complete, current graph, and a cycle detected on stale state may already have resolved while a real one goes unseen.

Large-scale schedulers therefore supplement wait-for detection with mechanisms that preserve liveness under incomplete state. A lease-based resource gives every allocation a time-to-live; expiry authorizes reclamation only when the resource manager enforces revocation and cleanup before reuse. Progress monitoring checks advancement through signals such as completed-step counters, using GPU utilization and heartbeats as supporting observations rather than proof of useful work. When the scheduler must preempt a lower-priority job, holistic preemption reserves the CPU, network, and storage bandwidth needed for that job to checkpoint and exit cleanly; otherwise, the victim can be too starved to release the accelerators. False failure suspicions can cost useful work, but must not bypass the ownership checks required for safe recovery.

8.4.5 Heterogeneous gang scheduling

In preference-training pipelines such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF), allocating the policy-training GPUs while the rollout-generation or reward-scoring workers sit queued stalls the workflow just as completely as a missing AllReduce rank. Homogeneous gang scheduling allocates \(\DeviceCount\) identical workers executing the same compute graph. Heterogeneous workflows instead combine trainers, evaluators, and inference-style workers that need different GPU counts, memory footprints, and lifetimes, yet still must run together as a single job.

↩︎ The models that alignment training co-locates, and their unequal resource demands, are established in Multi-Model Training for Alignment and RLHF.

Systems Perspective 8.1: Routing over synchrony
Heterogeneous gang scheduling extends atomic allocation to the roles that must coexist for a workflow to progress. A viable group might pair high-HBM nodes for memory-bound generation with compute-oriented nodes for policy updates. The orchestration problem then shifts from packing a homogeneous ring toward routing data. Rather than reducing gradients among identical workers, the job streams rollouts from generation workers to the workers that compute policy updates, so placement must also support the data exchanged between those roles. Rollout and training stages may exchange work in batches or through a buffered pipeline; their measured production and consumption rates determine where queues grow or accelerators wait. The scheduler needs a minimum viable resource bundle and a supported scaling policy for each role, rather than an assumption that all workers are interchangeable or perfectly balanced.

Homogeneous and heterogeneous jobs share an allocation requirement: the scheduler must assemble a viable group before expecting progress. Backfill can use capacity while that group waits, provided runtime estimates or preemption contracts protect the reservation. Enforcing these policies requires a control plane that represents ownership and reconciles allocations after failures.

Self-Check: Question

  1. True or False: Under Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF), if Team A’s workload requires \(4\text{ GPUs}\) and \(8\text{ CPU cores}\) per worker while Team B’s workload requires \(1\text{ GPU}\) and \(64\text{ CPU cores}\) per worker on a cluster with \(100\text{ GPUs}\) and \(1000\text{ CPU cores}\), DRF equalizes each team’s share of their dominant resource (GPUs for Team A, CPUs for Team B).

  2. When multi-GPU jobs leave unallocated accelerators spread across nodes that cannot be combined to satisfy pending multi-GPU requests due to intra-node interconnect requirements, the cluster suffers from ____.

  3. In a 1,024-GPU cluster receiving two 1,024-GPU synchronous training jobs, what condition causes a hold-and-wait deadlock under a non-gang scheduler, and how does gang scheduling eliminate it?

    1. Non-gang schedulers allocate partial subsets (e.g., 512 GPUs to each job) where neither job can progress through synchronous AllReduce collectives, whereas gang scheduling enforces atomic all-or-nothing allocation so unallocated jobs hold zero resources.
    2. Non-gang schedulers force jobs to time-slice GPU compute cycles, causing NCCL timeouts during AllReduce, whereas gang scheduling disables CUDA preemption entirely.
    3. Non-gang schedulers assign GPUs across multiple racks, creating switch contention, whereas gang scheduling restricts all 1,024 GPUs to a single physical server chassis.
    4. Non-gang schedulers allow lower-priority jobs to preempt higher-priority jobs, whereas gang scheduling locks node allocations until all jobs in the queue complete.

  4. Describe how priority inversion occurs when a high-priority distributed training job requires accelerators held by a low-priority job, and explain why simply setting higher priority levels does not resolve the blockage.

  5. How does heterogeneous gang scheduling differ from traditional homogeneous gang scheduling in the context of reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) workflows?

See Answers →

8.5 Orchestration Paradigms

A pending gang and a queue of small backfill jobs need both a policy for sharing capacity and a control plane that can enforce it. The policies just developed apply across platforms; the orchestration paradigm determines how the platform represents allocations and recovers when observed state diverges from them. Slurm, shaped by HPC batch systems, favors explicit resource requests and predictable allocation. Kubernetes, shaped by cloud-native service management, favors declared desired state and continuous reconciliation. The choice is therefore not Slurm vs. Kubernetes as products; it is which control model best matches the workload mix, failure behavior, and operational culture of the fleet.

The two platforms embody two control models (figure 6). Slurm follows an imperative batch model, in which the user states the resources a job needs and the central slurmctld dispatches that allocation through per-node slurmd daemons. Kubernetes follows a declarative model, in which the user states what should be running and controllers reconcile observed state toward that declaration through the API server and node agents. Both platforms accept explicit resource requirements and track execution state, so the useful distinction is which lifecycle the platform manages directly: an allocated batch job or a collection of workloads maintained by controllers. That distinction shapes how ML workloads are admitted, monitored, and recovered from failure, and distributed training still needs framework-level coordination under either model.

8.5.1 Slurm and HPC scheduling heritage

When a multi-node job needs an exclusive, non-overlapping block of accelerators for days at a time without partial-allocation deadlocks or conflicting node ownership, a centralized batch allocator is the most direct control plane. The Slurm Workload Manager5 originated at Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory to solve that problem for Linux supercomputers (Yoo et al. 2003): long-running jobs, expensive shared hardware, and users who can specify resource needs in advance. In ML infrastructure, the same batch model maps to GPU training through partitions for heterogeneous accelerator pools and predictable atomic allocations for long-running distributed jobs.

5 Slurm Workload Manager: Originally “SLURM,” an acronym for Simple Linux Utility for Resource Management, the project dropped the acronym capitalization in 2012. Developed at Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory beginning in 2002, Slurm’s centralized controller (slurmctld) maintains a single authoritative view of resource allocations. At very large scale, operators may partition fleets or federate schedulers to keep scheduling latency, policy isolation, and failure domains manageable.

Yoo, A. B., M. A. Jette, and M. Grondona. 2003. “SLURM: Simple Linux Utility for Resource Management.” In Job Scheduling Strategies for Parallel Processing. Springer Berlin Heidelberg. https://doi.org/10.1007/10968987_3.

Slurm’s batch submission model makes resource requirements explicit. Users submit job scripts, and the scheduler allocates resources within eligible partitions according to policy and availability. A submission of sbatch --gres=gpu:8 --nodes=4 requests 8 GPUs on each of 4 nodes, a 32-GPU allocation. The request supplies a capacity contract, but useful execution still depends on the selected hardware and the application’s launch configuration. Overstating requirements strands resources; understating them can cause memory failures or poor performance.

Slurm’s architecture consists of a central controller daemon (slurmctld) that maintains the global view of cluster state and makes scheduling decisions, and per-node daemons (slurmd) that manage local resources and execute jobs. This centralized architecture provides the authoritative allocation model discussed in section 8.2: the controller is the source of truth for resource assignments, reducing the risk of conflicting allocations. The cost is a potential single point of failure (addressed through active-passive failover) and a scheduling-throughput limit. As fleets grow, organizations may partition the cluster or federate schedulers to bound scheduling latency and failure impact; within a cluster, a typical ML configuration defines Slurm partitions by accelerator type and interconnect (table 2).

Within a partition, a request specifies both the number of GPUs a job needs and the allocation shape: how those GPUs are distributed across nodes. In Slurm, --gres=gpu:N or --gpus-per-node=N requests a per-node count, while --gpus=N requests a job-wide total. Whether the scheduler may split a node between jobs is a separate consumable-resource policy (Slurm’s select/cons_tres plugin), and a node-exclusive policy strands the unused devices in the six-on-eight example of section 8.4.2. Shape decides communication. In an eight-GPU-per-node pool, a 16-GPU request that also fixes eight GPUs per node (--gpus-per-node=8) yields two fully populated nodes, whose NVLink domains follow from the node hardware rather than the count. A request for 16 GPUs with no per-node constraint gives the scheduler more freedom, so it can start sooner on partially occupied nodes, but it may spread the group across several of them. Whether that flexibility is useful depends on the parallelism configuration and its communication budget. Tensor-parallel groups with frequent exchanges may justify compact placement, while data-parallel groups with well-overlapped communication may tolerate a wider allocation.

The fair-share scheduling mechanism uses past consumption to influence future priority under contention. An illustrative heuristic combines base priority, the user’s target fair share (\(\TargetFairShare\)), recent actual consumption (\(\RecentUsageShare\)), and a small stabilizing constant (\(\FairShareStabilizingConstant\)); it illustrates the feedback without reproducing Slurm’s multifactor priority calculation:

\[\text{Priority}_{\text{effective}} = \text{Priority}_{\text{base}} \times \frac{\TargetFairShare}{\RecentUsageShare+ \FairShareStabilizingConstant} \tag{1}\]

With base priority and target share fixed, equation 1 lowers priority as recent consumption increases. Doubling consumption approximately halves this illustrative priority when the stabilizing constant is negligible. Actual admission also depends on other priority factors and feasible resources; low recent usage does not guarantee an immediate start.

The time decay of usage history determines how quickly the system “forgives” past heavy usage. A half-life of one week means that a researcher’s heavy usage from two weeks ago contributes only 25 percent to their current usage calculation. Short half-lives make the policy more responsive to recent changes but can encourage alternating bursts of usage. Long half-lives retain the effect of a completed large project when its researchers return with smaller jobs. The useful decay window depends on the fleet’s job durations and the period over which the organization intends to balance access. Usage itself need not count every device alike. Trackable resources (TRES) let accounting charge different accelerator classes different billing weights in fair-share usage, an administrative valuation that tracks economic cost only as far as the chosen weights do.

Preemption policies enable high-priority jobs to reclaim resources from running workloads, and for ML training the reclaim must leave time to save state. Slurm expresses that time as a grace period. It signals the selected job (SIGTERM), waits the configured GraceTime, and only then kills it (SIGKILL); under PreemptMode=REQUEUE it can also requeue an eligible job to run again later. Neither the signal nor the requeue saves model state. The application must handle the signal, publish a valid checkpoint within the grace period, and restore it on restart. If termination precedes completion of that save, recovery uses the last valid checkpoint and replays subsequent work. Preemption therefore buys access for high-priority work with the victim’s replay and restart time, a price that section 8.8.1 develops.

↩︎ Checkpointing for progress preservation, including the checkpoint interval and coordination patterns, is established in Checkpointing and Progress Preservation.

Several Slurm features expose ML structure to the batch system. A hyperparameter sweep is the opposite of a gang. Job arrays submit many trials as one record (for example, --array=0-99), and each trial reads its index from the SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID environment variable to select its configuration. Slurm schedules the elements independently, so individual trials become ideal backfill candidates that fill small gaps in capacity, while the array keeps submission and management overhead low without making the trials one indivisible gang.

Heterogeneous jobs carry the heterogeneous gang of section 8.4.5 into a batch allocation. One job can contain components with distinct resource requests, admitted together. The 64-GPU fine-tuning run of the 175B model, for example, could request 8 GPU nodes for training and 2 CPU-only nodes for on-the-fly tokenization as a single allocation. It could then launch training on the GPU component and data workers on the CPU component (srun --het-group=0 and --het-group=1). This separation helps only if the data workers and transfer path sustain the training stage’s consumption rate; a heterogeneous allocation exposes that choice but does not itself balance the pipeline. At the boundaries of each allocation, prolog and epilog scripts turn job transitions into health checks. A prolog tests reported topology, ECC counters, and link status on each allocated node and drains a failing node before launch. An epilog removes the job’s orphan processes and shared-memory segments. They apply the quarantine idea of section 8.6.4 at job boundaries, reducing exposure to known faults without establishing delivered bandwidth.

Slurm’s strengths for ML training include explicit batch allocations, configurable fair-share policies, and integration with MPI-based distributed training frameworks. A mixed training-serving fleet also needs a service lifecycle that maintains ready replicas as demand and failures change. Inside a batch scheduler, adding or removing an inference replica means submitting or canceling a job, a far heavier operation than a controller adjusting a replica count, and a failed replica stays down until something resubmits it. Operators must integrate replica health, replacement, and demand-driven scaling with the batch scheduler, or assign those responsibilities to a service orchestration platform. Kubernetes starts from that service contract, in which users declare the desired state of services and jobs and controllers continuously reconcile the cluster toward it.

8.5.2 Kubernetes declarative container orchestration

When a cluster runs always-on inference services alongside training jobs, managing serving replicas as batch scripts forces operators to rebuild health checks, rolling updates, and replica replacement by hand. Kubernetes addresses this mixed workload by managing both services and batch jobs through a declarative model that expresses a workload’s desired state and uses control loops to reconcile observations with that state (Burns et al. 2016; Cloud Native Computing Foundation 2024). The unit it places is the pod, one or more containers that share a node and a network identity, and its default scheduler places pods one at a time. The name collides with the hardware pod of The Pod, the many racks joined under one fabric. A Kubernetes pod is a software unit that never spans more than one node, so a 64-pod training job is 64 container groups placed onto nodes, all of which can sit inside one hardware pod. Controllers above the scheduler give groups of pods a purpose: a Deployment keeps a set number of identical replica pods running, while a Job runs pods until they complete, retrying failures under a stated policy. The workload controller therefore determines what completion, replacement, or continued availability means.

Burns, Brendan, Brian Grant, David Oppenheimer, Eric Brewer, and John Wilkes. 2016. “Borg, Omega, and Kubernetes.” Communications of the ACM 59 (5): 50–57. https://doi.org/10.1145/2890784.
Cloud Native Computing Foundation. 2024. Kubernetes: Production-Grade Container Orchestration.

Failure handling reveals the importance of the workload contract. Slurm can detect node failures and requeue eligible jobs when configured to do so. Kubernetes controllers maintain workload-specific goals, such as a Deployment’s replica count or a Job’s completion and retry policy. A replacement pod still has to be placed, become ready, and rejoin the distributed training run before progress resumes. Training operators can coordinate worker creation, while the framework remains responsible for valid state and group recovery. Both platforms therefore require an explicit agreement between allocation, process lifecycle, and application recovery.

Kubernetes’ control loop architecture uses the controller pattern:6 controllers observe and update API objects through the API server, which persists cluster state in etcd, a replicated key-value store. Those objects contain desired state and reported observations, not instantaneous physical truth. The pattern repeats at every level: the Deployment controller reconciles the number of pod replicas, the scheduler binds pending pods to nodes, the kubelet (the agent on each node) keeps its assigned containers running, and the node controller marks nodes that stop reporting. Each component acts independently and communicates only through API objects, which makes the system resilient to the failure of any single controller. The price of that independence is that their interactions require clear ownership and retry behavior, particularly when a failed node’s last reported state no longer describes its execution.

6 Controller pattern (reconciliation loop): A “level-triggered” design reconciles the current desired and observed states rather than depending on every intermediate event. A restarted controller can resume from durable state if its actions tolerate retries and its observations expose unfinished work. Multiple controllers still need distinct responsibilities so that one controller’s correction does not repeatedly undo another’s.

Kubernetes needs an explicit resource representation before it can place GPU workloads. In the device-plugin model, device plugins expose accelerators as schedulable extended resources. The NVIDIA device plugin registers GPUs with the kubelet, enabling pod specifications that request GPU resources declaratively through the standard Kubernetes resource model. A pod then asks for whole GPUs by naming the nvidia.com/gpu resource in its limits (listing 1).

When the device plugin exposes exclusive whole GPUs, a small model serving occasional requests can reserve a device while using only a fraction of its compute and memory capacity. Whole-device allocation suits workloads that need the capacity or isolation, but can strand resources for smaller workloads. The resource unit exposed by the plugin therefore matters, because sharing or partitioning changes what the scheduler can allocate and what isolation each allocation receives.
