Device plugins can also expose hardware partitions, such as MIG instances, as schedulable units, a choice that section 8.10.1 develops with the serving workloads it suits.

Kubernetes’ default scheduler places pods independently, so it does not supply the group admission that section 8.4.3 requires. Under that default, a 64-pod training job receives 64 separate placement decisions, with no guarantee that all will succeed. If the cluster has resources for 60 pods but not 64, those 60 can occupy capacity while the remaining 4 wait. The platform must therefore configure a group-level admission and placement contract for a rigid job. PodGroup-style abstractions give that contract a unit matching the job’s minimum viable execution group rather than an individual pod.

The mechanism that closes this gap is group admission. A PodGroup declares a group’s minimum runnable membership. The scheduler tentatively reserves a placement for each member as it finds one but binds none of them to nodes until the minimum membership is placed, and it releases every reservation if the attempt fails or times out. Admission and placement thereby become two decisions, with the group admitted only when placement has succeeded for enough of it. The Volcano7 batch scheduler and the Coscheduling scheduler plugin both implement this pattern. Group admission does not make physical launches simultaneous; nodes can still fail or start at different times, so the framework must establish the communication group before training proceeds.

7 Volcano: Volcano provides a scheduler with batch and gang-scheduling mechanisms. Pods select it through their scheduler name, so it can coexist with Kubernetes’ default scheduler. The operational contract must account for interactions between schedulers sharing the same nodes; installing Volcano does not automatically transfer every workload’s scheduling to it.

War Story 8.1: OpenAI's gang-scheduling example (2021)
Context: OpenAI scaled Kubernetes clusters to 7,500 nodes for workloads including GPT-3, CLIP, and DALL-E. Its account explains that MPI-coordinated optimizer groups needed all members scheduled before training could progress (Sigler 2021).

Mechanism: The account illustrates the risk with two experiments each requesting the entire cluster. Incremental pod placement could allocate half the capacity to each, leaving neither with a complete group.

Impact: In that example, both jobs hold capacity without making progress. It demonstrates the deadlock mechanism rather than documenting a particular pair of stalled production runs.

Fix: OpenAI reports adopting the coscheduling plugin to add group-aware admission to the Kubernetes scheduler.

Systems lesson: Distributed ML scheduling must encode the job’s execution semantics. For MPI-style training, “some pods scheduled” is not partial progress; it is wasted allocation. Production schedulers need gang scheduling, quota controls, and workload-aware policies rather than generic pod placement alone.

Priority classes control scheduling precedence and preemption eligibility. A fleet might give inference higher priority than training to help protect serving service-level objectives (SLOs), but priority cannot replace ready capacity during a demand spike. With preemptionPolicy: PreemptLowerPriority, a pending pod can displace lower-priority pods when their removal makes placement feasible. With preemptionPolicy: Never, it retains queue precedence without initiating preemption and remains subject to higher-priority preemption. Termination, cleanup, and replacement startup take time. Training applications also need a checkpoint-aware shutdown path that fits the allowed grace period; assigning priorities does not create that path. The policy must balance serving readiness against repeated interruption of training.

Kueue8 makes admission a separate control decision. It manages when workloads may consume quota, while a compatible scheduler binds their pods to nodes. The admission policy and placement policy must agree on resource types, group requirements, and topology. Separating these responsibilities lets each layer run its own policy, at the price of keeping the two consistent. Kueue represents each team or project as a queue with a configured quota (a ClusterQueue) and groups queues into cohorts whose members may lend one another unused quota, under the borrowing and reclaim contract of section 8.11.2.

8 Kueue: Kueue separates workload admission from pod placement. Its all-or-nothing mechanisms include waiting for admitted pods to become ready and requeuing workloads that exceed the configured timeout. Aggregate quota admission alone does not prove that a gang fits the physical topology; the guarantee depends on the configured readiness, topology, and placement mechanisms.

8.5.3 Kubernetes ecosystem for ML training

Group admission handles one constraint that microservice orchestration leaves implicit and distributed training cannot. Four more remain. Workers must launch knowing their peers, collective traffic must avoid network layers built for request traffic, checkpoint writes need bandwidth rather than only storage capacity, and progress must be observable below the container. Operators and plugins add each constraint to the platform; the mechanism matters more than the product that happens to implement it.

Peer discovery is the job of the training operator ecosystem, which extends Kubernetes with job-specific resource types (custom resource definitions). In the Kubeflow Training Operator, a PyTorchJob declares the number of workers, the container image, and resource requirements. The operator creates the pods and injects the rendezvous address (MASTER_ADDR and MASTER_PORT), the group size, and each worker’s rank (WORLD_SIZE and RANK), which the framework uses to form its communication group. If a worker pod fails, the operator can detect the deviation and create a replacement pod to restore the desired replica count, but training-state recovery still depends on the framework’s checkpoint and rendezvous logic.

Collective traffic is the second constraint. The pod overlay network that the Container Network Interface (CNI) configures, designed for microservice requests, routes packets through software switching on the host CPU, which adds latency and caps bandwidth when it sits on the critical gradient-synchronization path. For bandwidth-sensitive training, host networking (hostNetwork: true) places pods in the host’s network namespace, bypassing the ordinary pod overlay path. It does not itself allocate remote direct memory access (RDMA) devices or grant permission to use them; device exposure, resource requests, drivers, and access controls require separate configuration. Operators such as NVIDIA’s Network Operator automate that exposure through RDMA device plugins and GPUDirect RDMA configuration, letting containerized workloads approach bare-metal interconnect performance once the rest of the stack is configured.

Checkpoint bandwidth is the third constraint, because storage integration must account for the checkpoint storm of definition 4.5, in which many workers write large amounts of state together. Kubernetes PersistentVolumeClaims (PVCs) request capacity on a configured storage system but do not reserve checkpoint throughput. A 1,024-GPU training job writing the 2.45 TB resumable checkpoint of the 175B model needs a measured write budget shared with other workloads. Backend bandwidth controls, client throttling, or staggered writes can limit interference; the platform must verify which controls the storage implementation enforces.

Visibility into progress is the fourth. The observability stack for training clusters combines general-purpose cluster monitoring (commonly Prometheus and Grafana) with accelerator telemetry such as the Data Center GPU Manager (DCGM) exporter, which reports utilization, memory bandwidth, temperature, and ECC errors. These observations locate symptoms. Correlating them with step progress, input waits, and communication traces is what distinguishes useful computation from stalls and identifies which dependency limits a training job.

↪ Fleet telemetry across device, network, and facility levels, and the correlation that narrows a failure domain, is developed in Fleet telemetry and hardware observability.

The 1,024-GPU pretraining run for our 175B model illustrates how these responsibilities fit together. One possible deployment defines a PyTorchJob with 128 worker pods, each requesting 8 GPUs. Host networking removes the pod overlay from the gradient path, and the Network Operator exposes each node’s InfiniBand adapters to the workers through RDMA device plugins. A PVC backed by Lustre provides access to the 6 TB tokenized training corpus and a checkpoint path whose sustained bandwidth must be validated. DCGM metrics stream to Prometheus, supporting alerts on uncorrectable ECC errors and deviations from workload-specific communication baselines.

8.5.4 Paradigm trade-offs and selection criteria

Selecting a control plane requires matching its allocation and recovery contract to the workload mix. Rigid training groups need reliable atomic placement and checkpoint-aware interruption; serving replicas need readiness checks and continuous recovery. Existing operational expertise and the cost of running a second control plane also shape the choice. A fleet dedicated to long, homogeneous training runs that use bare-metal collectives, operated by a team fluent in batch scheduling, gains most from Slurm’s authoritative allocation and mature fair-share. A fleet that mixes training with always-on serving, and already runs cloud-native deployment and observability tooling, gains more from Kubernetes with a gang-admission layer added for its rigid jobs. The Slurm vs. Kubernetes trade-off organizes around the dimensions most relevant to ML workloads (table 3).

The gang-scheduling row of the table compresses a naming collision that is easy to miss. This chapter uses gang scheduling for atomic allocation of a whole execution group, which is the requirement distributed training imposes. Slurm reserves the name for the older, Ousterhout-style meaning of the term, in which co-scheduled jobs share nodes by time-slicing in synchronized rounds. Slurm provides atomic multi-node allocation under its ordinary job model, so the requirement is met even though Slurm’s “gang scheduling” option means something else.

The choice of scheduling paradigm also reflects organizational trajectory. Teams starting with HPC-focused training infrastructure may begin with Slurm and add Kubernetes for serving. Teams starting from cloud-native application infrastructure may begin with Kubernetes and add HPC-oriented extensions (Volcano, Kueue) for training. Slurm has meanwhile added a REST interface and container support, so the two platforms now overlap in capability. Either path can reach a functional hybrid architecture, but the starting point determines which capabilities are strongest and which require the most investment to develop.

8.5.5 Hybrid architectures in practice

While the choice between Slurm and Kubernetes often appears binary, engineering organizations can deploy hybrid architectures that use the strengths of both systems. The relevant boundary again follows workload semantics rather than labels. Rigid, topology-sensitive jobs fit a batch scheduler’s atomic allocation contract, while malleable services fit a reconciliation loop that adjusts replica count continuously. Some training jobs are elastic, and some serving replicas are rigid multi-accelerator gangs, so neither “training” nor “serving” selects a control plane by itself.

The Kubernetes-over-Slurm pattern wraps the HPC scheduler within a cloud-native control plane. Kubernetes manages the lifecycle of training jobs through custom operators while delegating batch admission and placement to Slurm. The central engineering problem is ownership: exactly one control plane must authorize each placement, while the outer controller reconciles lifecycle state without issuing a conflicting allocation. The arrangement combines a declarative management surface with Slurm’s batch and topology policies, but it also creates a cross-control-plane failure boundary that operators must fence explicitly.

The Slurm-alongside-Kubernetes pattern takes a coarser-grained approach, maintaining separate clusters for training and serving that draw from a shared pool of physical nodes. A meta-scheduler or capacity broker acts as an arbitrator, dynamically reassigning nodes between the Slurm partition and the Kubernetes cluster based on demand signals. During intensive training campaigns, nodes are drained from Kubernetes and joined to Slurm; during product launches or traffic spikes, the flow reverses. The primary challenge is the mode-switching cost: draining a node, changing its ownership and configuration, and admitting it to the other control plane takes much longer than an ordinary placement decision. This latency limits sharing to planned capacity shifts rather than second-by-second inference bursts.

A shared application runtime such as Ray provides task and actor abstractions for both training and serving. Ray’s placement groups, unlike the cloud network-locality abstraction of the same name, reserve bundles of logical resources for work that must run together, and serving replicas can also require multi-device groups. Inside a shared runtime the workloads keep their different state, latency, and recovery requirements, so the platform must still coordinate Ray’s logical allocations with the underlying resource manager and validate the isolation and recovery behavior needed by each workload.

Threading these patterns together, consider the lifecycle of our 175B parameter model. A hybrid platform can allocate the training gang through Slurm and later place serving replicas through Kubernetes. The orchestration boundary concerns resource ownership: the training job releases its allocation, and the serving control plane admits replicas against its own capacity and readiness requirements. Deciding whether the trained artifact is ready for production requires validation and release policy beyond the scheduler; CI/CD for ML at Scale develops those validation gates and rollout policies.

A hybrid platform is useful when its separate allocation contracts justify the additional ownership and recovery boundary. The cost to measure is the coordination between control planes, including node draining and reassignment, rather than a presumed performance advantage from either product name. Because Slurm and Kubernetes now overlap in capability, the paradigm choice is a workload-specific design decision, and teams must establish the allocation and recovery guarantees they need before layering physical placement constraints on top of them.

Checkpoint 8.1: Scheduling paradigm trade-offs

Before the chapter turns to topology-aware placement, review the core trade-offs:

A control plane can now represent, admit, and reconcile an allocation, yet it still counts accelerators as if any group of the requested size were interchangeable. Two allocations of the same size can meet very different communication budgets depending on where the devices sit in the cluster fabric. The next layer of the scheduler must therefore weigh physical location alongside resource count.

Self-Check: Question

  1. What is the fundamental difference in control philosophy between Slurm and Kubernetes when managing distributed ML training workloads?

    1. Slurm relies on continuous reconciliation loops against desired state stored in etcd, whereas Kubernetes executes imperative one-time allocation scripts via a centralized daemon.
    2. Slurm uses peer-to-peer gossip protocols to schedule tasks across nodes, whereas Kubernetes enforces hierarchical master-slave token ring passing.
    3. Slurm employs an imperative allocation model where users request explicit physical resources guaranteed at launch, whereas Kubernetes uses a declarative model where controllers continuously reconcile observed cluster state toward declared desired state.
    4. Slurm schedules containerized microservices with dynamic horizontal auto-scaling, whereas Kubernetes only supports uncontainerized bare-metal MPI processes.

  2. Kubernetes’ default scheduler places a 64-pod training job one pod at a time, and the cluster has room for only 60 of the pods. How does a PodGroup with a minimum membership of 64 change the outcome?

    1. The scheduler binds the first 60 pods immediately and starts training with a smaller communication group, adding the last 4 pods as capacity frees.
    2. The scheduler tentatively reserves placements but binds no pod until all 64 members are placed, releasing every reservation if the attempt fails or times out.
    3. The scheduler preempts every lower-priority pod in the cluster until 64 GPUs are free, regardless of priority-class settings.
    4. The PodGroup guarantees that all 64 containers start at the same instant, so the framework no longer needs to establish its communication group.

  3. In the illustrative fair-share priority model (\(\text{Priority}_{\text{effective}} = \text{Priority}_{\text{base}} \times \frac{F_{\text{target}}}{F_{\text{actual}} + \epsilon}\)), what role does the usage decay half-life play, and what is the trade-off between setting a short versus a long half-life?

  4. Why do high-performance distributed training jobs in Kubernetes typically enable hostNetwork: true and leverage the NVIDIA Network Operator instead of relying on default Container Network Interface (CNI) overlay networks?

  5. True or False: Kueue replaces the native Kubernetes kube-scheduler binary entirely to implement custom gang scheduling algorithms, whereas Volcano acts purely as an admission controller layered on top of the default scheduler.

  6. Order the dependencies in this illustrative launch path for a Kubernetes training job configured with a compatible training operator and Volcano group admission:

  1. Volcano verifies a feasible placement for the PodGroup’s minimum viable membership.
  2. The developer submits the training-job manifest defining workers and resource requirements.
  3. Volcano authorizes group placement, and node agents launch the admitted workers using the separately configured device and network resources.
  4. Launched workers establish their communication group through rendezvous before synchronized training begins.
  5. The operator and configured scheduling integration create worker pods and their PodGroup.

8.6 Topology-Aware Scheduling

A scattered allocation can place frequent collective traffic on shared cross-rack paths, exposing latency or bandwidth limits that a more compact allocation avoids. The penalty is set by how much of the job’s traffic crosses those paths and how much capacity the fabric provisions there, not by the rack count itself. The earlier feasibility rules already required compatible topology; topology-aware scheduling makes that requirement explicit in the placement objective, accounting for communication paths as well as raw compute.

8.6.1 The topology hierarchy

Splitting a tensor-parallel group across node boundaries moves its per-layer activation exchanges off intra-node NVLink onto the external network fabric, where an H100’s per-direction NVLink rate is 9× the rate of one NDR port (Bandwidth hierarchy). Each level of a cluster’s communication hierarchy comes from the hardware that builds it and sets a distinct bandwidth and latency budget. GPUs inside a node exchange data over NVLink, the tier fast enough for the per-layer exchanges of tensor parallelism. Nodes under one leaf switch communicate over an external fabric such as InfiniBand or RoCE. Racks joined through spine switches add switch hops whose delivered bandwidth depends on concurrent flows and oversubscription even in a fat-tree. Cross-rack communication also adds tail latency variability: while median latency may be only slightly higher, p99 latency can spike during congestion events, creating straggler effects in synchronous training where the slowest communicator determines the pace for all workers.

↩︎ Bisection bandwidth and fat-tree network topologies are established in Fat-tree (Clos) networks.

A 64-GPU job within one rack can avoid cross-rack paths that a spread allocation must use. It outperforms the spread allocation only when those paths add exposed latency or bandwidth contention; a non-blocking, uncongested spine need not reduce sustained throughput. The performance difference stems directly from the hierarchical communication analysis in Hierarchical AllReduce: a flat ring AllReduce runs at the speed of the slowest link in the ring, and cross-rack hops introduce both higher latency and increased contention risk. For synchronous training, every iteration waits for the slowest worker to complete its AllReduce, so even occasional cross-rack congestion inflates the Fleet Law’s communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) (equation 1.3) on every step it touches.

The scheduler must therefore model the cluster as a hierarchical graph with discrete bandwidth boundaries (figure 7). In the illustrated H100 fleet, per-direction bandwidth steps down from 450 GB/s across the NVSwitch inside a node to 50 GB/s per port under a leaf switch, and to roughly 12.5–25 GB/s per endpoint when balanced traffic saturates an oversubscribed spine. A node boundary can introduce a large bandwidth reduction, but a non-blocking rack or pod boundary need not reduce per-endpoint bandwidth. Oversubscription adds a separate constraint when traffic saturates shared uplinks. Topology-aware placement minimizes the communication cost of the job’s actual traffic pattern rather than applying a fixed penalty to every boundary.

For a 4-GPU collective group, the routing paths make that cost concrete (figure 8). In the illustrated contended fabric, scattering ranks across Rack A and Rack B forces a 3-hop leaf-spine-leaf path with about 12.5 GB/s of effective bandwidth per endpoint, a share of contended uplinks rather than a physical link rate. Consolidating all four ranks (G0–G3) under Rack A’s leaf switch avoids those shared uplinks and provides about 50 GB/s per GPU under the example’s load assumptions.

8.6.2 Placement algorithms

The illustrated 4× bandwidth difference between the 1-hop leaf path and the 3-hop spine route (figure 8) stretches the transfer component by the same factor when transfer time is bandwidth-dominated. Crossing a spine does not impose that ratio independently of load and topology. Whether communication exceeds computation also depends on the model and overlap schedule, and repeated exchanges can amplify the exposed cost. Topology-aware placement algorithms assign jobs to nodes that reduce this cost, turning resource packing into graph optimization over the physical topology. A simple locality cost, defined in equation 2, penalizes allocations spanning multiple topology domains:

\[ \text{Cost}_{\text{locality}} = \sum_{i < j} \LocalityPenaltyFunction(\GpuTopologicalDistance(\AssignedJobGpu_i, \AssignedJobGpu_j)) \tag{2}\]

where \(\AssignedJobGpu_i\) and \(\AssignedJobGpu_j\) are GPUs assigned to the job, \(\GpuTopologicalDistance(\AssignedJobGpu_i, \AssignedJobGpu_j)\) is their topological distance (0 for same node, 1 for same rack, 2 for different racks), and \(\LocalityPenaltyFunction(\cdot)\) is a weighting function that increases with distance. The scheduler minimizes this score when selecting an allocation from the set of feasible placements. An illustrative heuristic assigns locality penalties of \(\LocalityPenaltyFunction(0) = 0\) (same node, NVLink, no penalty), \(\LocalityPenaltyFunction(1) = 1\) (same rack, one switch hop), and \(\LocalityPenaltyFunction(2) = 10\) (different racks, multiple switch hops). These dimensionless weights are policy assumptions rather than measured latency or bandwidth ratios, so a deployment must calibrate them to its traffic and topology. An unweighted pairwise score still cannot distinguish heavily communicating rank pairs from pairs that rarely exchange data.

More sophisticated algorithms distinguish the communication requirements of different parallelism strategies. Frequent tensor-parallel activation exchanges favor a high-bandwidth, low-latency domain that meets the measured communication budget; that domain need not coincide with one server. Pipeline parallelism exchanges activations across stage boundaries rather than between every layer, but its tolerance for link delay depends on microbatch size and the pipeline schedule. Data-parallel gradient synchronization can overlap computation, making wider placement feasible when the exposed communication remains within budget. These patterns guide placement without establishing a universal ranking for every model or fabric.

Algorithm 1 combines the all-or-nothing gang constraint of section 8.4.3, the locality cost of equation 2, and an illustrative parallelism-to-topology mapping. Its enumeration expresses the decision conceptually: construct feasible rank mappings, score their allocations, and choose the lowest illustrative locality score, leaving the job queued when none fits. This surrogate does not guarantee minimum communication time or completion time. A fleet-scale implementation must restrict that search using heuristics rather than enumerate every allocation.

\(N\)

Applied to a whole job, the same reasoning ranks placement strategies. The latencies in this comparison are illustrative, not derived. They are constructed to stay above the ring-AllReduce bandwidth bound for the payload, and they show the order in which placement removes delay rather than values a reader could recompute from topology names. For a 0.5 GB gradient synchronization on 64 GPUs, random placement spans multiple racks and spine switches and takes an assumed 120 ms. Each successive strategy removes a source of delay that the previous one leaves. Confining the group to a single rack removes spine hops and reduces latency to 85 ms. Rail-optimized alignment maps ranks so that every member of each collective group sits at the same local GPU index and therefore on the same network rail (developed in section 8.6.3). Traffic that previously hopped between rails through the spine now stays within one rail’s switches, which cuts latency to 40 ms. Topology-optimal placement additionally avoids leaf ports that other jobs are using and reaches 25 ms, a 4.8× reduction (figure 9, where each bar also carries its algorithm throughput). This ratio compares whole collectives under four assumed placements, so it need not match the link-bandwidth ratio of the two-rack paths above.

A hierarchical placement algorithm applies these constraints from the most restrictive communication domain outward, matching each 3D parallelism dimension to the interconnect tier its traffic can tolerate (figure 10). In the illustrated fleet, it places each tensor-parallel group within an NVLink-connected node, where the per-layer activation exchanges run at 450 GB/s. It places consecutive pipeline stages under the same leaf switch, where stage boundaries cross at 50 GB/s. It distributes data-parallel replicas across racks only when the once-per-step gradient synchronization fits the roughly 12.5–25 GB/s per endpoint that an oversubscribed spine leaves. This alignment reduces costly boundary crossings, but placement alone cannot reserve the fabric’s full bisection bandwidth against other jobs.

Evaluating all possible placements for a 256-GPU job across a 10,000-GPU cluster is combinatorially intractable, so production schedulers rely on heuristic approaches. Common choices include greedy algorithms that build placements bottom-up (first fill NVLink domains, then fill racks, then spread across racks), tree-search algorithms with pruning that explore the most promising placements first, and scoring-based approaches that evaluate a sample of feasible placements and select the best. Long jobs can amortize a more expensive search, but frequent submissions, short jobs, and recovery bursts also consume scheduler capacity, making end-to-end completion time, including placement delay, the useful comparison rather than execution throughput alone.

Napkin Math 8.2: Placement impact on 3D-parallel training
Scenario: Consider a 256-GPU training job using 3D parallelism: 8-way tensor parallel, 4-way pipeline parallel, 8-way data parallel.

Good placement (topology-aware):

  • Tensor parallel groups: 32 groups of 8 GPUs, each confined to one 8-GPU node (NVLink: 450 GB/s per direction)
  • Pipeline stages: 4 consecutive nodes within the same rack (1 switch hop, InfiniBand: 50 GB/s per direction)
  • Data parallel replicas: 8 racks (3 switch hops through leaf-spine-leaf paths)

Bad placement (topology-unaware):

  • Tensor parallel groups split across 2 nodes (InfiniBand instead of NVLink)
  • Pipeline stages scattered across racks (3 switch hops instead of 1)
  • Data parallel replicas randomly distributed

Math: The tensor parallel communication alone illustrates the impact. With NVLink delivering 450 GB/s per direction, a 1 GB activation transfer takes approximately 2.2 ms. Over InfiniBand at 50 GB/s, the same transfer takes approximately 20 ms, a 9× slowdown.

Systems insight: This calculation prices only the tensor-parallel component of the bad placement. Splitting a tensor-parallel group across nodes makes each activation exchange 9× slower, and the group performs such exchanges at every transformer layer of every step, so the penalty multiplies with model depth rather than appearing once. How much of it reaches step time depends on how much of the exchange was hidden behind computation, and it is why hierarchical placement gives tensor-parallel groups the fastest tier first. The scattered pipeline stages and data-parallel replicas add their own penalties on top, and the scheduler must profile the complete job before assigning a throughput benefit to the placement.

8.6.3 Rail-optimized scheduling

Even when two nodes sit in the same pod, placing corresponding data-parallel shards on mismatched local GPU indices forces their gradient collective to cross between network rails through the spine instead of staying on a single rail switch. In rail-optimized topologies, corresponding GPU positions across nodes connect through parallel network “rails” that a suitable rank mapping can use concurrently. Balanced bandwidth depends on distributing traffic across those paths rather than concentrating it on a subset. Keeping a collective group on one rail avoids cross-rail transfers for that group, while the placement of other groups determines how much rail capacity it must share.

↩︎ Rail-optimized fabric architectures and rail-to-rail traffic isolation are established in Rail-optimized topology.

Rail-aware scheduling assigns corresponding model shards across data-parallel replicas to GPUs attached to the same rail. When intra-node parallelism partitions a model replica across the eight GPUs of a node, each data-parallel gradient collective reduces only one shard \(\LocalRankIndex\) across replicas rather than combining all eight GPUs on the node. In the eight-GPU-per-node example, local GPU index \(\LocalRankIndex\) connects to rail \(\LocalRankIndex\). Placing every replica’s copy of shard \(\LocalRankIndex\) at that same local index on its selected node means the data-parallel replica rank varies across nodes rather than across local GPU indices, so the collective for shard \(\LocalRankIndex\) stays entirely within rail \(\LocalRankIndex\). This alignment removes cross-rail transfers for that group, though other groups or tenants can still contend for the same rail’s links, and full dedicated bandwidth requires an explicit capacity and sharing contract.

Rail alignment therefore acts at two levels, and they belong to different components. Within a node it is a rank-mapping decision. The launcher or framework must give every member of a data-parallel group the same local GPU index on its node, which is what removes the cross-rail hops in the placement comparison above. A mapping that puts shard \(\LocalRankIndex\) at index \(\LocalRankIndex\) on one node and at another index elsewhere sends that group’s traffic between rails. Across nodes it constrains the scheduler only when allocations are smaller than a node. When each replica’s pipeline stage occupies a complete node and every node wires local GPU \(\LocalRankIndex\) to rail \(\LocalRankIndex\), any free node already offers every rail, so rail alignment does not narrow which racks the scheduler can choose. With partial-node allocations, a job can receive different local indices on different nodes, and the scheduler must then choose GPU positions as well as nodes so that each group’s members share a rail. Those positions can conflict with the tensor-parallel budget and the paths between pipeline stages when only particular GPU positions remain free. The packing cost therefore follows the allocation granularity and the available topology rather than an inherent conflict among the three parallelism dimensions. Even a single-node allocation can have meaningful topology constraints when GPUs occupy different interconnect, PCIe, or non-uniform memory access (NUMA) domains, so packing efficiency should be optimized only among placements that satisfy the job’s local and external communication contract.

8.6.4 Topology discovery and dynamic adaptation

When a spine switch fails or an optical link degrades and begins retransmitting, a static configuration file such as Slurm’s topology.conf or Kubernetes node labels still reports the rack as healthy and full-bandwidth. The scheduler then places communication-heavy gangs onto a crippled path. Runtime topology discovery supplements static maps with information exposed by the operating system and device libraries. NCCL uses system topology information, including the PCIe hierarchy and network devices exposed through paths such as /sys/class/infiniband, to identify communication paths and network interface card (NIC) proximity. The discovered graph describes reported connectivity; health checks and traffic measurements must establish whether those paths deliver the expected performance.

Cluster topology is an operating state, not a fixed schematic. Hardware failures, maintenance windows, and thermal throttling change the paths and effective capacity available to the scheduler. A spine switch failure in a redundant Clos network can leave connectivity intact while removing capacity. The effect on our 175B parameter model’s data-parallel AllReduce depends on rerouting, concurrent traffic, and how much bandwidth the job was using. If the failure increases exposed communication time, the scheduler must compare continuing at reduced throughput with the cost of moving to a healthier allocation. A degraded link can also pass binary up/down probes while cutting delivered bandwidth, so the scheduler needs performance evidence such as retransmission counts and collective bandwidth, the link-level telemetry of Link-level telemetry, as well as reachability.

A checkpoint-and-restart migration spends time saving state, releasing the old allocation, obtaining a new one, and restoring execution. Its value depends on measured transition cost and remaining work. If migration takes 10 minutes and restores throughput after a sustained 20 percent penalty, it pays back in less than an hour of subsequent training. That comparison assumes a healthy destination is available and the gain persists. A practical policy also accounts for queue delay and requires evidence that placement causes the slowdown, such as rising collective time on the affected path, before it moves the job.

War Story 8.2: Performance-aware health checks (2021)
Context: OpenAI’s account of scaling Kubernetes describes automated health checks that remove misbehaving nodes from the schedulable pool (Sigler 2021).

Mechanism: Passive checks monitor reachability, disks, and GPU errors, but error codes do not expose every hardware or driver problem. Active GPU tests exercise the device before admitting ordinary workloads and periodically during its lifetime.

Fix: Nodes enter with a preflight taint, a node marking that repels ordinary pods, which blocks normal scheduling until the tests pass. Failed passive checks cordon a node; more serious failures can trigger eviction requests.

Systems lesson: Reachability alone is not a sufficient admission test. A scheduler needs health evidence that reflects the resources its jobs depend on, together with a policy for removing suspect nodes from placement.

Sigler, Eric. 2021. Scaling Kubernetes to 7,500 Nodes. OpenAI Blog.

9 Taints and tolerations: Node taints can block scheduling of pods without matching tolerations. A toleration permits consideration of a node but neither requires placement there nor reserves it exclusively for training. Dedicated GPU pools also need controlled toleration assignment and placement constraints; removing existing workloads requires an eviction or drain policy appropriate to the taint’s effect.

The general scheduling idea is quarantine: when runtime evidence says a node, rack, or link is degraded, the scheduler should stop placing sensitive work there even if the nominal resource count still looks available. Kubernetes implements this pattern through taints and tolerations,9 while Slurm clusters commonly use drain states, node features, or partition rules. The complementary problem is how jobs adapt when available resources change dynamically. Rather than treating resource allocation as fixed for a job’s lifetime, elastic training allows logical jobs to grow, shrink, and recover. Whether worker processes restart depends on the framework; TorchElastic restarts its worker group when membership changes.

↩︎ Slow components that pass liveness checks while setting the pace of every synchronous step, and how to tell them from fail-stop failures, are established in Stragglers versus fail-stop failures.

Self-Check: Question

  1. When scheduling a 3D-parallel large language model across a multi-rack GPU cluster, how should Tensor Parallelism (TP), Pipeline Parallelism (PP), and Data Parallelism (DP) be mapped across the topology hierarchy to maximize throughput?

    1. Map DP strictly within intra-node NVLink domains, TP across inter-rack spine switches, and PP across rail-aligned network switches.
    2. Map PP strictly within intra-node NVLink domains, DP across Top-of-Rack switches, and TP across multi-hop spine switches.
    3. Map TP to the highest-bandwidth domain, place PP stages along suitable low-contention paths, and align corresponding DP shards with network rails while checking available capacity.
    4. Map TP, PP, and DP uniformly at random across all available nodes to balance switch thermal loads across the datacenter fabric.

  2. A persistent degraded switch path lowers measured end-to-end training throughput by 30 percent. A checkpoint-and-migration pause would take 10 minutes, healthy replacement capacity is available, and days of training remain. What is the appropriate orchestration response?

    1. Continue running the job indefinitely because a 10-minute checkpoint-and-reschedule migration would cost more compute than a 30% slowdown over a multi-week run.
    2. Rely on the GPU driver to disable NVLink and route all tensor-parallel traffic through host memory over PCIe.
    3. Trigger a performance-aware topology quarantine via taints or drain states, checkpoint the running job, and migrate it to healthy nodes where the break-even time is under an hour.
    4. Terminate the job and permanently de-allocate all 10,000 GPUs in the datacenter until all network transceivers are manually replaced.

  3. In an 8-GPU-per-node cluster with eight network rails, how should rail-aware placement map corresponding model shards across replicas, and what contention limits remain?

  4. Why are static topology configuration files (such as Slurm’s topology.conf or static Kubernetes node labels) insufficient on their own for optimal collective placement in large-scale ML clusters?

  5. For the illustrated eight-GPU-per-node fleet, explain how a scheduler combines tensor-parallel locality, pipeline paths, and data-parallel rail alignment when selecting a placement. Why does minimizing the chapter’s illustrative locality score not establish minimum communication time?

  6. In cloud orchestration environments such as AWS and GCP, a user specifies a ____ to request close placement of virtual machine instances for high-bandwidth, low-latency communication.

See Answers →

8.7 Elastic Scheduling

When cluster demand fluctuates, rigid allocation creates two categories of waste. During off-peak hours, a job running on 256 GPUs cannot absorb idle resources to speed up training. During peak demand, the same job cannot release excess resources to accommodate higher-priority work without being fully preempted. The scheduler’s only options are “let it keep all its resources” or “kill it entirely,” with nothing in between.

Elastic scheduling expands this binary choice. The recovery mechanics of elastic training show how a distributed job can adjust its worker count, adapt its batch policy, and reconstruct its communication group after a failure. From the scheduler’s perspective, the same capability solves a resource-allocation problem under fluctuating demand. A training job might start with 128 GPUs, scale up to 256 when resources become available, and scale back to 192 when higher-priority work arrives, provided each allocation supports a viable worker group. Resizing may pause training and restart workers from saved state. The scheduler therefore chooses among supported allocation sizes, each with a transition cost, rather than assuming uninterrupted progress at any resource level.

↩︎ Batch-size and learning-rate adjustment and communication-group contraction during elastic recovery are established in Recovery adaptation mechanisms.

8.7.1 The scheduler-framework contract

If a scheduler reclaims four nodes from a running job by abruptly killing their containers, or adds four idle nodes without triggering a new rendezvous, the surviving ranks treat the change as a fatal collective timeout rather than an elastic resize. Resizing a live job without crashing it requires an explicit contract between the scheduler and the training framework. For planned resizing, the scheduler and framework coordinate resource release through defined signals and an agreed adaptation window. Unexpected failures can bypass that window, so the framework must also recover from valid saved state. Its recovery path reconstructs communication groups and either preserves the global batch or applies a validated batch-size and learning-rate policy. The worker-membership lifecycle therefore behaves as a state machine centered on active training (figure 11). Idle GPUs trigger scale-up, preemptions trigger scale-down, and host timeouts or ECC errors divert ranks to worker replacement; each path returns to active training once the communication group is rebuilt or a spare node joins.

From the scheduler’s perspective, this contract is defined by three governing parameters: the elastic range, the transition cost, and the scaling efficiency curve. The elastic range, \([\ElasticMinimumWorkerCount, \ElasticMaximumWorkerCount]\), specifies lower and upper worker bounds; the job’s parallelism layout determines the valid allocation increments within them. The transition cost includes any checkpoint, restart, state redistribution, and rendezvous required by the runtime. Measuring the full interruption determines how frequently the scheduler can trigger reallocations before transition overhead wipes out any throughput advantage. Finally, the scaling efficiency curve quantifies the marginal throughput gained from each added worker, indicating whether assigning idle GPUs to this job yields more aggregate progress than holding them for an assembling gang-scheduled workload. Together, these parameters provide the scheduler with the quantitative bounds needed to resize jobs dynamically without treating elasticity as free capacity.

The scheduler must also account for the effect of worker count on global batch size (\(\GlobalBatchSize = \PerWorkerBatchSize\times \DeviceCount\)), where the per-worker batch counts examples contributing to one optimizer update, including gradient accumulation. Holding the global batch constant requires each remaining worker to process more examples after a shrink. Larger microbatches consume more memory; additional accumulation steps extend the update time. Preserving the batch removes one source of optimization change but does not guarantee identical convergence. Allowing the global batch to change instead requires validating the learning-rate policy and resulting model quality, so throughput alone cannot determine the best allocation.

TorchElastic sets the elastic range in its launch configuration as a minimum and maximum node count (listing 2). There, the --nnodes=4:16 specification configures TorchElastic’s rendezvous and worker-membership range as 4 to 16 nodes (32 to 128 workers with 8 processes per node). It does not acquire or resize cluster resources; a separate cluster scheduler or operator must place the nodes and start or stop elastic agents as capacity changes.

Systems Perspective 8.2: Elastic vs. rigid scheduling
Consider a congested cluster where a 128-GPU job waits 8 hours for a full gang allocation. This first comparison assumes throughput scales linearly with worker count, equivalent learning progress per processed example, and negligible resize overhead; the later scaling decision relaxes the throughput and transition assumptions.

Rigid scheduling: Wait 8 hours, then train for 24 hours at full throughput. Total wall-clock: 32 hours.

Elastic scheduling (min 32, max 128):

  • Start immediately with 32 GPUs (throughput = 25 percent of full)
  • At hour 2, when 64 GPUs free, scale to 64 (throughput = 50 percent)
  • At hour 8, when the full gang frees and the rigid job would also start, scale to 128 (throughput = 100 percent)

Work completed during the 8-hour queue wait, in hours of full-scale training:

  • Hours 0 to 2: 25 percent throughput \(\times\) 2 hours = 0.5
  • Hours 2 to 8: 50 percent throughput \(\times\) 6 hours = 3
  • Total: 3.5 hours of equivalent full-scale work

The elastic job completes in approximately 28.5 hours (8 hours of partial-scale training plus 20.5 hours at full scale), saving 3.5 hours compared to rigid scheduling. This improvement of about 11 percent comes entirely from using resources productively during what would otherwise be idle queue time.

The scheduler cannot treat every worker count as a valid elastic configuration. Model parallelism constrains the allocation increment when the resize policy keeps tensor and pipeline layouts fixed and adds or removes whole data-parallel replicas. For a 175B-parameter job that relies on 8-way tensor parallelism within each node and 8 pipeline stages across nodes, this policy operates at the granularity of a whole model replica of 64 GPUs. The pipeline depth of 8 is the smallest power of two at which each GPU’s share of the mixed-precision Adaptive Moment Estimation (Adam) training state fits in its HBM when that state is not sharded across replicas. The feasible layout of Distributed Training instead uses a shallower pipeline and shards optimizer state across the data-parallel group with ZeRO (Memory-efficient data parallelism with ZeRO and FSDP). Keeping each replica’s state self-contained costs memory, but it lets the scheduler add or remove a replica without redistributing every other replica’s optimizer shards. The job can scale between 128 nodes (1,024 GPUs) and 32 nodes (256 GPUs) one replica at a time, changing the data-parallel degree. Removing a single GPU from a tensor-parallel group would instead require a new sharding layout and redistribution of training state. Changing the pipeline degree would also require repartitioning stages and rebuilding the pipeline schedule. A runtime that supports these transitions can expose them to the scheduler, but their feasibility and measured costs belong in the resize contract rather than following automatically from data-parallel elasticity.

The transition cost also constrains the scheduler’s resize frequency. Each scaling event can interrupt useful work while the framework reconstructs its communication group and restores or redistributes state. The duration must be measured for the chosen runtime and checkpoint path. The scheduler must amortize this cost over the subsequent run duration, so small resize increments are only worthwhile if the job will run at the new scale for significantly longer than the transition window. Section 8.7.3 shows the thrashing that results when a policy ignores this amortization.

8.7.2 Scheduler integration

Without scheduler integration, a job that declares an elastic range \([\ElasticMinimumWorkerCount, \ElasticMaximumWorkerCount]\) in its launch script still waits in the queue for its full node count and still gets killed outright when a higher-priority job arrives. Integrating the \([\ElasticMinimumWorkerCount, \ElasticMaximumWorkerCount]\) bounds into the scheduler lets it start a job early at the minimum, expand the job into newly idle capacity, or shrink it to release borrowed capacity without terminating the logical job. Which action wins depends on the job’s scaling curve and transition cost, and the policies below compare the choices by the work completed over the same time window.

Each action must respect the framework contract. The most consequential of the three is graceful preemption: when higher-priority work arrives, the scheduler first shrinks an elastic job toward \(\ElasticMinimumWorkerCount\), reclaiming whole replicas at the next resize boundary, and resorts to checkpoint-and-preempt only when the final viable group itself must yield. Quota borrowing across teams, developed in section 8.11, depends on this primitive whenever an owner reclaims capacity it lent. Scaling curves and transition costs determine whether the extra progress repays the disruption. Because batch changes can affect convergence, the test of a resize is time to target model quality, not throughput.

8.7.3 Elastic scaling policies

Adding resources to a running job does not always accelerate time-to-convergence. Communication and synchronization overhead limit throughput gains, requiring the scheduler to enforce elastic scaling policies based on empirical efficiency curves rather than resource availability alone. With fixed global work per step, a job’s scaling efficiency \(\ScalingEfficiency(\ScalingCurveWorkerCount)\) at \(\ScalingCurveWorkerCount\) workers (definition 6.4) typically passes through three regimes. In the near-linear regime, per-worker computation dwarfs every fixed per-step cost. In the sublinear regime, pipeline fill and drain and the unhidden part of gradient synchronization become a visible share of each step. In the saturation regime, those fixed costs rival the shrinking computation itself. Assigning available GPUs to a saturated job can yield less useful progress than serving queued work. The lower allocation bound answers a different question. Below its minimum viable scale, a job may lack the memory or complete parallel groups needed to execute at all. A policy can impose a higher minimum to meet a deadline, even when a smaller allocation remains computationally efficient.

Throughput falls away from ideal linear scaling and saturates.

The 175B reference job shows these regimes with the chapter’s own numbers. Modeled at a fixed global batch with the step-time decomposition of the Fleet Law (equation 1.3), its scaling efficiency falls from 87 percent at 256 GPUs to 83 percent at 1,024 GPUs and 69 percent at 4,096 GPUs (figure 12, panel a). Efficiency falls, yet quadrupling from 1,024 to 4,096 GPUs still raises throughput 3.3×, so a lower efficiency is a declining marginal return rather than a slowdown. Panel (b) locates the loss. Tensor-parallel communication shrinks along with each worker’s computation, so its share of the step never rises above 11 percent. The decline comes instead from pipeline fill and drain and unhidden gradient synchronization, which take roughly fixed time per step while each worker’s share of the batch shrinks. The pipeline bubble grows fastest of the three, for a reason the bubble fraction \((\PipelineParallelDegree-1)/(\MicrobatchCount+\PipelineParallelDegree-1)\) of Pipeline parallelism makes explicit. With the global batch fixed, each added replica takes a share of the sequences, so the microbatch count \(\MicrobatchCount\) per replica falls while the stage count stays at 8, and the idle fraction rises. The shaded window from 256 to 1,024 GPUs is therefore a policy choice, not an interval derived from these efficiencies alone. A minimum allocation could follow from a completion deadline or the job’s declared resource contract. It does not follow from insufficient per-worker computation at smaller scale, since with fixed global work fewer workers generally perform more work each. The upper limit likewise requires comparing additional useful progress with resize cost and the value of serving queued jobs. A real scheduler must obtain those inputs from profiling and the workload’s quality target.

This logic extends to the temporal dimension, where the scheduler must weigh the immediate progress of a smaller allocation against waiting for a larger one. If a 512-GPU job can start immediately with 128 GPUs or wait for the full request, the better choice depends on the integration of throughput over time. The resize overhead (the cost of stopping, checkpointing, and restarting when the runtime requires them) must be amortized over the subsequent run duration. A policy of “opportunistic scaling” that resizes a job every time a single node becomes free can lose its throughput advantage to repeated interruptions, completing less useful work than a stable allocation.

Napkin Math 8.3: The elastic scaling decision
The comparison of elastic and rigid scheduling above assumed linear scaling and free resizing; this decision drops both assumptions. Consider a training job with a target batch size that requires 512 GPUs for the highest throughput among the configurations considered. Three scheduling strategies are evaluated over a window of 24 hours.

Given: Throughput at 128 GPUs is 1 epoch/hour (baseline). Throughput at 256 GPUs is 1.8 epochs/hour (90 percent scaling efficiency relative to that baseline). Throughput at 512 GPUs is 3.2 epochs/hour (80 percent scaling efficiency). Re-scaling cost is 10 minutes (0.17 hours) to checkpoint, re-shard, and restart.

Strategy 1 (immediate start, no scaling): The job starts immediately with 128 GPUs and runs for the full 24 hours. Total work: \(24 \times 1.0 = 24\) epochs.

Strategy 2 (wait for the full allocation): The job waits in the queue for 4 hours until 512 GPUs become available, then runs for 20 hours. Total work: \(20 \times 3.2 = 64\) epochs.

Strategy 3 (elastic step-up): The job starts immediately with 128 GPUs. After 4 hours, the capacity that lets Strategy 2 start frees up, but the elastic policy caps growth at 256 GPUs so that the remaining GPUs stay available to queued jobs. The job pauses, scales up, and resumes. Phase 1: \(4 \times 1.0 = 4\) epochs. Phase 2: \((24 - 4 - 0.17) \times 1.8 \approx 35.7\) epochs. Total work: 4 + 35.7 = 39.7 epochs.

The elastic strategy completes nearly 1.7× the epoch-equivalent work of the immediate small-scale run, but still trails the full allocation by about 38 percent. That comparison counts this job’s epochs, not the GPU-hours spent on them. Strategy 2 holds twice as many GPUs as Strategy 3 for most of the window and runs them at 80 percent scaling efficiency against Strategy 3’s 90 percent. The elastic job therefore does more work per GPU-hour, and the GPUs it leaves free serve queued jobs. Which strategy wins therefore depends on whether the fleet values this job’s completion time or the total work completed across all jobs. That gap shrinks when queue waits grow or peak capacity stays scarce. Re-scaling overhead is only 10 minutes, so the step-up strategy keeps its lead over the immediate small-scale run unless slow checkpoint transfer or compilation stretches that overhead beyond about 9 hours.

Placement and elasticity determine which allocations are feasible and how much useful progress they produce across competing jobs. Fleet economics adds the price of those choices. Every placement, preemption, and reservation consumes capacity that could serve another workload, so the scheduler must compare both useful progress and opportunity cost.

Self-Check: Question

  1. When an elastic scheduler resizes a distributed training job from 64 GPUs to 128 GPUs, what trade-off exists between maintaining a constant global batch size versus scaling batch size proportionally with worker count?

    1. Constant global batch size halves the effective examples per worker per optimizer update, removing one source of optimization change without guaranteeing identical convergence; proportional batch scaling preserves that per-worker count but changes optimization and requires a validated learning-rate policy.
    2. Constant global batch size requires restarting the model weights from epoch zero, whereas proportional batch size allows continuous gradient descent without learning rate adjustment.
    3. Constant global batch size disables NVLink communication across workers, whereas proportional batch size enables ring AllReduce.
    4. Constant global batch size doubles the learning rate automatically via PyTorch hooks, whereas proportional batch size keeps the learning rate fixed to prevent divergence.

  2. An elastic job running on 64 GPUs at 100 steps/minute can scale to 128 GPUs, reaching 180 steps/minute. If resizing triggers a dynamic rendezvous and checkpoint-sync transition pause of 60 seconds (0 steps during pause), what is the break-even duration the job must run after the pause at 128 GPUs to recover the lost progress in total steps completed?

    1. At least 15 seconds.
    2. At least 30 seconds.
    3. At least 45 seconds.
    4. At least 75 seconds.

  3. What three parameters define the operational contract between an elastic scheduler and an elastic training framework (such as TorchElastic), and why is each necessary?

  4. True or False: Under a supported resize and checkpoint contract, an elastic scheduler can reclaim capacity by shrinking a training job to a smaller viable configuration without terminating the logical job, although worker processes may restart.

  5. A worker encounters an unhandled CUDA fault during a TorchElastic training run. Explain the dependencies that must hold before useful training resumes, and why recovery cannot require every surviving worker to finish a clean step boundary.
