8.8 Cost Optimization

An idle accelerator in an enterprise fleet incurs operational costs every hour whether computing or blocked in a queue, turning placement and preemption decisions into direct financial trade-offs. The orchestrator translates this utilization-to-dollars relationship from section 8.1 into an online scheduling signal, balancing resource prices, reservation commitments, interruption risk, checkpoint cost, and job deadlines to decide whether to admit, delay, preempt, or resize work. Capital planning, portfolio return, cost attribution, and lifecycle total cost of ownership are larger, slower decisions developed in Fleet Economics and Utilization; the scheduler consumes their outcomes as prices and constraints.

At a fixed utilization, idle capacity grows linearly with fleet size, so the dollar cost of a scheduling inefficiency grows with every procurement (figure 13). At 10,000 GPUs operating at 50 percent utilization, annual waste reaches $87.6M under the chapter’s $2/GPU-hour accounting scenario. Published measurements show that such underutilization occurs in production. Microsoft’s Philly cluster study (Jeon et al. 2019) measured mean GPU utilization of approximately 52 percent. The 30 to 70 percent shaded band in the figure is an illustrative operating range for the cost model, not a universal empirical claim about all production clusters. Moving from 50 percent to 80 percent utilization, the improvement priced in the chapter opening, recovers over $52M annually in equivalent capacity value. That is the same 3,000 GPU-equivalents the opening counted as waste at 30 percent idle, and it becomes cash savings only when it avoids billed usage or a planned purchase.

Recovering that idle capacity requires pricing each scheduling lever against the disruption it causes. Because reclaiming GPUs from running jobs is the most direct way to admit higher-value work, the cost of a single preemption and the cascades it can trigger come first. Discounted spot capacity then lowers the hourly price of restart-tolerant training, diversification and checkpoint policies keep correlated reclamations from erasing that discount, and capacity-reservation tiers combine with workload cost policies to route jobs across reserved, on-demand, and interruptible pools.

8.8.1 Preemption cost and cascades

Each eviction can evict again, so one admission reaches \(\PreemptionFanoutCount + \PreemptionFanoutCount^2\) jobs.

When a high-priority job enters a saturated cluster, the scheduler must decide which running workloads to terminate to free up resources. This decision is rarely isolated. In tightly packed clusters, evicting a medium-priority job to accommodate a high-priority request often triggers a preemption cascade, where the evicted job immediately attempts to reschedule itself by displacing lower-priority workloads. The disruption multiplies with each level. If the urgent job displaces \(\PreemptionFanoutCount\) medium-priority jobs and each of those, on rescheduling, displaces \(\PreemptionFanoutCount\) lower-priority jobs, one admission interrupts \(\PreemptionFanoutCount+ \PreemptionFanoutCount^2\) jobs after two levels. Every one of them pays the preemption overhead quantified below and adds a checkpoint reload to the storage system at the same moment. Two dampening controls cap that growth. A rule that evicted jobs requeue without preempting anyone themselves stops the chain at the first level, so one admission costs at most \(\PreemptionFanoutCount\) interruptions. A preemption budget limits how often any single job, or what fraction of capacity, may be displaced in a time window, which protects the jobs at the bottom of the priority order that every chain would otherwise reach.

The engineering cost of preemption extends beyond scheduling latency. The preemption overhead depends on the exit path. A successful graceful checkpoint adds the time to save and restart while preserving completed progress; a forced stop can instead discard work since the last durable checkpoint. Consider such a victim, a forced interruption of the 64-GPU fine-tuning run for our 175B parameter model. The scenario assumes lost work equivalent to 15 minutes of execution. The restart then repeats the cold-restart steps of the recovery budget in Recovery procedures, without its detection step because the scheduler itself stopped the job. These steps take 3 minutes to relaunch, 24.5 seconds to reload the 2.45 TB checkpoint with its optimizer state, and 2 minutes of warmup as data pipelines refill and just-in-time (JIT) compilation and runtime initialization complete. The calculation prices replay and restart across the same allocation; it does not also charge for a successful final save.

Reconstructing the distributed data pipeline also belongs in the preemption contract. The checkpoint must preserve the sampling and loader state needed to resume the intended data order, alongside model and optimizer state. If the restart path omits that state, it can repeat or skip samples and lose exact replay of the training trajectory. A different shuffle order does not by itself prove statistical corruption, but the recovery policy must specify which changes are acceptable. The scheduler should therefore measure restoration and pipeline warmup as part of recovery cost, rather than assume that reloading model weights alone makes the job productive.

The resulting 20.4-minute overhead combines discarded execution, its larger part, with relaunch, reload, and warmup time; it is not a measurement of continuous kernel activity after eviction. Spread over the run, it belongs to the Fleet Law’s coordination term \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) (equation 1.3), which counts scheduling decisions and failure recovery alongside barrier waits, so frequent preemption lengthens the average step exactly as frequent failure does. At $2/GPU-hour, the scenario values each event at $44 in allocation time. A low-priority run sits at the bottom of every cascade, so without a budget it can be struck many times a day. At the scenario’s 12 forced preemptions per day, it loses 4.1 hours of equivalent allocation time, or 261 GPU-hours for this 64-GPU run. A per-job budget bounds that loss directly. When a budget binds, the scheduler must consider waiting, another placement, or a defined emergency exception. The trade-off is reduced recovery churn against delayed admission, measured under the workload’s deadlines.

8.8.2 Spot instances and preemptible VMs

Renting on-demand cloud GPUs for a multi-week training run protects the job from provider reclamation, but pays full price even when spare GPUs sit idle in the provider’s data center. Renting interruptible capacity cuts the hourly rate by 60 to 90 percent, but lets the provider reclaim those GPUs on best-effort notice. Cloud providers sell this spare capacity through spot instances10 (AWS) or preemptible VMs (GCP), where actual discounts depend on the resource and market. The reference interruption windows range from up to 30 seconds for GCP preemptible shutdown to 2 minutes for AWS stop or termination notices, with best-effort delivery rather than a guaranteed recovery interval. At the scenario rates of $0.70/GPU-hour instead of $2/GPU-hour, the worked comparison below shows how savings can exceed half the on-demand cost after accounting for its assumed interruption overhead.

10 Spot instances: The scenario’s 60 to 90 percent discount purchases reclaimable capacity, not a guaranteed interval for saving state. AWS’s best-effort stop or termination notice targets 2 minutes, while GCP preemptible shutdown allows up to 30 seconds; AWS hibernation begins immediately. If a job’s measured checkpoint time exceeds the reference 30-second window, a notice-triggered save cannot be its only recovery path.

The viability of spot instances for ML training depends on two factors that determine whether the discount translates into actual savings. The first is the cost of each interruption (checkpoint overhead plus restart time plus lost work since the last checkpoint). The second is the frequency of interruption (which varies by instance type, region, time of day, and overall cloud demand). Equation 3 gives the effective cost of spot training:

\[ \EffectiveSpotTrainingCost= \SpotHourlyPrice\times \frac{\BilledAllocationTime}{\RetainedProductiveTime} \tag{3}\]

where \(\BilledAllocationTime\) is billed allocation time, including checkpointing, recovery, and work later discarded after an interruption, and \(\RetainedProductiveTime\) is the equivalent training time represented by retained progress. The ratio assumes a fixed allocation size and price, with unchanged useful throughput while training; variable allocations require summing the cost of each allocation interval. Waiting for replacement capacity extends completion time but contributes to billed time only while resources remain allocated. When checkpointing and interruption losses consume a small fraction of billed time, effective cost stays near the spot price. Frequent interruptions, expensive recovery, or substantial replay can instead erase the discount.

Spot savings and fault tolerance infrastructure are coupled. Fault tolerance mechanisms (frequent asynchronous checkpointing, fast checkpoint loading, elastic recovery) make spot capacity usable by lowering the cost of each interruption. Elastic training can resume a logical job with fewer workers after reclamation, provided the remaining allocation is viable, although the framework may still restart worker processes and restore checkpointed state, so elasticity reduces recovery cost rather than eliminating it. The same infrastructure therefore earns a double return. It protects against hardware losses, and it enables the deliberate use of interruptible capacity. Both benefits require measurement. The scheduler combines observed interruption rates, recovery costs, and retained training progress with resource prices to estimate whether spot savings repay the additional recovery infrastructure. The same evidence supports valuing protection against hardware failures; neither benefit follows from the discount or checkpoint mechanism alone.

8.8.3 Spot instance strategies for ML training

Spot capacity pays off only when an interruption rarely removes the whole job at once, so schedulers that use it diversify where capacity comes from instead of simply retrying on failure. Availability zone diversification reduces the probability of simultaneous fleet-wide reclamation. Cloud providers typically manage spot pools independently per availability zone (AZ). If a training job requires 1,024 GPUs, allocating them as a single block in one zone exposes the job to a complete stop if that specific zone’s spot pool is reclaimed. Diversifying independent jobs or restart destinations across zones reduces exposure to one exhausted pool. Splitting a single synchronous gang across zones also changes its communication paths, so the scheduler must include cross-zone bandwidth, latency, and transfer costs before accepting that placement. The scheduler still must model correlation carefully: independent per-instance interruptions make large simultaneous losses unlikely, but pool-level or AZ-level reclamations can remove hundreds of GPUs at once and must be treated as elastic-shrink or failover events rather than ordinary isolated failures.

Instance type diversification expands the set of capacity pools a job can use, though availability across those pools can remain correlated. A job that requests only one instance type cannot use spare capacity in another pool. Accepting alternatives therefore requires a compatibility list that includes memory capacity, runtime support, communication paths, and measured throughput. An instance with more memory can still be an unsuitable substitute if its runtime support, communication paths, or throughput differ. The scheduler compares each feasible alternative’s price and recovery cost per unit of retained progress; mixing devices within a synchronous gang also requires accounting for unequal worker speeds.

Spot capacity signals can guide placement but do not necessarily predict interruption frequency. For example, the AWS Spot Placement Score estimates the current likelihood that a capacity request will succeed in a region or availability zone; it explicitly does not predict interruption risk. A scheduler can use this signal to choose where to request capacity, but estimating interruptions per day requires separate historical measurements for the instance type and region. If the resulting workload-specific recovery model shows that interruption overhead erases the discount, the scheduler should migrate the job to on-demand capacity or a different region.

Checkpoint frequency optimization for spot instances extends the reliability logic. The Young-Daly formula developed in The Young-Daly checkpoint interval models losses from unpredicted interruptions, whether caused by hardware failure or reclamation without a usable warning. Provider notices offer an additional opportunity to save state, using the reference windows of 2 minutes on AWS and up to 30 seconds on GCP preemptible VMs. A two-tier checkpointing approach combines periodic checkpoints, with an interval chosen for the workload’s interruption model, and a best-effort checkpoint triggered by a notice. The notice-triggered checkpoint reduces lost work only if it becomes durable before reclamation; otherwise, the job restores its last durable state, whether produced by a periodic, asynchronous, or incremental checkpoint, and replays subsequent work. That contingency planning matters more when spot interruptions are correlated across a pool rather than isolated to single machines.

Systems Perspective 8.3: Correlated spot risk
Context: Spot capacity is cheap because it is reclaimable. That risk is not independent across instances: cloud regions, instance types, product launches, and deadline cycles can create correlated demand.

Failure mode: A fleet that concentrates all interruptible training in one region and one instance type can lose a large fraction of workers at once, even if each individual worker appears restartable.

Consequence: Checkpointing handles isolated interruptions; it does not solve fleet-wide preemption when every retry targets the same exhausted pool.

Systems insight: A robust scheduler diversifies compatible restart destinations, monitors interruption risk, and plans an on-demand fallback when spot capacity cannot meet the job’s deadline or cost target. That fallback still depends on available capacity and the time needed to restore state.

8.8.4 Capacity reservation strategies

Committing to long-term reserved capacity for 100 percent of peak demand leaves expensive GPUs idle whenever traffic dips, while relying solely on spot or on-demand pools risks capacity shortages during product launches and sudden reclamations during deadline crunches. To avoid paying for idle reservations or starving urgent workloads, organizations that rely on cloud infrastructure (or that price on-premises capacity in economic terms) combine three tiers of compute capacity. Each tier trades cost and commitment against availability and interruption risk (table 4).

The constructed portfolio in figure 14 divides capacity into a reserved tier covering the steady 60 percent baseline, an on-demand band between 60 and 80 percent, and an interruptible spot tier serving peaks up to 92 percent. An organization with 60 percent steady-state utilization and 40 percent burst demand might reserve capacity for 60 percent of peak need, use on-demand for bursts whose deadlines tolerate admission and startup delay, and use spot for restart-tolerant work. These shares illustrate a policy rather than establish an optimal portfolio. The scheduler must match each workload’s deadline and recovery contract to the capacity tier. Reserved capacity can support the steady serving load or a training job’s base allocation, while spot can support elastic replicas or independent experiments that tolerate reclamation. A peak in urgent demand cannot automatically be assigned to spot merely because it lies above the reserved band.

Systems Perspective 8.4: Spot vs. on-demand training
Consider training a 70B parameter model, a smaller workload than the chapter’s 175B reference, on 512 GPUs for 14 days. The scenario fixes the event budget at 196 interruptions over the run and treats each as graceful, so no progress is replayed.

On-Demand: 512 GPUs \(\times\) 336 hours \(\times\) $2/GPU-hour = $344,064

Spot (65 percent discount, 5 percent checkpoint overhead, 14 interruptions per day of useful training with 30 minutes restart):

  • Base cost: 512 GPUs \(\times\) 336 hours \(\times\) $0.70/GPU-hour = $120,422
  • Checkpoint overhead: \(5\%\) \(\times\) 336 hours = 16.8 hours
  • Restart overhead: 196 interruptions \(\times\) 0.5 hours = 98 hours
  • Total time: 336 hours + 16.8 hours + 98 hours = 450.8 hours
  • Total cost: 512 GPUs \(\times\) 450.8 hours \(\times\) $0.70/GPU-hour = $161,567

Savings: $182,497 (53.0 percent), at the cost of approximately 34.2 percent longer wall-clock time.

These savings assume fault tolerance infrastructure is already in place. Without checkpointing, a single interruption forces restart from scratch, potentially wasting days of compute and negating all savings.

8.8.5 Cost-aware scheduling policies

Once a fleet spans reserved, on-demand, and spot tiers, a newly submitted job can often launch immediately on reserved A100 GPUs or wait two hours for spot H100 instances that deliver twice the throughput at half the execution cost. Deciding whether those savings repay the two-hour delay and interruption risk turns the purchasing tiers into an online scheduling decision. Alongside completion time, utilization, and fairness, a cost-aware orchestrator evaluates three recurring trade-offs: waiting versus running immediately, preempting lower-priority work on reserved capacity (such as a hyperparameter sweep) versus routing a high-priority training run to more expensive on-demand instances, and replacing reclaimed spot instances with on-demand nodes versus shrinking elastically. Each choice requires the costs of waiting, recovery, and displaced work as well as the price of the new allocation.

These decisions require the scheduler to model both the time-value of computation (the organizational cost of a 2-hour delay in researcher productivity, product launch schedules, or competitive position) and the financial cost of different resource allocations. Delaying a production model retrain by 2 hours might violate a service level agreement (SLA), while the same delay to an exploratory experiment can postpone a research decision or block dependent work. A workload class’s delay tolerance is therefore a policy input grounded in its dependencies and deadlines, not an assumption that exploratory work has no time cost.

Production systems can define cost policies per workload class that encode these trade-offs as configuration rather than requiring per-decision human judgment. One policy places steady inference demand on reserved capacity and maintains ready replicas to meet its latency target; the reservation alone does not guarantee application availability or latency. Large training runs can combine a reserved minimum viable allocation with interruptible elastic expansion, requesting on-demand replacements when a deadline justifies the cost and capacity is available. Exploratory workloads with flexible deadlines can use spot capacity while accepting interruptions and variable completion times. These assignments express the organization’s priorities rather than a universal mapping from workload type to purchasing tier.

The scheduler does not implement the organization’s financial accounting system. It consumes the resulting price and policy signals: the marginal price of each capacity tier, the reservation a project may claim, the interruption risk it may accept, and the deadline it must meet. Showback, chargeback, and attribution belong to that accounting system; the scheduler uses their outputs only to rank feasible scheduling actions. Lifecycle total cost of ownership (TCO) reaches the scheduler the same way, as per-resource opportunity costs and constraints that record whether capacity is owned, reserved, on demand, or interruptible, rather than as a scheduling mechanism.

↪ Tag-based cost attribution, shared-cost allocation, and chargeback are developed in Cost visibility and attribution.

↪ The lifecycle TCO framework, which combines training, inference, data, and iteration costs, is developed in ML systems TCO framework.

Checkpoint 8.2: Cost-aware scheduling trade-offs

Before moving to ML-specific schedulers, review how the cost-optimization mechanisms interact:

Spot pricing and reservation strategy reduce the cost of capacity, but they do not determine which competing job should receive it. Runtime uncertainty, unequal completion delays, and the cost of interrupting an iteration remain even when prices are known. Resolving them requires signals from the workload itself, such as how much service a job has consumed, where its iteration boundaries fall, or how fast it is learning. A scheduler that tracks learning progress can optimize time-to-accuracy, the wall-clock time until a run reaches a target validation metric, rather than execution speed alone.

Self-Check: Question

  1. What is a preemption cascade in a shared GPU cluster, and which control stops it at the first level?

    1. A cascade is a burst of hardware failures in one rack; spare-capacity reservation stops it.
    2. A cascade is a high-priority admission that evicts medium-priority jobs, each of which then evicts lower-priority jobs to reschedule itself; requiring evicted jobs to requeue without preempting anyone stops it after the first level.
    3. A cascade is a queue of jobs waiting behind a large gang; backfill scheduling stops it.
    4. A cascade is repeated checkpointing by one job; lengthening its checkpoint interval stops it.

  2. A training job moves from on-demand to spot capacity at the chapter’s scenario discount. According to the effective-cost relation, in which effective cost equals the spot price times billed allocation time divided by retained productive time, what determines whether the discount survives?

    1. Only the advertised discount, because interruptions are free once a provider sends a notice.
    2. The ratio of billed allocation time to retained productive time, which grows with checkpoint overhead, restart time, and work replayed after each interruption.
    3. The number of GPUs in the job, because larger jobs always receive deeper spot discounts.
    4. The provider’s capacity-placement score, which predicts how many interruptions the job will see per day.

  3. An organization runs steady 24/7 inference, urgent retraining with tight deadlines, and a large hyperparameter sweep. Which assignment of these workloads to reserved, on-demand, and spot capacity matches each workload’s deadline and recovery contract?

    1. Inference on spot, retraining on reserved, sweep on on-demand.
    2. Inference on reserved, retraining on on-demand, sweep on spot.
    3. All three on spot, because the discount outweighs any interruption risk.
    4. Inference on on-demand, retraining on spot, sweep on reserved.

  4. This chapter treats lifecycle total cost of ownership (TCO) as an input to the scheduler rather than as a scheduling mechanism. What does the scheduler consume from TCO analysis and cost accounting, and how does it use those inputs?

  5. A low-priority 64-GPU fine-tuning run is forcibly preempted. What makes up the preemption overhead for that event, and why does a per-job preemption budget matter more for this run than for a high-priority job?

  6. True or False: Because each VM instance in a cloud provider has an independent lifecycle, placing worker nodes of a 64-node distributed training job across different instance types within the same Availability Zone guarantees that preemption events will remain uncorrelated and statistically independent.

8.9 Custom ML Schedulers

A short experiment waiting behind a long training run can suffer even when every running job has an efficient placement. Improving that outcome requires a policy for deciding which job receives the next available resources. Custom ML schedulers use different signals to make that decision: consumed service can prioritize jobs without runtime estimates, iteration boundaries can make preemption cheaper, normalized finish time can expose unfair delay, and measured goodput can guide joint resource and batch-size changes. Only the last of these requires a model of learning progress; the others improve completion time, utilization, or fairness without predicting convergence.

8.9.1 Duration-agnostic scheduling (Tiresias)

When a researcher submits an exploratory training run without knowing whether it will fail in ten minutes or run for a week, scheduling policies that depend on upfront duration estimates (section 8.4.3) either mispack the queue or penalize inaccurate guesses. Tiresias (Gu et al. 2019) eliminates the requirement for duration estimates entirely. It uses a two-dimensional attained service11 scheduler that makes priority decisions based on what a job has already consumed, not what it claims it will consume. Jobs accumulate “service” based on GPU-time consumed, with priority decreasing as service increases. The two dimensions are time (how long the job has been running) and resources (how many GPUs the job uses), capturing both elapsed duration and resource intensity.

11 Attained service scheduling: A discipline that uses consumed service to set priority when remaining work is unknown. Unlike shortest remaining processing time (SRPT), it cannot distinguish a newly arrived long job from a short one before either receives service. Its benefit depends on the workload distribution; protection against starvation remains necessary when new jobs repeatedly displace older ones.

A discretized version groups jobs into service bins (illustratively, less than 1 GPU-hour, 1 to 10 GPU-hours, 10 to 100 GPU-hours, greater than 100 GPU-hours), giving lower-service bins higher priority. When job durations are heavy-tailed, a job that has consumed little service is likely to be short, so favoring low-service jobs approximates shortest-remaining-processing-time ordering without any runtime estimate. New jobs can receive service before long-running jobs, but their eventual duration remains unknown, and a high-priority gang still needs a feasible allocation. Tiresias also promotes jobs that have waited too long, trading some average-completion-time benefit for protection against starvation. The priority rule therefore replaces runtime prediction with an observable signal and an explicit waiting-time policy; it does not guarantee that every short job starts promptly.

The Tiresias evaluation reports lower average job completion time (JCT) on its testbed and trace-driven workloads (Gu et al. 2019). The result depends on job arrivals, sizes, and the comparison policy. Prioritizing low-service jobs can delay long-running jobs, so average completion time must be evaluated alongside starvation prevention and the delay experienced by each job class.

Gu, J., M. Chowdhury, K. G. Shin, Y. Zhu, M. Jeon, J. Qian, H. H. Liu, and C. Guo. 2019. “Tiresias: A GPU Cluster Manager for Distributed Deep Learning.” Proceedings of the 16th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI ’19), 485–500.

8.9.2 Iteration-aware scheduling (Gandiva)

Preempting a job in the middle of a training step either discards the partial step or requires saving gigabytes of temporary forward-pass activations alongside the model weights. Gandiva (Xiao et al. 2018) avoids that mid-step state transfer by exploiting repetitive training iterations to profile jobs and time-slice GPUs at iteration boundaries. To give a GPU to another job, Gandiva suspends the running job at the end of a minibatch, copies its live GPU memory to host memory, and resumes another job on the device; switching back reverses the copy. Several jobs can therefore take turns on one GPU, and the same suspend-and-resume path lets Gandiva migrate a job to better-placed devices or pack compatible jobs onto one GPU when their combined memory fits. The boundary is the cheap place to switch because temporary activations have been released, so far less state must move than in the middle of a step. Weights, optimizer state, and runtime allocations remain, so each switch still costs a transfer proportional to that persistent state, and a job whose persistent state nearly fills the device cannot share it. The scheduler must measure memory demand, interference, and transition cost before admitting additional jobs.

Xiao, Wencong, Romil Bhardwaj, Ramachandran Ramjee, Muthian Sivathanu, Nipun Kwatra, Zhenhua Han, Prabal Patel, et al. 2018. “Gandiva: Introspective Cluster Scheduling for Deep Learning.” 13th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 18), 595–610.

Gandiva also implements grow-shrink elasticity, applying the allocation mechanism of section 8.7 to jobs that explicitly permit resizing. It expands eligible jobs into idle capacity and shrinks them when demand returns, using profiles to estimate which jobs benefit. The published implementation limits growth to one server and waits through an idle period before expanding. Its contribution is the connection between measured job behavior and resource control, rather than a claim that arbitrary training jobs can resize cheaply.

8.9.3 Finish-time fairness (Themis)

Equal GPU-time shares can produce unequal completion delays when workloads respond differently to placement. Themis (Mahajan et al. 2020) measures fairness in normalized finish time. Each application, which can contain related training jobs, compares its completion time in the shared cluster with its completion time on an isolated \(1/\ContendingApplicationCount\) share of the cluster, where \(\ContendingApplicationCount\) is the number of applications contending for it. The ratio has the same form as the slowdown \(\JobSlowdown_j\) defined with the scheduling objectives, except in its denominator. Themis divides by the completion time on a fair share of the cluster rather than by the isolated runtime on the job’s own allocation, so a ratio of one means the application finished no later than its fair share would have allowed.

Mahajan, Kshiteej, Arjun Balasubramanian, Arjun Singhvi, Shivaram Venkataraman, Aditya Akella, Amar Phanishayee, and Shuchi Chawla. 2020. “Themis: Fair and Efficient GPU Cluster Scheduling.” 17th USENIX Symposium on Networked Systems Design and Implementation (NSDI 20), 289–304.

Themis targets the worst finish-time fairness ratio across applications. Its auction rounds offer available resources to the applications farthest from fair completion, while allowing placement efficiency to influence which specific GPUs each one receives within a round. With two applications whose current estimates are 1.2 and 2.0, the second is projected to finish twice as late as its fair share would allow, so it enters the round ahead of the first. The first application’s ratio rises while it waits, and once it overtakes the second it moves to the front of later rounds. The signal is relative completion delay, not proximity to completion or GPU-hours already consumed.

Within a round, contending applications bid on candidate GPU subsets by reporting the finish-time fairness ratio they would reach on each subset. Because a communication-heavy job finishes much faster on a compact subset than on a scattered one while a communication-light job sees little difference, these bids expose placement sensitivity rather than monetary willingness to pay. The arbiter can then assign compact subsets to the lagging applications whose finish-time ratios gain the most from locality. This auction trades short-term allocation efficiency against the long-term fairness objective, making the quality of completion-time and placement estimates part of the scheduler’s operating assumptions.

8.9.4 Adaptive resource allocation (Pollux)

Adding GPUs to a running job by increasing its global batch size raises throughput in examples per second. Past the critical batch size, however, each example contributes less optimization progress, so the extra GPUs can slow convergence rather than speed it up. Pollux (Qiao et al. 2021) addresses this coupling by extending resource adaptation to training hyperparameters. Where Gandiva changes only device allocations, Pollux jointly considers allocation, batch size, and learning-rate scaling because a larger batch can use more devices efficiently while changing the amount of work needed to converge. The preferred configuration can therefore change with both cluster demand and the stage of training.

Pollux uses statistical goodput to connect system throughput, measured in training examples per second, with statistical efficiency, which measures progress per example relative to a reference batch size. It is a third use of the word, and One word, several measures sets it beside the others. The run-level goodput of The Fleet Law discounts work lost to failures and restarts, and Google’s ML productivity goodput multiplies program, runtime, and scheduling factors. Statistical goodput instead discounts each example by the optimization progress it contributes at the current batch size. Statistical efficiency falls as the batch grows past the critical batch size of Critical batch size and diminishing returns, which is why more GPUs at a larger batch can raise throughput while lowering goodput. Pollux estimates how allocation and batch changes affect both quantities, then uses those estimates to guide resource sharing across jobs. Additional GPUs are valuable only when their throughput gain survives the corresponding change in statistical efficiency and the cost of changing the allocation.

The Pollux evaluation reports average job completion times 37 to 50 percent lower than the state-of-the-art deep learning schedulers it was compared against, which do not make the same combined adjustment of resources and batch size (Qiao et al. 2021). The number belongs to that testbed and trace. Its durable lesson is the coupling: an allocation can increase samples per second while reducing progress per sample. The scheduler must estimate both effects and validate completion time at the same target quality, rather than transferring a reported improvement to a different workload.

Qiao, Aurick, Sang Keun Choe, Suhas Jayaram Subramanya, Willie Neiswanger, Qirong Ho, Hao Zhang, Gregory R. Ganger, and Eric P. Xing. 2021. “Pollux: Co-Adaptive Cluster Scheduling for Goodput-Optimized Deep Learning.” 15th USENIX Symposium on Operating Systems Design and Implementation (OSDI 21), 1–18.

8.9.5 Scheduler comparison framework

Each research scheduler pursues a different objective, and each objective requires its own observations and controls (table 5). Consumed service can be obtained from resource accounting, while iteration timing and learning-progress estimates require closer runtime integration. The choice therefore turns on what the platform can observe and control, not on a ranking of scheduler sophistication.

The common lesson is to match the policy signal to the scheduling objective. Attained service addresses runtime uncertainty and can work from accounting records. Iteration boundaries lower the cost of interrupting work but need reliable timing profiles. Normalized finish time measures unequal delay, and goodput couples resource allocation to learning progress, which requires the training loop to accept allocation and batch-size changes. Each added control creates a failure mode of its own: inaccurate profiles can increase interference, and an inaccurate learning-progress model can choose allocations that fail to improve convergence. A platform should therefore test the chosen policy against queue delay, starvation risk, and measured recovery cost before adopting it.

8.9.6 From research to production

A production cluster containing V100s alongside A100s and H100s, with both InfiniBand and Ethernet paths, gives the same GPU count very different performance across placements, while live jobs crash, hang, or exceed declared memory needs. Because a research scheduler’s evaluation establishes results on a specific testbed, workload trace, and set of runtime controls, a production fleet must test whether its own hardware and failure conditions preserve those gains. Deploying a policy therefore requires integrating its decisions with admission, failure recovery, and the existing orchestrator, then measuring its benefit under the fleet’s actual workload.

Consider applying adaptive allocation to our 175B-parameter model training run. The runtime must first expose viable resize configurations and their costs. A checkpoint-and-restart path can require persisting and restoring large optimizer state, while other state-transfer mechanisms impose different costs. Repeated resizing can consume the expected throughput gain before the job benefits from its new allocation. The policy should therefore compare predicted additional progress over the expected allocation duration with the measured transition cost, retaining a stable placement when resizing does not repay that cost. The decision follows the workload and cluster demand, not a universal training phase.

The production decision is whether a specialized policy improves marginal allocation enough to justify its operational burden, judged by the measures this chapter has already built: queue delay and slowdown, useful GPU-hours, preemption overhead, and placement efficiency. Group admission settles whether a gang can start at all; the research schedulers answer the next question, which feasible group to admit, and each imposes different requirements on the runtime. Selecting a policy therefore means matching its objective and assumptions to the fleet rather than choosing a single scheduler as universally best.

Checkpoint 8.3: Custom scheduler design space

Consider the trade-offs between the four research schedulers:

The training policies just compared balance completion time, fairness, utilization, and learning progress. Serving adds a different deadline: capacity must be ready before an arriving request exhausts its latency budget. That requirement makes provisioning delay and burst headroom central to how the fleet allocates capacity to serving.

Self-Check: Question

  1. How does the Tiresias scheduler solve the problem of inaccurate and inflated user runtime estimates in GPU cluster scheduling?

    1. It uses two-dimensional least attained service based on observed spatial (GPU count) and temporal (elapsed execution time) resource attainment, prioritizing jobs that have consumed less cumulative compute without asking users for duration estimates.
    2. It requires users to pay monetary deposits that are forfeited if their job runtime deviates by more than \(10\%\) from their initial estimate.
    3. It parses Python ASTs at submission time to statically calculate the exact loop iteration count and FLOP requirements of every training script.
    4. It forces all jobs to run for exactly 60 minutes before automatically killing and requeueing them.

  2. In the Pollux scheduling architecture, how is the Goodput metric (\(G_j\)) defined to co-optimize cluster resource allocation and job-level training dynamics?

    1. Goodput is the total number of raw GPU clock cycles executed divided by the cluster’s electricity consumption in kilowatt-hours.
    2. Goodput is the ratio of network packets transmitted over InfiniBand to the number of retransmissions caused by packet drops.
    3. Goodput is training throughput in examples per second multiplied by statistical efficiency, the progress per example relative to a reference batch size.
    4. Goodput is the percentage of time GPUs spend running CUDA kernels compared to CPU data loading time.

  3. What mechanisms does Gandiva use to achieve fine-grained, low-overhead time-slicing and job migration across GPUs?

  4. The custom ML scheduler Themis optimizes for ____ fairness, ensuring that each job completes in a time comparable to what it would achieve in a dedicated cluster share, thereby preventing gaming and multi-resource allocation monopolies.

  5. 8.10 Serving Capacity in the Shared Fleet

When an e-commerce platform launches a major sale, requests can arrive faster than its serving replicas can process them. A training job may tolerate waiting in the admission queue; a serving request misses its deadline while additional capacity is still starting. To the fleet orchestrator, serving is therefore a claim on accelerators that must be honored quickly and that competes with training gangs for the same devices.

The orchestrator’s unit is the serving replica, a ready execution group with a loaded model that may occupy one accelerator, a device partition, or a multi-accelerator gang. A 175B-parameter model may make an eight-GPU tensor-parallel group the schedulable replica. Archetype B (DLRM at Scale) imposes the same constraint through its embedding tables: a replica whose tables are sharded across several accelerators can serve only when every shard is placed. Each replica has a measured request-processing capacity at a specified workload mix and latency target, and that measured capacity is the quantity the orchestrator uses to size and place replicas. It decides how many such groups exist, where they reside, how they are protected from interference, and which lower-priority allocations yield when serving demand grows. Language models add a memory dimension to the claim. Each in-progress response retains per-token attention state, the key-value (KV) cache, whose footprint grows with context length and concurrency even though the weights stay fixed, so a replica can exhaust its memory before it exhausts its compute.

How many replicas to run at each moment is a control problem with its own signals and delays. Accelerator utilization alone does not identify the capacity needed to meet a request-latency target, and loading large models can delay a new replica’s readiness. The controller must combine demand signals with measured startup time and ready reserve capacity; Autoscaling and Capacity Control develops that control loop. For the orchestrator, the immediate consequence is that reclaiming a device from training does not instantly make it available for serving. Three fleet-level questions follow, namely how serving workloads share devices without unacceptable interference, when sharing pays for itself, and where the boundary between training and serving pools should sit.

8.10.1 Resource isolation

When multiple inference workloads share physical hardware, noisy-neighbor contention12 arises whenever one workload’s resource consumption degrades another’s execution speed. On GPUs, shared execution units can limit concurrent kernels, while saturated HBM interfaces constrain memory-bound work. Contending working sets can evict lines from the shared L2 cache, and simultaneous host-to-device transfers can compete for PCIe bandwidth. Aggregate power and temperature limits can also reduce operating clocks. These mechanisms connect the placement decision to the request’s critical path. A placement that fits every model in memory can still miss its service-level objective if sharing extends execution or queueing enough to exhaust the latency budget.

12 Noisy neighbor: In GPU clusters, interference arises when one job’s bursty network traffic or memory bus utilization slows down a co-located job on the same node. In synchronous training, a delayed flow can extend collective completion when it lies on the communication critical path, propagating one tenant’s interference across a worker group.

13 Multi-Instance GPU (MIG): Introduced with the A100 (2020), MIG partitions a single GPU into up to 7 hardware-isolated instances with dedicated memory controllers, L2 cache slices, and compute units (NVIDIA Corporation 2020). The isolation is enforced at the hardware level, not by time-slicing, reducing noisy-neighbor interference relative to software-only sharing. Changing the partition layout requires stopping affected GPU workloads; the scheduler must include that disruption when adapting profiles to a changing workload mix.

NVIDIA Corporation. 2020. NVIDIA A100 Tensor Core GPU Architecture. NVIDIA Whitepaper, V1.0.

Multi-Instance GPU (MIG)13 closes several of these channels in hardware by partitioning A100 (80 GB) and H100 (80 GB) GPUs into hardware-isolated instances. Unlike software-based GPU sharing, such as time-slicing that context-switches several processes over one whole device, MIG dedicates memory, cache, and compute resources to each instance, reducing interference through those partitioned resources. The A100 MIG profiles in table 6 show the scheduling consequence. A single physical A100 becomes several fixed-size resources that the device plugin of section 8.5.2 can expose as independently schedulable accelerators.

MIG therefore sits at the strong resource-isolation end of this design space. The operational implication is that hardware partitioning reduces cross-workload interference but turns partition choice into a placement constraint. MIG is available on supported MIG-capable NVIDIA data-center GPUs, profiles may require draining affected workloads before reconfiguration, and fixed partition sizes may not match workload requirements. The memory-fit and sharing examples use the displayed device and profile capacities as conservative decimal-GB admission budgets, rather than as conversions of their modeled byte capacities. A workload that needs 15 GB of GPU memory leaves 5 GB of a 20 GB MIG instance budget unused or exceeds a 10 GB instance budget.

Software sharing offers different controls over execution, memory, and fault isolation; these do not form a single performance-versus-security scale. At the simplest level, CUDA time-slicing allows multiple processes to share a GPU by context-switching the compute resources. While flexible, it offers weak performance isolation because contexts compete for the entire device over time. NVIDIA’s Multi-Process Service (MPS)14 allows kernels and memory copies from different processes to overlap, reducing context-switch overhead and improving utilization when one process cannot fill the device (NVIDIA 2026). In the shared-resource MPS configuration considered here, Volta and newer clients have distinct GPU virtual address spaces but compete for execution and memory-system resources. Additional partitioning controls depend on the hardware, software version, and selected mode, and those configured limits, not address-space separation, determine how much performance and fault isolation MPS provides.

14 CUDA MPS (Multi-Process Service): NVIDIA’s runtime supports concurrent CUDA clients, with distinct virtual address spaces on Volta and newer GPUs. Its isolation contract depends on configured resource limits and partitioning support; the shared-resource mode used in this example does not reserve an independent hardware slice for each client. A deployment must also account for its fault domain, since address-space separation alone does not contain every GPU fault (NVIDIA 2026).

NVIDIA. 2026. NVIDIA CUDA Multi-Process Service.

GPU memory accounting must still prevent one model from consuming capacity reserved for another. A distinct virtual address space blocks direct addressing of another client’s allocations, but it does not create a fixed memory-capacity partition. Without enforceable per-client limits, a memory leak or unexpectedly large batch can exhaust shared device memory and disrupt colocated workloads. MPS therefore fits mutually trusting workloads whose aggregate memory footprint and interference have been measured; MIG or full-device assignment fits stronger trust and predictability requirements. At the host level, CPU core pinning and NUMA-aware placement complement these device controls by keeping inference processes off contended cores and away from remote memory, which shortens tail latency whenever those host paths lie on the request’s critical path.

The GPU mechanisms form a spectrum from flexible software sharing (time-slicing and MPS) through hardware partitioning (MIG) to exclusive assignment, and each point gives up packing efficiency for isolation (table 7). For serving, the choice turns on interference and memory accounting; when colocated workloads belong to different teams, it also becomes a trust-boundary decision, which section 8.11.3 takes up with the rest of multi-tenant security.

8.10.2 GPU sharing economics

The decision to co-locate multiple models on a single GPU trades potential capacity savings against interference. Choosing exclusive access avoids competition from another device tenant but can leave capacity unused when one workload cannot fill the device. Production evidence suggests such stranded capacity is common. On NERSC’s Perlmutter supercomputer, Li et al. (2023) found that 50 percent of jobs used less than 25 percent of their allocated GPU memory. Moving to aggressive sharing (via MPS or time-slicing) can admit more work, provided contention does not erase the gain or violate latency objectives. Exclusive device assignment still depends on the surrounding host, driver, identity, network, and storage controls for tenant security. The scheduler must therefore evaluate memory fit and measured service capacity together.

Li, Jie, George Michelogiannakis, Brandon Cook, Dulanya Cooray, and Yong Chen. 2023. “Analyzing Resource Utilization in an HPC System: A Case Study of NERSC’s Perlmutter.” Lect. Notes Comput. Sci., Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 13948: 297–316. https://doi.org/10.1007/978-3-031-32041-5_16.

Sharing packs two tenants, cutting the unused memory budget from 67.5 percent to 35 percent.

Using the same conservative admission budget, consider an A100 GPU with a 80 GB memory budget serving a 7B parameter model. The model weights in FP16 consume approximately 14 GB. With the scenario’s assumed KV cache and activation overhead, the total runtime footprint is roughly 26 GB. Exclusive access leaves 54 GB (67.5 percent) of the memory budget unused at this load. That budget is reclaimable only to the extent that it exceeds required runtime and burst headroom. A MIG partition (for example, a 3g.40gb profile) leaves roughly 35 percent of the assigned partition budget unused; the remaining device capacity is subject to valid partition combinations. Packing two such models onto the same 80 GB device through MPS consumes 52 GB, leaving 35 percent of the device memory budget unused under the same footprint assumption. Reducing the device count also requires enough compute and bandwidth headroom to preserve throughput and latency under concurrent demand; fitting the models in memory is only the first admission condition.

This density introduces interference. When two workloads share execution and memory resources through MPS, their demands can overlap on SMs and memory bandwidth. A compute-bound model paired with a memory-bound model is a candidate for complementary placement because, in isolation, each saturates a different resource, but the bottleneck labels describe isolated behavior, not a guarantee of compatibility. Both workloads still use multiple resources, and concurrent demand can change the limiting resource. The scheduler must measure the pair’s throughput and tail latency across its expected load range before treating memory density as usable capacity.

For a heterogeneous fleet, this logic dictates a bifurcated strategy. Our 175B parameter model requires about 350 GB for FP16 weights alone; with the 80 GB per-device admission budget that means at least 5 GPUs before KV cache, activation buffers, runtime workspace, and tensor-parallel granularity are included. In practice, an 8-GPU tensor-parallel replica is the cleaner scheduling unit and is a candidate for exclusive access. Conversely, the dozens of 1B to 7B parameter support models, such as toxicity classifiers, query embedding encoders, and auxiliary generation models, are candidates for sharing. Packing these smaller models onto shared utility nodes frees the high-performance nodes for foundation-model replicas.

Napkin Math 8.4: GPU sharing ROI
Consider a fleet serving 100 distinct 7B models (26 GB footprint each) and 10 distinct 175B models (350 GB footprint each). Assume load testing has established that each proposed co-resident pair meets its throughput and tail-latency objectives with the required headroom and isolation. The memory-utilization percentages use the same conservative admission budgets. The example computes the resulting capacity reduction under the load-testing assumption; memory fit alone would not establish it.

Strategy A (exclusive access, one model per GPU): The 7B models require 100 A100 GPUs (80 GB each), achieving only 32.5 percent memory utilization (100 \(\times\) 26 GB used out of 8000 GB total). The 175B models require 80 GPUs (10 models \(\times\) 8 GPUs each for tensor parallelism). Total fleet: 180 GPUs.

Strategy B (MPS for small models, exclusive for large): Pack 2 models per A100 for the 7B models (2 \(\times\) 26 GB = 52 GB, well within 80 GB), requiring only 50 GPUs at 65 percent memory utilization. The 175B models remain unchanged at 80 GPUs. Total fleet: 130 GPUs.

Under the load-testing assumption, sharing reduces the required fleet size by 27.8 percent (50 fewer GPUs). At $2/GPU-hour, the released capacity is worth approximately $876,000 annually at the scenario rate. It becomes a cash saving when the organization releases billed resources or avoids purchasing that capacity.

8.10.3 The training-serving resource boundary

Separate training and serving pools simplify capacity ownership but can leave useful work queued beside idle devices. Consider a serving pool reserved for peak traffic while a training pool remains backlogged. This static partitioning protects serving capacity from training demand, but unused serving resources cannot help the training queue. Dynamic sharing allows that borrowing only when the orchestrator can return ready serving capacity before it is needed. The mode-switching cost determines how much capacity can safely move between the pools.

Repurposing a GPU from training to serving can require saving training state, releasing the old allocation, launching the inference runtime, loading weights, and warming the replica. The orchestrator must measure the complete path to readiness, including any overlap between these operations. If that path takes minutes, reclaiming training capacity cannot cover a burst that exhausts the serving reserve in seconds. Forecasts can schedule transfers ahead of predictable demand, while already-ready headroom covers shorter bursts; the required reserve depends on forecast error and measured reclaim time.

Consider a daily traffic pattern with low demand at 3 AM, growth at 8 AM, a peak at 2 PM, and a decline at 10 PM. A forecast with a 30-minute lead allows the orchestrator to begin reclaiming training allocations at 7:30 AM, provided measured replica startup fits within that lead time. As traffic falls, it drains surplus replicas before returning their devices to training. Pricing that policy against a static split needs three quantities: what serving reserves, what it uses on average, and how much of the difference training can absorb without eroding the surge buffer. The example below assumes its surge buffer suffices; a production policy must size the buffer against its own burst distribution and forecast errors.

Napkin Math 8.5: The diurnal GPU shift
Consider a fleet of 1,024 GPUs managed under two regimes:

Scenario A (static partitioning): The serving pool reserves 400 GPUs. That reservation is the forecast peak of 283 GPUs plus a 117-GPU surge buffer that absorbs forecast error and bursts shorter than a replica’s startup time. Average usage is only 150 GPUs (37.5 percent utilization). The training pool takes the rest of the fleet after the serving reservation and 6 GPUs held back as maintenance headroom, which leaves 618 GPUs, and its backlog keeps them at 100 percent utilization. Result: 150 GPUs + 618 GPUs = 768 active GPUs. Fleet utilization: 75 percent. The maintenance headroom stays unallocated in both scenarios.

Scenario B (dynamic sharing): Serving holds forecast demand (average 150 GPUs) plus the same 117-GPU surge buffer. Training reclaims the rest of the serving reservation, which is the unused 250 GPUs on average minus the buffer and averages 133 GPUs over the course of the day (the medium-blue band in figure 15). Because each role switch costs minutes, the orchestrator moves this capacity on the forecast rather than chasing individual traffic spikes. Result: serving (150 GPUs) + training (618 GPUs + 133 GPUs) = 901 active GPUs (the second training term is reclaimed serving capacity). Fleet utilization: 88 percent.

Dynamic sharing recovers 133 GPUs of effective capacity. At $2/GPU-hour, this creates approximately $2.3M per year in value without purchasing a single additional accelerator.

Safe sharing requires both a measured interference budget and an enforceable right to reclaim capacity. Isolation mechanisms address the first requirement; they do not decide which team owns a device or when a borrower must release it. That second requirement is an allocation contract across teams, and it applies to every pool the orchestrator manages, serving and training alike.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why does the fleet orchestrator schedule a 175B-parameter serving replica as an eight-GPU group rather than as eight independent GPUs?

    1. Kubernetes cannot address more than one GPU per pod unless the pod is marked as a gang.
    2. The replica’s weights are sharded across a tensor-parallel group, so it can serve only when every member is placed; a partial placement delivers no serving capacity at all.
    3. Eight independent GPUs would each need a full copy of the weights, which improves fault tolerance but wastes memory.
    4. Grouping GPUs lets the orchestrator skip readiness checks for the replica.

  2. In a multi-tenant inference environment hosting multiple smaller models on shared GPUs, what is the primary architectural trade-off between NVIDIA Multi-Process Service (MPS) and Multi-Instance GPU (MIG)?

    1. MIG is only supported on consumer GeForce cards, whereas MPS requires enterprise Grace Hopper superchips.
    2. MPS provides sub-nanosecond hardware memory encryption, whereas MIG only supports unencrypted cleartext transfers.
    3. MPS requires dedicated physical PCIe switches per tenant, whereas MIG operates over standard Ethernet cables.
    4. MPS permits concurrent clients sharing execution and memory-bandwidth resources, while MIG partitions device compute and memory resources more strongly at the cost of fixed profiles and placement constraints.

  3. How can an organization exploit predictable diurnal serving demand, and what readiness and reclamation contract is necessary to protect serving SLOs?

  4. Why can a shared fleet not move GPUs between training and serving in response to second-by-second traffic bursts, and how does the orchestrator use the diurnal traffic curve instead?

  5. True or False: If two serving models fit together within an 80 GB GPU’s memory under MPS, co-locating them is guaranteed to halve the number of GPUs the fleet needs without affecting latency.

  6. Instead of statically partitioning a serving cluster sized for peak traffic and a separate training cluster, an orchestrator that lends idle overnight serving GPUs to training and reclaims them before the morning ramp practices dynamic ____.

8.11 Multi-Tenancy and Quotas

When the computer vision team hoards 500 GPUs for a week while the natural language processing team sits blocked trying to push a critical bug fix to production, shared clusters devolve into a tragedy of the commons. Teams with the most aggressive job submission rates, the largest jobs, or the most persistent resubmission scripts consume disproportionate resources, while teams with more modest or intermittent needs find the cluster perpetually occupied. The result is organizational friction and underinvestment in slower-moving but potentially higher-value projects whose teams cannot compete for capacity on submission volume alone. A static serving reservation faces the same pressure from the other direction, since a training team that sees idle serving devices will want them.

Multi-tenancy and quotas turn organizational priorities into rules for resource allocation, borrowing, and reclamation that the scheduler can enforce. A quota defines an entitlement or a limit. Whether that entitlement becomes prompt, productive access depends on borrowing that lends idle guarantees to other teams and on reclamation that returns them within a bounded time, both developed below. Accounting that separates the capacity a team holds from the capacity it uses well belongs with utilization debugging in section 8.12.1.

8.11.1 Hierarchical fair-share

Assigning fixed GPU quotas at the namespace or project level (for example, 500 GPUs to Team A, 300 to Team B, and 200 to Team C) gives each team a clear entitlement, but leads to systematic underutilization whenever workloads fluctuate. If Team A’s 500-GPU allocation sits 50 percent idle during a quiet period while Team B’s queue overflows with urgent work, rigid quotas leave 250 GPUs wasted that Team B is forbidden to use. Allocating smaller static quotas to each team creates the opposite failure: teams are blocked from running legitimate work during peak periods even when the cluster has spare capacity overall.

Hierarchical quotas, which Slurm expresses through its account hierarchy and fair-share accounting (equation 1) and Kueue through ClusterQueues grouped into borrowing cohorts, separate a team’s guaranteed share from its maximum permitted allocation. In equation 4, \(\TeamQuotaCap\) denotes a team’s maximum burst cap rather than its guarantee, \(\DepartmentQuotaLimit\) is the department’s total capacity limit, and \(\SiblingTeamAllocation\) is each sibling team’s current active allocation. Assuming those allocations remain within the department limit, the smaller of the team’s burst cap and the department’s currently unallocated capacity sets the team’s admission ceiling before any reclamation:

\[ \EffectiveAdmissionCeiling= \min\left(\TeamQuotaCap,\ \DepartmentQuotaLimit- \sum_{\text{other teams}} \SiblingTeamAllocation\right) \tag{4}\]

The second term inside the minimum is what lends idle guarantees across sibling teams. If Team A uses only 60 percent of its 500-GPU guarantee, its active allocation \(\SiblingTeamAllocation\) drops below its guarantee and leaves the remaining 200 GPUs in \(\DepartmentQuotaLimit - \sum_{\text{other teams}} \SiblingTeamAllocation\) for sibling teams to claim, up to each borrower’s own cap \(\TeamQuotaCap\). This ceiling bounds what a team may hold at admission, and it encodes neither fair-share weights nor guarantees. When several teams contend for that spare capacity at once, the scheduler still applies its fairness policy, the dominant-resource calculation of section 8.4.1, to the feasible gangs that fit under each ceiling.

8.11.2 Burst capacity and quota borrowing

Lending turns unused guarantees into burst capacity. Suppose a small team holds a guarantee of 64 GPUs but needs 256 for a weekend-long training run while a sibling team’s guaranteed share sits idle. The small team may borrow idle whole devices up to its maximum quota. Those devices remain part of physical cluster capacity rather than becoming virtual capacity, and they carry lower preemption priority than owned resources. When the sibling team submits work that needs its guarantee, the scheduler reclaims the borrowed devices by preempting or shrinking the borrower’s opportunistic jobs.

Borrowing resolves that static-quota dilemma only when the lent capacity remains reclaimable within the platform’s response-time objective, safety headroom, and current physical capacity. A team entitled to 500 GPUs can reclaim borrowed devices according to that contract, but the scheduler must not promise an immediate 500-GPU allocation when failures, nonpreemptible reservations, or recovery headroom reduce reclaimable capacity.

Burst capacity therefore expands aspirational maxima and fair-share weights, not simultaneous guarantees. The sum of guarantees that the platform promises to honor concurrently must fit reclaimable physical capacity after nonpreemptible reservations and safety headroom. Teams may advertise higher maximums or weights because borrowed jobs yield when guaranteed demand returns, but those values are not guarantees. Fractional sharing is a separate mechanism that requires an explicit MIG, MPS, or time-slicing contract.

Reclamation couples quota policy to the checkpoint infrastructure. The admission controller separates guaranteed jobs from opportunistic jobs. Guaranteed jobs run on the team’s owned quota and face preemption only from higher-priority work. Opportunistic jobs run on borrowed capacity, yield first, and must checkpoint often enough that reclamation wastes little work, since every forced reclaim pays the preemption overhead of section 8.8.1. Elastic jobs soften reclamation further through the graceful preemption of section 8.7.2, shrinking one replica at a time toward their minimum viable allocation rather than terminating.

One policy for quota borrowing uses four tiers. Production serving (Priority 0) holds capacity protected from routine preemption. Production training (Priority 1) yields only under a defined serving-failover policy. Interactive and debug sessions (Priority 2) receive short execution limits to preserve iteration speed without indefinite resource occupation. Batch and research tasks (Priority 3) use surplus capacity and yield under their reclamation contract. These numbers label this example’s tiers; they are not Kubernetes priority values, where larger integers indicate higher priority. The hierarchy establishes precedence, but deadline protection still requires feasible capacity and bounded reclamation time, while lower-priority work needs an explicit starvation policy.

As the over-subscription discussion in section 8.4.2 showed, a partly idle device is still a whole allocated device, so the safe burst limit follows from reclaimability rather than average SM utilization. The scheduler must measure how quickly borrowed jobs can checkpoint, shrink, or terminate, and it must reserve the I/O and network bandwidth required for that transition. Only then can it bound the amount of opportunistic capacity that is safe to lend while still honoring the guarantee’s response-time objective.

8.11.3 Security and namespace isolation

Teams that share a fleet must also be kept from observing or altering one another’s model weights, training data, and inference traffic. A namespace provides only an administrative boundary that host, device, network, and storage controls must enforce. Network policy must cover the collective paths an admitted gang actually uses, and host networking moves the enforcement point off the pod network, so each path needs its own verification.

↩︎ Fabric-level isolation, through virtual lanes and traffic classes that keep one job’s bursts from stretching another job’s collectives, is established in Traffic isolation and quality of service.

Across teams, the isolation spectrum of table 7 becomes a trust-boundary choice, because colocated workloads now belong to owners with different exposure to one another. Side-channel attacks on shared GPUs are a documented vulnerability; by monitoring contention on shared caches or memory controllers, a malicious tenant can infer the architecture or even data properties of a co-resident model. In sensitive environments, MIG or dedicated devices can strengthen device-resource isolation, but neither creates a complete external-tenant security boundary by itself. A multi-tenant platform combines device partitioning with VM or container isolation, IOMMU configuration, driver and runtime hardening, authentication and authorization, and network and storage controls. Single-organization workloads with a narrower trust boundary can use MPS or time-slicing when weaker fault and performance isolation is acceptable.

↪ The attack surface of shared accelerators, and which sharing mechanisms leave co-resident tenants observable to one another, is developed in Shared accelerators and caches.

8.11.4 Quota governance and organizational dynamics

Even when a scheduler enforces quotas accurately, a guarantee set six months ago can leave 60 percent of a team’s reserved GPUs underused after its flagship model ships while another team’s urgent launch queue backs up. While the scheduler manages immediate resource assignments, quota governance revises the entitlements and limits those assignments must honor. Periodic quota review cycles compare observed use, forecast demand, and the cost of meeting each team’s capacity promise. A team using only 40 percent of a 128-GPU guarantee does not necessarily block other teams if unused devices can be borrowed. The governance question is whether the guarantee remains justified and whether its reclamation contract lets the fleet use spare capacity productively between demand peaks.

The central tension in quota management is the hoarding problem, in which teams retain idle reservations to avoid queuing again when work becomes ready. A workflow that pauses policy training while awaiting human annotations or a training run delayed by unfinished tokenization can leave its reserved accelerators unused, reflecting dependencies in particular workflows rather than an unavoidable idle phase of all training. Utilization-based reclamation can release idle physical allocations to borrowers, provided the platform preserves any promised return time; revising the team’s longer-term guarantee instead requires comparing expected demand with observed use. Clear return contracts reduce the incentive to hold idle devices without making low activity alone grounds for breaking a capacity promise.

Quota accounting should remain close to the scheduling decision. Every admitted job needs an owner, project, workload class, and preemption contract so the scheduler can apply quota and recovery policy consistently, and Kubernetes labels and Slurm accounts carry this scheduling identity. GPU-hours charged against a reservation, borrowed share, or preemption class then make the opportunity cost of hoarding visible and let the control plane reclaim idle guarantees.

Example 8.1: Quota hoarding
Scenario: A research team reserves a static block of 500 GPUs for an upcoming model refresh.

Diagnosis: Upstream data delays postpone training, but the team retains the reservation to ensure future availability. The GPUs sit idle for 3 weeks, accumulating $504,000 in capital waste while other teams’ queues back up.

Systems lesson: An idle reservation should trigger a review of lending eligibility and future demand. The orchestrator can admit opportunistic borrowers under a bounded reclamation contract without changing the owner’s guarantee. Reducing that guarantee is a separate governance decision; lending owned capacity does not turn it into cloud spot capacity.

A “use it or lose it” reclamation policy addresses the most visible symptom of quota hoarding. Lending idle capacity to another team, however, requires the system to distinguish borrowed capacity that is preemptible on short notice from guaranteed reservations protected by SLOs. Defining borrowing, preemption, and guaranteed tiers as first-class policy primitives is the core multi-tenancy design decision.

Checkpoint 8.4: Multi-tenancy design decisions

Consider a 2,000-GPU cluster shared between a research team (60 percent allocation) and a production team (40 percent allocation):

Even with isolation, quotas, and dynamic sharing in place, a cluster can report low utilization beside a full queue. Top-level dashboards rarely explain why pending jobs cannot claim the free devices. Locating that loss requires systematic utilization debugging across hardware pools, scheduler constraints, and user request habits.

Self-Check: Question

  1. In a multi-tenant cluster, how does decoupling ‘guaranteed quota’ from ‘limit (burst) quota’ prevent stranded idle capacity while protecting team allocations?

  2. What is ‘quota hoarding’ in shared GPU clusters, and what automated scheduler policies prevent researchers from engaging in it?

  3. Team A in Department X submits a new 16-GPU job. Which entitlement, feasibility, fairness, and ownership checks must its scheduler enforce before granting the allocation, and why is checking free GPU count alone insufficient?
