8.12 Debugging Cluster Utilization

A cluster dashboard shows average GPU utilization stuck at 60 percent, yet developers complain about three-day queue times for small debugging jobs. Hundreds of GPUs sit idle, but no jobs are starting. Explaining the gap requires tracing the interactions among hardware topology, scheduler policies, and user behavior. Part of the paradox is the queueing arithmetic of notebook 8.1. The \(\ServingUtilization/(1-\ServingUtilization)\) factor grows without bound as offered load approaches service capacity, so mean wait rises gently through moderate load and then steeply above 80 percent for small, mixed, and large workloads alike (figure 16, plotted on a logarithmic scale). The curves use the notebook’s exponential reference case (\(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient= 1\)), so they are the gentlest version of the effect. A mixed ML queue with the notebook’s representative \(\ServiceTimeVariationCoefficient= 3\) multiplies every curve by the larger variability factor, shifting each one upward without changing where it bends. The curves also describe offered service load rather than allocated or kernel-active GPU percentages, and their 75 percent headroom marker is an illustrative choice rather than a universal cluster threshold. Queueing explains why a busy fleet waits. A fleet whose queue is full while devices sit idle is waiting for a different reason, and finding it requires looking past the dashboard.

8.12.1 Allocated, active, and productive utilization

The 64 GPUs that sat idle for a weekend because a researcher forgot to cancel a reservation represent invisible waste in a multi-tenant cloud fleet. In a dedicated cluster, such waste is at least visible to the team that owns the hardware. In a shared environment, the cost is hidden and compounds across thousands of jobs. Effective governance requires moving beyond simple “allocation” metrics to a tiered accounting model that distinguishes between what was requested, what was used, and what was useful.

Three tiers of measurement expose this gap between requested hardware and actual progress. Allocated capacity counts resources held by complete allocations, so devices assigned to a gang that still waits for its remaining members do not count. Active utilization measures hardware activity under a stated counter definition, which can include communication kernels or work later replayed. Productive utilization requires a workload-level measure of retained training progress or serving work that meets its objective, normalized to the job’s own fault-free step rate. For example, a team allocated 500 GPUs might average 350 active-device equivalents (70 percent activity) while delivering 280 device-equivalents of useful work at its fault-free rate (56 percent of its allocation). These are time-averaged equivalents, not necessarily distinct sets of active and productive devices. The gap motivates profiling; it does not identify its cause or imply that necessary communication and recovery can all be eliminated. Accounting boundaries uses the allocated and productive tiers as the boundaries of scheduling and runtime goodput.

Allocated, active, and productive capacity in GPU equivalents, each tier counting less than the one above.

This data feeds the utilization dashboard, the cluster operator’s primary instrument for fleet health. This view must synthesize per-team utilization against quotas, queue depth by priority class, and the fragmentation index, a measure of free GPUs that cannot be allocated due to topology constraints. It must also track preemption rates, spot interruptions, checkpoint traffic, and the recovery overhead they induce. These measurements let the scheduler distinguish idle capacity it can reclaim from allocated capacity whose apparent idleness reflects communication, I/O, or a rigid gang dependency.

Automated anomaly detection turns these metrics into investigation triggers. A sudden spike in a team’s consumption to 3\(\times\) its historical norm might reflect a runaway submission script or an intentional experiment. A growing queue can indicate insufficient capacity, but it can also reflect quota limits, fragmented placements, or a stalled dependency. A sudden power drop can accompany a shared storage stall, though job completions and changes in workload mix can produce a similar signal. Operators must correlate these alerts with admission decisions and job progress before changing capacity or policy.

Systems Perspective 8.5: The three utilization metrics
Each tier narrows the investigation. Low allocated utilization calls for checking demand, quotas, device health, and placement feasibility. Low active utilization on allocated devices calls for tracing input supply, communication waits, and runtime execution. Low productive utilization despite busy kernels calls for comparing retained progress with replay, synchronization, and other overhead. The metric selects the next evidence to collect; it does not by itself select the remedy.

8.12.2 Policy-infrastructure mismatch

When a cluster adds a new generation of high-memory GPUs alongside older pools, job templates written for the newest hardware can leave the older pools sitting idle while dozens of jobs queue behind the newest partition. Diagnosing this policy-infrastructure mismatch requires comparing scheduler state with physical capability and inspecting job requests against each hardware pool. A full queue and idle accelerators can coexist when the scheduler is honoring constraints that the top-level dashboard has aggregated away. The cluster at the center of example 8.2 runs the heterogeneous hardware in table 8, accumulated across three procurement cycles.

Example 8.2: Debugging low GPU utilization
Scenario: A 1,024-GPU cluster reports 60 percent average utilization despite over 50 pending jobs in queue.

Diagnosis: Over-specification (users copying A100-80 GB templates for 35 GB memory jobs), pool fragmentation, and legacy V100 avoidance leave A100-40 GB and V100 nodes idle while large jobs wait for A100-80 GB allocations. The scheduler correctly enforced policies written for a homogeneous fleet that no longer matched the heterogeneous infrastructure.

Systems lesson: Surface-level diagnosis often mistakes policy-infrastructure mismatches for scheduling algorithm bugs. Updating scheduler policies to support automatic capability matching, dynamic memory profiling, and backfilling raises cluster utilization to 84 percent, recovering $7M in annual compute capacity. Table 9 breaks that gain out pool by pool, using the allocated and active tiers of section 8.12.1.

For this capacity valuation, assume that each active GPU-hour delivers the same retained useful work before and after the policy change, including across hardware pools. Under that simplifying assumption, the observed utilization gain is equivalent to approximately 246 fully active GPUs of productive work (table 9). A heterogeneous production fleet must validate that conversion against workload progress and measured throughput in each pool. As at the start of the chapter, procurement planning compares the gain with the hardware it replaces, here 410 additional raw GPUs, because new devices under the old policies would also run at only 60 percent utilization. At $2/GPU-hour, that avoided purchase represents approximately $7M in annual recovered value, achieved through policy changes requiring zero additional hardware investment.

8.12.3 Common utilization anti-patterns

Fixing policy-infrastructure mismatches gets queued jobs onto GPUs, but a dashboard reporting 85 percent allocation still says only how much capacity is assigned, not whether those allocations deliver productive work. Inside allocated capacity, four runtime patterns commonly widen the gap between the allocated, active, and productive tiers, and separating them requires correlating device telemetry with execution timelines.

A zombie job retains an allocation after its useful execution has stopped. In a distributed run training a 175B parameter model, a rank failure can leave a 128-GPU group idle if worker cleanup or failure propagation does not complete. The surviving ranks may block inside a collective that will never finish, or a process left behind by a driver-level fault may resist reaping by the container runtime, so the devices stay allocated until a timeout or node-level remediation clears them. Persistent memory allocation combined with low SM activity is an investigation signal, not proof of a driver fault; valid input or communication waits can look similar. The operator must compare runtime health, step progress, and the workload’s timeout contract, then verify that old workers have stopped before releasing or reassigning their devices.

The data starvation pattern manifests as a high-frequency sawtooth wave in GPU utilization, where the device oscillates between 100 percent load (forward/backward pass) and 0 percent load (waiting for the next batch). While the average utilization might appear acceptable at 60 to 70 percent, the GPU is effectively idling for a third of its life. This occurs when the CPU-bound data pipeline cannot sustain the throughput required by the GPU, a common scenario in computer vision where JPEG decoding and augmentation saturate host CPUs. The data stall ratio of equation 4.4 quantifies the loss as the fraction of each step spent waiting for input. A timeline that aligns the idle intervals with input waits supports the diagnosis; utilization variance alone does not identify the cause. The remedy depends on whether decoding, storage, or transfer limits the input path.

A communication bottleneck can leave ranks waiting for collectives, though communication kernels can also keep SM activity high. Per-rank gradient traffic depends on gradient precision, sharding, and the parallelism layout, so the ratio of step time to compute-only time (forward plus backward passes in isolation) is a quicker first bound on exposed overhead. If total iteration time exceeds 1.5\(\times\) compute-only time, non-compute overhead exceeds 0.5\(\times\) compute time, which is material but not dominant. Setting \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\) and \(\OverlappedTime\) to zero in the Fleet Law (equation 1.3) leaves \(\DistributedStepTime(\DeviceCount) = \IronLawComputeTime/\DeviceCount+ \CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\), under which communication exceeds per-device computation only when the ratio is greater than 2\(\times\). Real steps also contain synchronization waits and input stalls, so profiling must separate communication from those before the ratio is read as a communication diagnosis. Placement can reduce exposed communication when it improves the relevant paths. Gradient accumulation can reduce synchronization frequency only when the runtime defers gradient exchange across accumulation steps.

Memory fragmentation can prevent a serving replica from admitting new requests even when its HBM occupancy suggests free capacity. The KV-cache allocator, rather than the aggregate occupancy percentage, decides whether a new sequence fits in contiguous or paged memory blocks. For the fleet scheduler, the allocator matters through its result, the number of concurrent requests a replica can hold, because that measured capacity sets how many replicas the serving envelope needs.

↪ Paged KV-cache allocation and its effect on replica concurrency are developed in PagedAttention.

The debugging example connects scheduling mechanics to fleet governance. Technical policies (GPU type matching, gang scheduling timeouts, preemption grace periods) interact with organizational policies (team quotas, priority hierarchies, cost allocation) and human behavior (job template reuse, resource request habits, queue submission patterns). Effective fleet orchestration requires attending to all three layers simultaneously, because the most expensive scheduling bug is often not a software defect but a policy that was correct for the old infrastructure and was never updated for the new one.

Self-Check: Question

  1. A cluster dashboard reports \(85\%\) GPU utilization, yet researchers see slow progress. Which distinction between utilization tiers explains the contradiction?

    1. Allocated capacity can be high while active utilization is low because jobs wait on input or communication, and productive utilization can be lower still because of restarts and replayed work.
    2. The dashboard counts only CPU time, so GPU activity is invisible to it.
    3. High utilization always means the cluster is healthy, so the researchers’ perception must be wrong.
    4. Productive utilization always exceeds allocated utilization, so the dashboard understates the cluster’s progress.

  2. An administrator observes that average GPU compute utilization across a 1,000-GPU cluster is only \(55\%\), yet users experience multi-day queue wait times for 64-GPU jobs. Which diagnostic finding points to the primary root cause?

    1. Demand exceeds the cluster’s capacity, so the queue can shrink only after more GPUs are purchased.
    2. Severe resource fragmentation and strict gang scheduling constraints prevent 64-GPU contiguous blocks from forming, while short jobs hold scattered GPUs across nodes without filling them.
    3. At 55 percent load, the queueing factor \(\ServingUtilization/(1-\ServingUtilization)\) alone already makes multi-day waits unavoidable.
    4. Input-pipeline stalls lower SM activity on running jobs, and low SM activity is what keeps the scheduler from admitting the 64-GPU jobs.

  3. During a multi-GPU training job, GPU SM utilization oscillates between 10 percent and 95 percent, host CPUs are busy, and disk wait is high. What hypothesis should the operator test next before choosing a remedy?

    1. A zombie allocation; the low phases show that the job has stopped making progress, so it should be terminated.
    2. A communication bottleneck; the ranks are waiting on collectives, so the job should be moved under a single leaf switch.
    3. The input pipeline may be limiting progress; align GPU idle intervals with batch-readiness waits, then measure whether decoding, storage, or transfer is the limiting stage.
    4. KV-cache memory fragmentation; the allocator cannot fit the next batch even though HBM occupancy shows free capacity.

  4. What is a ‘zombie allocation’ (or ghost job) in a GPU cluster, and how can automated health monitors detect and remediate it?

  5. True or False: In a ring AllReduce across 32 nodes, if 31 nodes show \(95\%\) GPU utilization and 1 node shows \(10\%\) utilization, the node with \(10\%\) utilization is the only healthy node while the 31 nodes are encountering network hardware faults.

  6. A dashboard shows 120 free GPUs, yet a 64-GPU gang waits for days because the free devices sit one or two per node across racks that its topology constraint cannot combine. A ____ index, which counts free GPUs that cannot be allocated because of topology constraints, makes this stranded capacity visible where aggregate utilization hides it.

8.13 Fallacies and Pitfalls

Those policy-infrastructure mismatches recur as a small set of misconceptions that can make an apparently healthy scheduler waste expensive fleet capacity. A cluster running at 98 percent utilization can look healthy while researchers have stopped submitting jobs because wait times have stretched into weeks. Each misconception below follows the same pattern, in which optimizing one visible metric degrades the outcome the fleet exists to deliver.

Fallacy: More sophisticated scheduling algorithms always improve utilization.

Engineers facing low utilization often reach for more advanced schedulers, assuming the algorithm is the bottleneck. As the debugging example in section 8.12 demonstrates, the root cause is frequently policy misconfiguration, not algorithmic limitation. A simple FIFO scheduler with correct policies (capability-based matching, backfill with timeouts, appropriate gang scheduling constraints) often outperforms a sophisticated scheduler with incorrect policies. Before upgrading the scheduler, audit the policies: confirm that users are not requesting resources they do not need, that heterogeneous resources are properly exposed, and that gang-scheduling timeouts are configured. The audit recurs as workloads evolve, because a preemption grace period that suffices for one model can be too short for another to save state, and placement weights suited to an earlier topology can strand capacity after an expansion.

Pitfall: Treating all GPU-hours as equal when measuring utilization.

An unlabeled “GPU utilization” percentage can conflate allocation with activity or useful work. For example, 80 percent of fleet capacity might be assigned to jobs while 15 percent of the fleet sits in groups still waiting for their remaining members and another 5 percent waits for input. Only 65 percent is then allocated to complete groups, and at most 60 percent executes useful training in that snapshot. Such a breakdown comes from scheduler and runtime evidence, which an activity counter cannot supply. The allocated, active, and productive tiers of section 8.12.1 keep these measurements distinct, with productive capacity tied to a stated workload objective and the job’s fault-free rate.

Fallacy: Gang scheduling is always necessary for distributed training.

Gang scheduling prevents partial-allocation deadlock for a rigid synchronous group, but the required group need not equal the job’s maximum requested size. Asynchronous workers may progress independently, while elastic training can begin with its minimum viable gang and resize at supported boundaries. Independent hyperparameter trials can also run separately. The scheduler gains flexibility by admitting the actual unit of progress, while preserving atomic allocation wherever synchronous dependencies require it. Changes to worker count or synchronization policy still require convergence validation.

Pitfall: Setting static quotas based on peak demand.

Organizations commonly set team quotas to handle worst-case demand, reasoning that teams need guaranteed access during crunch periods. If Team A’s peak demand is 500 GPUs and Team B’s is 300 GPUs, the cluster needs 800 GPUs with static quotas. In practice, peaks rarely coincide. A 600-GPU cluster can accommodate either peak when the other team’s simultaneous demand leaves enough capacity, but it cannot guarantee both peaks concurrently. Hierarchical fair-share with borrowing uses that statistical opportunity only when the declared guarantees fit physical capacity and borrowed work can yield within the reclamation contract. In this example, sizing static quotas to the sum of the peaks provisions 200 GPUs, a quarter of the 800-GPU cluster, as guarantees that sit unused whenever the peaks do not coincide.

Fallacy: Spot instances are always cheaper for training.

Actual savings depend on the billed overhead each interruption adds, not on the scenario’s 60 to 90 percent discount band. Consider 15-minute checkpoint overhead to slow storage and 30-minute restart overhead to reacquire capacity and reload, far longer than a restart on a cluster’s own file system, totaling 45 minutes of billed nonproductive time per event. If each event follows 2 hours of retained training progress, the nominal 65 percent discount becomes about 52 percent savings under the fixed-allocation model. This illustration assumes a successful save and no replay; a real interruption budget must also include discarded progress when the save cannot finish. The scheduler must separately account for unbilled capacity waits when evaluating deadlines. Sufficient billed overhead can erase the discount, regardless of the advertised rate.

Pitfall: Ignoring topology when scheduling distributed training.

Schedulers that ignore connectivity can place communication-intensive groups across paths with less available bandwidth or more contention. On a fleet with NVLink confined to each node, spreading a tensor-parallel group across nodes moves its traffic onto the inter-node fabric; other hardware can expose larger NVLink domains. Crossing a spine switch does not itself prove congestion, so the placement decision must compare effective path capacity and competing traffic. A measured throughput penalty accumulates over a long run and may exceed the delay of waiting for a more suitable allocation. The scheduler must weigh that gain against queue delay and fragmentation.

Pitfall: Using elastic training to avoid gang-scheduling decisions.

Elastic training complements gang scheduling by moving between viable execution groups. With a fixed 8-way tensor-parallel layout, each group still requires eight workers; changing the number of data-parallel replicas does not relax that requirement. A runtime that changes the tensor or pipeline layout must also redistribute state and establish a new viable group. The scheduler must honor the supported resize granularity, atomic admission requirements, and placement budget for each configuration. Elasticity adds feasible choices; it does not make an incomplete group productive or an arbitrary placement efficient.

Pitfall: Treating high utilization as proof of cluster health.

A high allocated-GPU percentage does not establish that the cluster has enough headroom to admit new work promptly, because waiting time climbs steeply as offered load nears capacity (figure 16). That queueing model does not turn a GPU-busy percentage into a prediction for a gang-scheduled fleet. Real headroom targets depend on arrival bursts, job sizes, placement constraints, and the queue-delay or serving-latency objective. Measuring those outcomes instead of applying a universal utilization threshold keeps platform teams from optimizations that improve a dashboard metric while delaying the work the fleet exists to complete.

Self-Check: Question

  1. Why is targeting near \(100\%\) GPU cluster utilization an operational anti-pattern for an interactive AI research and development organization?

    1. GPU hardware components melt if utilization exceeds \(90\%\) for more than 5 consecutive minutes.
    2. Modern deep learning frameworks automatically downgrade model accuracy when cluster utilization exceeds \(85\%\).
    3. Operating above \(95\%\) utilization causes cloud providers to double their hourly billing rates without warning.
    4. Near saturation, queueing delays can rise sharply and delay experimental feedback; allocation utilization alone does not establish a safe headroom target for the fleet.

  2. Explain the fallacy that ‘more sophisticated scheduling algorithms always improve utilization,’ and describe what operators should audit before replacing their cluster scheduler.

  3. True or False: Gang scheduling is strictly mandatory for every distributed ML workload, including asynchronous parameter-server training, hyperparameter sweeps, and elastic fine-tuning jobs.

8.14 Summary

Fleet orchestration turns data center capacity into useful ML work by deciding which jobs hold which accelerators, where, and for how long. Orchestration spends Coordination, the third axis of C³, so that Compute and Communication capacity turn into useful progress. In Fleet Law terms (equation 1.3), placement sets the communication term \(\CommunicationTime(\DeviceCount)\) of every step, and preemption and recovery, amortized over the run, enter the coordination term \(\SynchronizationTime(\DeviceCount)\). Queue wait and gang admission are coordination costs too, but they delay the first step rather than lengthening any step, so they appear in end-to-end completion time rather than in the per-step Fleet Law. Multi-tenancy and quota policy decide how much of that time allocated compute spends doing useful work. Hardware capability sets the feasible choices; the scheduler turns those choices into useful allocations by preserving ownership, coordinating recovery, and matching placement to workload demand.

Feasibility is the first filter. Gang size, topology locality, multi-dimensional fit, and accelerator compatibility decide which allocations a job can actually run, and only then can a fairness policy choose among them (principle 15). Quota borrowing extends the same reasoning across teams, since a guarantee is worth what the platform can reclaim within its response-time objective rather than the number printed on the quota.

Placement changes the physical paths available to a workload. Aligning model parallelism with the network hierarchy reduces exposed communication when placement is the binding constraint, but it cannot create fabric capacity that is not there. Elastic resizing and spot capacity widen the set of feasible choices by trading worker continuity for flexibility and price. ML-aware schedulers sharpen the choice among them with workload signals such as attained service, iteration boundaries, finish-time ratios, and goodput. Every one of these levers pays a transition or integration cost that must be measured against the progress it buys.

At serving time, the fleet scheduler preserves a capacity envelope of indivisible replicas with SLO headroom and moves the training-serving boundary on measured demand, leaving request-level routing and batching to the serving system. When that envelope, the quotas around it, or the placements inside it go wrong, the visible symptom is usually a full queue beside idle accelerators. In practice, the root cause is more often a policy written for an earlier fleet than a defect in the scheduling algorithm.

Key Takeaways: Scheduling is systems engineering
  • Distributed scheduling is hard: Cluster scheduling must coordinate allocation ownership across machines despite partial failures, network partitions, and stale observations. Control planes balance prompt recovery against the risk of authorizing a replacement while an old worker still runs.
  • Gang scheduling prevents deadlock but reduces flexibility: Gang scheduling gives rigid execution groups atomic resource allocation to prevent hold-and-wait deadlocks, while backfilling and elastic resizing recover flexibility within the job’s minimum viable gang and transition constraints.
  • Fairness is measured over feasible bundles: DRF equalizes each tenant’s dominant normalized share across GPUs, CPU, memory, and network, but only among bundles that already pass gang, topology, and compatibility constraints (principle 15). Slowdown and maximum feasible wait test whether the resulting policy avoids delay and starvation, and a quota guarantee holds only as far as lent capacity can be reclaimed in time.
  • Topology determines performance: Where GPUs are placed within the cluster hierarchy changes the communication paths available to a job. Locality can reduce exposed communication, but waiting for a compact placement also costs time; compare end-to-end completion rather than iteration throughput alone.
  • Scheduling signals must match the objective: Workload characteristics support different improvements: attained service addresses runtime uncertainty, iteration boundaries reduce interruption cost, finish-time ratios expose unfair delay, and goodput guides adaptive allocation. Each policy requires validation against the fleet’s workload mix and recovery costs.
  • Useful utilization creates economic capacity: Reducing avoidable idle time can deliver more useful work from an existing fleet. Distinguish allocated, active, and productive capacity before valuing an improvement, and preserve the headroom required for recovery and latency targets.
  • Policies must evolve with infrastructure: Scheduling algorithms are only as effective as the policies they implement. When infrastructure changes (heterogeneous hardware additions, topology upgrades, workload mix shifts), policies must be re-evaluated and updated.

The same placement decision makes the displacement of overhead (principle 14) concrete. Co-locating a tensor-parallel gang keeps its collectives on NVLink and shrinks the exposed communication of every step, but the scheduler pays for that locality in coordination time while it waits for a feasible contiguous allocation. The bottleneck moves from collective bandwidth to queue delay or fragmentation rather than disappearing, so the right placement is the one that minimizes end-to-end completion time, not iteration time alone.

What’s Next: From allocation to useful work
An allocation is the start of fleet efficiency, not evidence of it. A training job can hold a complete allocation while it restarts, waits for input, or exposes communication in every step. A serving replica can hold a full node while its decode steps stream weights and leave most of its arithmetic idle. The productive tier says how much of an allocation became useful work but not which part of a job’s life lost the rest. The owners of scheduling, recovery, and execution therefore each see their own loss, and none can see which is largest. Those separate reports become one product whose levers can be compared once every hour of capacity is followed from the moment the fleet offers it until it becomes useful work, with each loss charged to the boundary where it occurs.

↪ How much of an allocation becomes useful work, and whether scheduling, runtime, or the program loses the rest, is developed in Fleet Efficiency.

8.15 Self-Check Answers

Self-Check: Answer

  1. In the Kalos language-model cluster trace, most jobs requested a single GPU yet consumed a tiny share of GPU time, while jobs of 256 or more GPUs were a few percent of submissions and consumed nearly all GPU time. What does this distribution imply for judging a scheduling policy?

    1. Judge the policy by job count alone, because most users submit small jobs.
    2. Judge the policy by GPU-hours alone, because large jobs hold nearly all of the capacity.
    3. Judge the policy under both views, because small jobs set most users’ waits while a few large gangs hold most of the capacity.
    4. Treat the distribution as an artifact of one cluster, because production fleets have uniform job sizes.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Counted by jobs, the cluster is a stream of small experiments whose users feel queue delay; counted by GPU-hours, it is a few large gangs that decide packing, reservation, and preemption. A policy tuned to either view alone harms the other. Job count alone ignores where the capacity goes, GPU-hours alone ignores most users’ experience, and the trace shows this mix is the normal production case rather than an artifact.

    Learning Objective: Interpret a production job-size distribution under both job-count and GPU-time views.

  2. A 10,000-GPU cluster raises utilization from 50 percent to 80 percent with no new hardware, holding useful work per active GPU-hour constant. Why does the gain count as 3,000 productive GPU-equivalents but replace 6,000 raw GPUs, and when does it become a cash saving?

    Answer: The productive gain is 10,000 times 0.30, or 3,000 fully utilized GPU-equivalents of useful work. Procurement asks how much hardware that gain replaces: new GPUs run under the old policy would also reach only 50 percent utilization, so delivering 3,000 productive equivalents would take 6,000 raw GPUs. The recovered capacity becomes cash only when it avoids a planned purchase or reduces billed resource time; otherwise it is more useful work from the same investment.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish productive GPU-equivalents from raw hardware replaced when valuing a utilization improvement.

  3. True or False: Whether a job must be scheduled as a rigid gang follows from whether it is labeled an ML training job or an HPC job.

    Answer: False. Rigidity is a property of the execution graph, not of the workload’s field. Rigid synchronous jobs in either domain need a fixed set of co-scheduled ranks, while malleable jobs can progress across a declared range of resource counts. The scheduler must model each job’s minimum viable gang and permitted resize points.

    Learning Objective: Classify jobs as rigid or malleable by workload semantics rather than by domain label.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A scheduler control plane separates admission control, placement, and reconciliation. State what each decides, and explain why keeping them separate helps an operator diagnose a job that has not started.

    Answer: Admission decides whether a job may enter the runnable set under quota, priority, gang, and safety constraints. Placement binds an admitted job to a feasible set of nodes given accelerator capability, topology, power, storage, and network capacity. Reconciliation compares desired allocations with observed node and job state and starts, retries, evicts, or fences work until they agree. Separating them distinguishes an admission denial (for example, quota) from a placement failure (no feasible nodes) and from a reconciliation problem (allocation granted but not realized on the nodes).

    Learning Objective: Explain the roles of admission, placement, and reconciliation in a cluster scheduler.

  2. A network partition cuts the scheduler off from four nodes running a training job, and their heartbeats time out. What must hold before the scheduler reassigns those nodes’ GPUs to another job?

    1. Nothing beyond the timeout, because a missed heartbeat proves the old processes have stopped.
    2. The old allocation must be revoked with confirmed cleanup or fenced, for example by rejecting stale allocation generations, so the old work cannot keep using or writing through those GPUs.
    3. The partition must heal first, because no recovery may begin while nodes are unreachable.
    4. The new job must run at higher priority, because priority resolves conflicting ownership.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. A timeout can trigger recovery, but it does not prove the old execution has stopped; reassigning without revocation or fencing risks two owners of the same devices when the partition heals. Waiting for the partition to heal is one policy choice, not a requirement, and priority orders requests without preventing duplicate ownership.

    Learning Objective: Identify the ownership guarantees required before reusing resources after a network partition.

  3. True or False: A hypothetical per-device annual hardware-fault probability of 0.001 is sufficient to conclude that all-cause interruptions will remain rare during multi-week jobs on a 10,000-GPU fleet.

    Answer: False. That hypothetical probability covers only the stated device-fault event. Job interruptions also depend on worker count, runtime faults, links, hosts, and shared power or storage domains. A fleet interruption model must include those causes and their dependencies; a device-level annual figure does not establish a measured all-cause job failure rate.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the mathematical relationship between individual component reliability and aggregate fleet failure frequency in large GPU clusters.

← Back to Questions

Self-Check: Answer

  1. A one-hour job waits nine hours in the queue and then runs for its isolated runtime of one hour. Its ____, the ratio of its queueing-plus-running time to its isolated runtime, is 10, even if its team eventually receives its nominal GPU share.

    Answer: slowdown. Slowdown divides the time from submission to completion by the isolated runtime, here (9 + 1) / 1 = 10. It measures the delay a job experiences, which resource-share parity cannot reveal.

    Learning Objective: Define job slowdown as an outcome measure of the delay a job experiences.

  2. Two teams receive equal dominant shares of the fleet over a month, yet one team’s jobs routinely wait days. Which measurements expose this unfairness, and why does share parity miss it?

    Answer: Share parity measures allocated resources, not the delay users experience before obtaining results. The scheduler should report the distribution of slowdown by user and job class and the age of the oldest pending feasible job: slowdown exposes unequal delay, and maximum feasible wait exposes starvation risk. A policy can be fair in GPU-hours while unfair in the time users wait.

    Learning Objective: Distinguish resource-share fairness from completion-delay fairness and name the metrics for each.

  3. Based on the Pollaczek-Khinchine queue model \(\frac{W_q}{1/\mu} = \frac{\rho}{1-\rho} \cdot \frac{1+C_s^2}{2}\), under this illustrative single-server approximation, how does a queue with heavy-tailed job durations (\(C_s = 3\)) operating at \(\rho = 0.8\) utilization compare in expected waiting time (\(W_q\)) to a queue with the same mean service time and exponential durations (\(C_s = 1\)) at the same utilization?

    1. The ML cluster experiences a \(2\times\) increase in expected wait time (\(8\times\) vs. \(4\times\) the average job duration).
    2. The ML cluster experiences a \(5\times\) increase in expected wait time (\(20\times\) vs. \(4\times\) the average job duration).
    3. The ML cluster experiences a \(9\times\) increase in expected wait time (\(36\times\) vs. \(4\times\) the average job duration).
    4. The ML cluster experiences no change in expected wait time because utilization \(\rho\) dominates the Pollaczek-Khinchine denominator.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. For \(\rho = 0.8\), the utilization multiplier \(\frac{\rho}{1-\rho} = \frac{0.8}{0.2} = 4\). For an exponential distribution (\(C_s = 1\)), the variance term is \(\frac{1+1^2}{2} = 1\), yielding \(W_q = 4 \times (1/\mu)\). For an ML cluster with heavy-tailed durations (\(C_s = 3\)), the variance term is \(\frac{1+3^2}{2} = 5\), yielding \(W_q = 4 \times 5 = 20 \times (1/\mu)\), which represents a \(5\times\) increase in expected wait time. The assertion of a \(2\times\) increase mistakenly scales linearly with \(C_s\) rather than quadratic variance. The \(9\times\) multiplier erroneously squares \(C_s\) without averaging with the baseline offset. The claim of no change ignores service-time variability. This single-server model illustrates queue pressure; a gang-scheduled fleet requires its own measured arrival, placement, and service behavior.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the expected queue waiting time multiplier for heavy-tailed ML workloads compared to uniform workloads using the Pollaczek-Khinchine formula.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. True or False: Under Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF), if Team A’s workload requires \(4\text{ GPUs}\) and \(8\text{ CPU cores}\) per worker while Team B’s workload requires \(1\text{ GPU}\) and \(64\text{ CPU cores}\) per worker on a cluster with \(100\text{ GPUs}\) and \(1000\text{ CPU cores}\), DRF equalizes each team’s share of their dominant resource (GPUs for Team A, CPUs for Team B).

    Answer: True. Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF) multi-resource allocation identifies each tenant’s dominant resource (the resource type for which the tenant requests the largest fraction of total cluster capacity) and equalizes the dominant shares across all active tenants, ensuring multi-resource equity without allowing single-resource monopolies.

    Learning Objective: Apply Dominant Resource Fairness (DRF) principles to multi-resource ML allocations.

  2. When multi-GPU jobs leave unallocated accelerators spread across nodes that cannot be combined to satisfy pending multi-GPU requests due to intra-node interconnect requirements, the cluster suffers from ____.

    Answer: fragmentation (or resource fragmentation). In ML scheduling, fragmentation refers to stranded, non-contiguous capacity across nodes that individually satisfy no pending job, despite the aggregate free GPU count being theoretically sufficient.

    Learning Objective: Identify the primary operational inefficiency caused by multi-dimensional bin-packing constraints in GPU clusters.

  3. In a 1,024-GPU cluster receiving two 1,024-GPU synchronous training jobs, what condition causes a hold-and-wait deadlock under a non-gang scheduler, and how does gang scheduling eliminate it?

    1. Non-gang schedulers allocate partial subsets (e.g., 512 GPUs to each job) where neither job can progress through synchronous AllReduce collectives, whereas gang scheduling enforces atomic all-or-nothing allocation so unallocated jobs hold zero resources.
    2. Non-gang schedulers force jobs to time-slice GPU compute cycles, causing NCCL timeouts during AllReduce, whereas gang scheduling disables CUDA preemption entirely.
    3. Non-gang schedulers assign GPUs across multiple racks, creating switch contention, whereas gang scheduling restricts all 1,024 GPUs to a single physical server chassis.
    4. Non-gang schedulers allow lower-priority jobs to preempt higher-priority jobs, whereas gang scheduling locks node allocations until all jobs in the queue complete.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. In synchronous distributed training, the marginal utility of a partial allocation is zero because every worker must participate in every AllReduce collective. If a non-gang scheduler grants 512 GPUs to Job 1 and 512 GPUs to Job 2, both jobs stall waiting for the remaining GPUs, burning idle power at 0% useful progress. Gang scheduling eliminates the Coffman hold-and-wait condition by mandating atomic all-or-nothing allocation: a job is either granted its full requirement or holds nothing in the queue. The time-slicing distractor confuses multi-tasking with resource allocation. The single-chassis distractor states a physical impossibility for 1,024 GPUs. The lock-queue distractor misrepresents priority preemption.

    Learning Objective: Analyze how gang scheduling prevents distributed deadlocks in synchronous multi-node training.

  4. Describe how priority inversion occurs when a high-priority distributed training job requires accelerators held by a low-priority job, and explain why simply setting higher priority levels does not resolve the blockage.

    Answer: Priority inversion occurs when a high-priority job is blocked waiting for accelerators held by a low-priority job that cannot complete its exit path because intermediate-priority jobs are saturating auxiliary resources (such as host CPU, storage I/O, or network bandwidth needed to write a checkpoint). Simply increasing priority levels fails because the blockage is transitive: the high-priority job depends on the low-priority job exiting, which in turn depends on the low-priority job receiving sufficient auxiliary bandwidth (holistic preemption) to finish checkpointing and release the accelerators.

    Learning Objective: Explain how priority inversion manifests in multi-tenant ML clusters and why strict priority levels alone fail to prevent it.

  5. How does heterogeneous gang scheduling differ from traditional homogeneous gang scheduling in the context of reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) workflows?

    Answer: Traditional gang scheduling allocates identical worker nodes executing symmetric bulk-synchronous parallel graphs. In contrast, heterogeneous gang scheduling atomically co-allocates asymmetric roles within a single logical job, such as memory-optimized nodes with high HBM for inference rollout generation alongside compute-dense nodes for policy gradient updates. The co-allocation requires viable resource bundles and sufficient data-transfer capacity across roles. Measured production and consumption rates reveal imbalance; continuous or perfectly balanced routing is not guaranteed.

    Learning Objective: Compare traditional homogeneous gang scheduling with heterogeneous gang scheduling in multi-role pipelines such as RLHF.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. What is the fundamental difference in control philosophy between Slurm and Kubernetes when managing distributed ML training workloads?

    1. Slurm relies on continuous reconciliation loops against desired state stored in etcd, whereas Kubernetes executes imperative one-time allocation scripts via a centralized daemon.
    2. Slurm uses peer-to-peer gossip protocols to schedule tasks across nodes, whereas Kubernetes enforces hierarchical master-slave token ring passing.
    3. Slurm employs an imperative allocation model where users request explicit physical resources guaranteed at launch, whereas Kubernetes uses a declarative model where controllers continuously reconcile observed cluster state toward declared desired state.
    4. Slurm schedules containerized microservices with dynamic horizontal auto-scaling, whereas Kubernetes only supports uncontainerized bare-metal MPI processes.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Slurm originates from HPC batch systems and uses an imperative model (sbatch requests explicit resources like --gres=gpu:8 --nodes=4), providing authoritative upfront allocation. Kubernetes uses a declarative model where controllers continuously observe actual state and execute reconciliation loops to converge toward desired state (e.g., Pods, Deployments). The option swapping Slurm and Kubernetes mechanisms reverses their core architectures. The gossip protocol alternative invents a non-existent scheduling model for Slurm. The containerization claim reverses the platforms’ primary native workload types.

    Learning Objective: Compare the core architectural control mechanisms of Slurm and Kubernetes for ML training workflows.

  2. Kubernetes’ default scheduler places a 64-pod training job one pod at a time, and the cluster has room for only 60 of the pods. How does a PodGroup with a minimum membership of 64 change the outcome?

    1. The scheduler binds the first 60 pods immediately and starts training with a smaller communication group, adding the last 4 pods as capacity frees.
    2. The scheduler tentatively reserves placements but binds no pod until all 64 members are placed, releasing every reservation if the attempt fails or times out.
    3. The scheduler preempts every lower-priority pod in the cluster until 64 GPUs are free, regardless of priority-class settings.
    4. The PodGroup guarantees that all 64 containers start at the same instant, so the framework no longer needs to establish its communication group.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. A PodGroup turns admission into a group decision. The scheduler reserves placements as it finds them but binds none until the minimum membership is placed, and it releases the reservations on failure or timeout, so 60 pods never hold GPUs while waiting for 4 more. Binding 60 pods recreates the partial allocation that strands capacity, and preemption follows priority-class policy rather than group admission. Group admission also does not make launches simultaneous; the framework must still form its communication group.

    Learning Objective: Explain how PodGroup minimum membership supplies the group admission that a rigid training job needs on Kubernetes.

  3. In the illustrative fair-share priority model (\(\text{Priority}_{\text{effective}} = \text{Priority}_{\text{base}} \times \frac{F_{\text{target}}}{F_{\text{actual}} + \epsilon}\)), what role does the usage decay half-life play, and what is the trade-off between setting a short versus a long half-life?

    Answer: The half-life determines how quickly the scheduler discounts past GPU consumption in \(F_{\text{actual}}\), allowing heavy users to gradually restore their priority. A short half-life (e.g., 1-2 days) rapidly restores priority but can cause oscillatory usage where teams cycle between hogging and starvation; a long half-life (e.g., 2-4 weeks) stabilizes priority order but penalizes researchers long after a major burst project has completed.

    Learning Objective: Explain how usage decay affects priority in the illustrative fair-share model.

  4. Why do high-performance distributed training jobs in Kubernetes typically enable hostNetwork: true and leverage the NVIDIA Network Operator instead of relying on default Container Network Interface (CNI) overlay networks?

    Answer: Host networking changes which network namespace a pod uses and can avoid an overlay path. It does not allocate an RDMA device or grant access to its device files. Device plugins and operators must arrange the required devices, drivers, permissions, and GPU-to-NIC mapping; the fabric must also support the intended transport. Validate bandwidth and latency for that configured path rather than inferring RDMA from hostNetwork alone.

    Learning Objective: Explain the separate network-namespace, device-access, and transport requirements of high-performance training in Kubernetes.

  5. True or False: Kueue replaces the native Kubernetes kube-scheduler binary entirely to implement custom gang scheduling algorithms, whereas Volcano acts purely as an admission controller layered on top of the default scheduler.

    Answer: False. Kueue governs admission and quota reservation while delegating pod placement to a scheduler. Its all-or-nothing mechanisms can track workload readiness and return quota when an admitted workload cannot become ready within its configured contract. Volcano provides batch and gang scheduling and may coexist with the default scheduler. Admission, placement, and readiness are separate controls.

    Learning Objective: Differentiate between scheduler replacement (Volcano) and queue admission control (Kueue) in the Kubernetes batch ecosystem.

  6. **Order the dependencies in this illustrative launch path for a Kubernetes training job configured with a compatible training operator and Volcano group admission:

  1. Volcano verifies a feasible placement for the PodGroup’s minimum viable membership.
  2. The developer submits the training-job manifest defining workers and resource requirements.
  3. Volcano authorizes group placement, and node agents launch the admitted workers using the separately configured device and network resources.
  4. Launched workers establish their communication group through rendezvous before synchronized training begins.
  5. The operator and configured scheduling integration create worker pods and their PodGroup.**

Answer: The correct order is (2) -> (5) -> (1) -> (3) -> (4). Submission supplies the desired job, the configured integration represents its workers and group, and the scheduler checks minimum-group feasibility before authorizing placement. Node launches can complete at different times; group admission does not make them simultaneous. RDMA access requires separate device, driver, permission, and fabric configuration. The framework then forms the communication group before training proceeds.

Learning Objective: Order the operational stages of launching a distributed PyTorch training job via the Kubeflow Training Operator and Volcano.

  1. When scheduling a 3D-parallel large language model across a multi-rack GPU cluster, how should Tensor Parallelism (TP), Pipeline Parallelism (PP), and Data Parallelism (DP) be mapped across the topology hierarchy to maximize throughput?

    1. Map DP strictly within intra-node NVLink domains, TP across inter-rack spine switches, and PP across rail-aligned network switches.
    2. Map PP strictly within intra-node NVLink domains, DP across Top-of-Rack switches, and TP across multi-hop spine switches.
    3. Map TP to the highest-bandwidth domain, place PP stages along suitable low-contention paths, and align corresponding DP shards with network rails while checking available capacity.
    4. Map TP, PP, and DP uniformly at random across all available nodes to balance switch thermal loads across the datacenter fabric.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Frequent tensor-parallel exchanges favor the highest-bandwidth domain, which is not universally limited to one node. Pipeline boundaries and data-parallel groups must then be mapped to the actual topology and traffic pattern. Same-rack placement and rail alignment can help, but neither guarantees uncongested bandwidth. Reversing bandwidth priorities or choosing random placement ignores the communication requirements.

    Learning Objective: Justify a feasible topology mapping of tensor-, pipeline-, and data-parallel groups under their communication budgets.

  2. A persistent degraded switch path lowers measured end-to-end training throughput by 30 percent. A checkpoint-and-migration pause would take 10 minutes, healthy replacement capacity is available, and days of training remain. What is the appropriate orchestration response?

    1. Continue running the job indefinitely because a 10-minute checkpoint-and-reschedule migration would cost more compute than a 30% slowdown over a multi-week run.
    2. Rely on the GPU driver to disable NVLink and route all tensor-parallel traffic through host memory over PCIe.
    3. Trigger a performance-aware topology quarantine via taints or drain states, checkpoint the running job, and migrate it to healthy nodes where the break-even time is under an hour.
    4. Terminate the job and permanently de-allocate all 10,000 GPUs in the datacenter until all network transceivers are manually replaced.

    Answer: The correct answer is C. Quarantine the degraded path and migrate onto verified healthy capacity. At a measured 30-percent end-to-end throughput loss, recovering the work forgone during a 10-minute pause takes about 23 minutes of subsequent healthy execution relative to staying on the degraded placement, or about 33 minutes counting the pause. This payback uses job throughput, not merely a collective microbenchmark, and assumes the slowdown would persist.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate scheduler diagnostic mechanisms and migration economics for degraded network hardware.

  3. In an 8-GPU-per-node cluster with eight network rails, how should rail-aware placement map corresponding model shards across replicas, and what contention limits remain?

    Answer: Keep corresponding model shard \(j\) at local GPU index \(j\) across replicas so its data-parallel group follows the matching GPU-to-NIC rail. A global rank modulo eight is insufficient unless the rank layout already encodes that shard mapping. Rail alignment reduces avoidable cross-rail traffic, but concurrent jobs, oversubscribed links, and skew can still contend for bandwidth.

    Learning Objective: Explain how rail alignment reduces cross-rail traffic and why contention can remain.

  4. Why are static topology configuration files (such as Slurm’s topology.conf or static Kubernetes node labels) insufficient on their own for optimal collective placement in large-scale ML clusters?

    Answer: Static files represent nominal design schematics rather than operational reality; they cannot reflect dynamic runtime anomalies such as degraded transceiver link widths, PCIe bus renegotiations, blown spine switch links, or thermal throttling. Runtime introspection supplements reported connectivity, while health tests and measured traffic performance establish whether the paths deliver the required communication budget.

    Learning Objective: Compare static topology configuration files with dynamic runtime topology introspection in distributed ML orchestration.

  5. For the illustrated eight-GPU-per-node fleet, explain how a scheduler combines tensor-parallel locality, pipeline paths, and data-parallel rail alignment when selecting a placement. Why does minimizing the chapter’s illustrative locality score not establish minimum communication time?

    Answer: Filter candidates by resource capability and health, then retain only complete allocations admitting the required tensor-, pipeline-, and data-parallel rank mappings. Within the illustrated topology, tensor groups use NVLink domains, pipeline stages use suitable rack-local paths, and corresponding data-parallel shards align with available rails where feasible. These constraints must hold together rather than in one universal serial ordering. The dimensionless pairwise score ranks locality under assumed weights; it omits traffic volumes, overlap, and concurrent contention, so measured communication and end-to-end completion still require validation.

    Learning Objective: Explain how feasibility constraints and an illustrative locality score guide topology-aware placement without guaranteeing minimum communication time.

  6. In cloud orchestration environments such as AWS and GCP, a user specifies a ____ to request close placement of virtual machine instances for high-bandwidth, low-latency communication.

    Answer: placement group. A cluster placement policy requests nearby, high-bandwidth placement under the provider’s documented contract. It is not a universal guarantee of identical bisection bandwidth or a substitute for measuring collective performance.

    Learning Objective: Identify the cloud-native abstraction used to enforce low-latency, high-bandwidth network proximity.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. When an elastic scheduler resizes a distributed training job from 64 GPUs to 128 GPUs, what trade-off exists between maintaining a constant global batch size versus scaling batch size proportionally with worker count?

    1. Constant global batch size halves the effective examples per worker per optimizer update, removing one source of optimization change without guaranteeing identical convergence; proportional batch scaling preserves that per-worker count but changes optimization and requires a validated learning-rate policy.
    2. Constant global batch size requires restarting the model weights from epoch zero, whereas proportional batch size allows continuous gradient descent without learning rate adjustment.
    3. Constant global batch size disables NVLink communication across workers, whereas proportional batch size enables ring AllReduce.
    4. Constant global batch size doubles the learning rate automatically via PyTorch hooks, whereas proportional batch size keeps the learning rate fixed to prevent divergence.

    Answer: The correct answer is A. With constant global batch, doubling worker count halves the effective per-worker batch, which includes gradient accumulation rather than necessarily requiring smaller microbatches. Shrinking instead raises the examples each worker must contribute, potentially increasing memory demand or accumulation time. Preserving global batch controls one source of optimization change but does not prove identical convergence or hardware efficiency. Keeping the per-worker batch fixed changes global batch, so the learning-rate policy and target quality must be validated. The other options incorrectly tie batch policy to restarting weights, disabling NVLink, or automatic learning-rate changes.

    Learning Objective: Analyze the trade-offs between constant global batch size and adaptive learning rate during elastic worker scaling.

  2. An elastic job running on 64 GPUs at 100 steps/minute can scale to 128 GPUs, reaching 180 steps/minute. If resizing triggers a dynamic rendezvous and checkpoint-sync transition pause of 60 seconds (0 steps during pause), what is the break-even duration the job must run after the pause at 128 GPUs to recover the lost progress in total steps completed?

    1. At least 15 seconds.
    2. At least 30 seconds.
    3. At least 45 seconds.
    4. At least 75 seconds.

    Answer: The correct answer is D. In the 60-second (1.0 minute) pause, the 64-GPU baseline would have completed \(100 \text{ steps/min} \times 1.0 \text{ min} = 100 \text{ steps}\). At 128 GPUs, the job produces \(180 \text{ steps/min}\), which is an incremental gain of \(\Delta = 180 - 100 = 80 \text{ steps/min}\). To recover the 100 lost steps, the job must run at 128 GPUs for \(t = 100 / 80 = 1.25 \text{ minutes} = 75 \text{ seconds}\). At 75 seconds the runs tie; a net gain requires running longer. The shorter choices do not recover the 100 lost steps.

    Learning Objective: Calculate the break-even duration required for an elastic scaling resize to deliver positive net training throughput.

  3. What three parameters define the operational contract between an elastic scheduler and an elastic training framework (such as TorchElastic), and why is each necessary?

    Answer: The contract specifies the elastic range and valid allocation increments, the measured transition cost including any checkpoint, restart, redistribution, and rendezvous, and the scaling-efficiency curve for supported configurations. The range identifies viable execution groups; the transition cost determines how long a new allocation must persist to repay disruption; the curve supports comparing marginal progress with the value of allocating those devices elsewhere. Batch and quality policies must also remain valid under the chosen resize.

    Learning Objective: Explain the three core parameters of the contract between an elastic scheduler and a training framework.

  4. True or False: Under a supported resize and checkpoint contract, an elastic scheduler can reclaim capacity by shrinking a training job to a smaller viable configuration without terminating the logical job, although worker processes may restart.

    Answer: True. Elasticity adds supported allocation increments between the declared bounds rather than permitting arbitrary or continuous worker counts. A planned shrink can save state, restart or redistribute workers, and rebuild the group at reduced throughput. It still pays transition overhead; preserving recent progress depends on a valid save, while a forced interruption can require replay from the last durable checkpoint.

    Learning Objective: Compare binary preemption with elastic worker downsizing under sudden cluster contention.

  5. A worker encounters an unhandled CUDA fault during a TorchElastic training run. Explain the dependencies that must hold before useful training resumes, and why recovery cannot require every surviving worker to finish a clean step boundary.

    Answer: Failure detection triggers worker-group recovery; the scheduler must preserve exclusive resource ownership and provide or authorize a viable allocation, while elastic agents stop and restart the worker group as required by the runtime. Restarted workers re-rendezvous, rebuild ranks and process groups, and restore valid durable training state before resuming. Detection, allocation, and cleanup can overlap rather than follow one universal serial order. A fault can strand peers inside a collective, so a clean step boundary is not guaranteed; recovery may replay work after the last valid checkpoint.

    Learning Objective: Explain the allocation, membership, and durable-state dependencies of elastic recovery after an unexpected worker failure.

Self-Check: Answer

  1. What is a preemption cascade in a shared GPU cluster, and which control stops it at the first level?

    1. A cascade is a burst of hardware failures in one rack; spare-capacity reservation stops it.
    2. A cascade is a high-priority admission that evicts medium-priority jobs, each of which then evicts lower-priority jobs to reschedule itself; requiring evicted jobs to requeue without preempting anyone stops it after the first level.
    3. A cascade is a queue of jobs waiting behind a large gang; backfill scheduling stops it.
    4. A cascade is repeated checkpointing by one job; lengthening its checkpoint interval stops it.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. If an urgent job displaces a handful of jobs and each of those displaces as many again on rescheduling, the interruptions multiply with each level, each paying the preemption overhead and adding checkpoint reload traffic at the same moment. A rule that evicted jobs requeue without preempting caps the damage at the first level, and a preemption budget limits how often any one job can be displaced. Failure bursts, queueing behind a gang, and checkpoint frequency are different problems.

    Learning Objective: Explain how preemption cascades multiply interruption cost and which controls bound them.

  2. A training job moves from on-demand to spot capacity at the chapter’s scenario discount. According to the effective-cost relation, in which effective cost equals the spot price times billed allocation time divided by retained productive time, what determines whether the discount survives?

    1. Only the advertised discount, because interruptions are free once a provider sends a notice.
    2. The ratio of billed allocation time to retained productive time, which grows with checkpoint overhead, restart time, and work replayed after each interruption.
    3. The number of GPUs in the job, because larger jobs always receive deeper spot discounts.
    4. The provider’s capacity-placement score, which predicts how many interruptions the job will see per day.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Effective cost is the spot price scaled by billed time over retained progress. Checkpointing, recovery, and discarded work all add billed time without adding retained progress, so frequent or expensive interruptions can erase the discount. A notice gives an opportunity to save state but does not make the interruption free, and a placement score estimates whether a request will succeed, not how often the job will be interrupted.

    Learning Objective: Apply the spot effective-cost relation to decide whether a discount survives interruption overhead.

  3. An organization runs steady 24/7 inference, urgent retraining with tight deadlines, and a large hyperparameter sweep. Which assignment of these workloads to reserved, on-demand, and spot capacity matches each workload’s deadline and recovery contract?

    1. Inference on spot, retraining on reserved, sweep on on-demand.
    2. Inference on reserved, retraining on on-demand, sweep on spot.
    3. All three on spot, because the discount outweighs any interruption risk.
    4. Inference on on-demand, retraining on spot, sweep on reserved.

    Answer: The correct answer is B. Reserved capacity suits predictable continuous load such as steady inference. On-demand capacity avoids long-term commitment for urgent work that cannot tolerate reclamation, at full price and with admission and startup delay. Spot capacity suits restart-tolerant work such as independent sweep trials. Placing inference or deadline-bound retraining on reclaimable capacity, or reserving capacity for a bursty sweep, mismatches the tier to the workload’s contract.

    Learning Objective: Map workloads to reserved, on-demand, and spot capacity tiers by deadline and restart tolerance.

  4. This chapter treats lifecycle total cost of ownership (TCO) as an input to the scheduler rather than as a scheduling mechanism. What does the scheduler consume from TCO analysis and cost accounting, and how does it use those inputs?

    Answer: It consumes per-resource opportunity costs and policy constraints: the marginal price of each capacity tier (owned, reserved, on-demand, or interruptible), the reservation a project may claim, the interruption risk it may accept, and the deadline it must meet. It uses them only to rank feasible scheduling actions such as waiting, preempting lower-priority work, falling back to on-demand capacity, or shrinking elastically. Building the TCO model, attributing costs, and chargeback belong to the fleet’s accounting and economics processes, not to the orchestrator.

    Learning Objective: Explain how lifecycle cost analysis reaches the scheduler as price signals and constraints rather than as a scheduling mechanism.

  5. A low-priority 64-GPU fine-tuning run is forcibly preempted. What makes up the preemption overhead for that event, and why does a per-job preemption budget matter more for this run than for a high-priority job?

    Answer: The overhead is the work discarded since the last durable checkpoint plus the relaunch, checkpoint reload, and warmup needed before the job is productive again, charged across every GPU of the allocation. A low-priority run sits at the bottom of every preemption cascade, so without a budget it can be struck many times a day and lose hours of allocation time; a budget caps how often it can be displaced, at the cost of delaying some higher-priority admissions.

    Learning Objective: Decompose the preemption overhead and explain why preemption budgets protect low-priority work.

  6. True or False: Because each VM instance in a cloud provider has an independent lifecycle, placing worker nodes of a 64-node distributed training job across different instance types within the same Availability Zone guarantees that preemption events will remain uncorrelated and statistically independent.

    Answer: False. Cloud capacity pressures and spot revocations are driven by macroeconomic cluster demand spikes and provider-level resource reclamation within a datacenter or Availability Zone. Preemption events for high-demand GPU types (e.g., A100/H100) are heavily correlated within an AZ; when capacity becomes scarce, providers reclaim multiple instances simultaneously, invalidating assumptions of independent failure.

    Learning Objective: Evaluate the assumption of statistical independence in cloud spot instance preemption events.
